A.21 Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.0 pages)

Climate Change Problem

1. Climate change is the most important challenge facing humanity. While Africa only contributes 3% to global GHG emissions, the impacts of climate change are predicted to be more severe on the continent than in other regions.
2. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) projects that by 2050, temperature increases in Sub-Saharan Africa are expected to be higher than the global mean temperature increase. This will have significant implications for agriculture and food security, water resources, public health, infrastructure, ecosystems, and biodiversity.
3. The IPCC also predicts that climate change will exacerbate existing environmental challenges such as desertification, land degradation, and loss of natural habitats. These factors can lead to increased migration, conflict, and poverty in the region.

IPCC Impact of 1.5°C of global warming on Natural and Human systems. Accessible [here](#)
IPCC Climate Change 2022: Mitigation of Climate Change. Accessible [here](#)

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

1. Africa is climate vulnerable, considering ecosystems, biodiversity, and human communities at global and regional levels, indicating that adaptation action is most urgent in Africa – requiring significant efforts to avoid rapid impact on lives and livelihoods.
2. While Africa has historically low emissions, this statistic is misleading given Africa's lack of energy access and its corresponding low rate of consumption. The AR5 estimates that the per capita consumption of energy in sub-Saharan Africa (SSA) was 0.3 MWh in 1980 compared to 4.2 MWh for Europe and North America, 6.7 MWh in the United States and 6.5 MWh in Europe.
3. The average Ethiopian family uses less than one-tenth of the power used by a typical American household. Africa has the lowest electrification rate at only 18% compared to 90% for Europe and North America, 74% in the United States and 62% in Europe. As a result, most Africans rely on kerosene and diesel generators for lighting, while another 90 million lack access to clean cooking fuels.
4. Africa's energy sector is also heavily dependent on fossil fuels, which are major contributors to air pollution. 75% of businesses across the continent experience disruptions due to energy causing them to resort to dirty oil or wood stoves and other inefficient technologies for heating and cooking.
5. Over the next two decades, Africa will need to build a new power sector from scratch in order to create the economic opportunities and jobs that can lift its people out of poverty. The cost of building this new power sector is estimated at $108 billion per year over the next 20 years, which is more than double what Africa has spent on energy infrastructure since independence.
6. Africa's emissions would double by 2050, derailing the Paris Agreement commitment to keep global warming below 2°C.

# Opportunities and Challenges

7. Africa has the opportunity and challenge of building much of its infrastructure, manufacturing capacity, and economy. This requires accelerated financial support to enhance mitigation and adaptation action in Africa.
8. The World Bank Group (WBG) is committed to supporting Africa's development agenda through a $10 billion per year financing pledge for developing countries at the 2015 Copenhagen climate talks will only be achieved if there are sufficient resources available to finance the energy sector, which accounts for over half of all sector financing complementing this critical financing gap is required to mobilize targeted capital to meet the energy needs of Africa.

# KawiSafi Fund

9. KawiSafi Fund has achieved significant impact in its focus countries Kenya and Rwanda – with combined 23.4M direct and 19.9M indirect lives impacted and over 20M tonnes of CO2 as of June 30, 2022.
10. While we continue to work on the remaining 50% of our pipeline in Kenya and Rwanda, we are now focusing beyond these two countries given the significant need highlighted above. KawiSafi Fund II intends to focus on priority counties in Ethiopia, Uganda, and Mozambique. As of June 15, 2022, we received 8 complete NOLs from Power Africa (Republic of Congo, DRC, Eritrea).
11. The Accredited Entity is in advanced discussions with several other countries, including (without limitation) Ghana, Malawi, and Tanzania.
12. As of June 30, 2022, KawiSafi Fund II has received 8 complete NOLs from Power Africa (Republic of Congo, DRC, Eritrea). Additional NOLs are expected to be received in the coming weeks as more countries submit their compliant NOL and the approval is added by the Board.

# Arrive at the Focus Countries

13. To arrive at the focus countries, the team conducted a geographic market evaluation and selection exercise prioritizing countries that have: (i) high potential for impact to meet NDC targets; (ii) investment ecosystem; (iii) Asumer's prior presence and reach; and (iv) country alignment to KawiSafi Fund II’s target sectors to identify where the greatest opportunities exist. The team identified Ethiopia, Uganda, and Mozambique as priority countries and increase adaptive capacity to climate risks.
14. Together, interventions in these countries have the potential to improve the resilience of approximately 2.5 million people and avert 203 million tonnes of CO2e.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 5

## The Green Climate Fund is an ambitious $200 million venture equity Fund intending to utilize a blended finance structure to unlock private sector climate investment into the African region.

The Fund expects to continue the role of the GCF in supporting countries and projects that are at the forefront of climate action, while also deploying low-carbon technologies in an inclusive and sustainable way and that enable people to participate and flourish in a green economy and growth out of poverty. As at the date of submission of this proposal, the project pipeline consists of 100+ approved projects across 25 countries with governments of Cote d’Ivoire, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Nigeria, Rwanda, Uganda and Zambia.

## The Kaival team has developed an indicative advanced pipeline of over 40 investment opportunities with an estimated total project value of $3.7 billion. This is supported by a larger opportunity pipeline of 200+ projects, with an estimated deal value of just under $28 billion across the ten focus countries.

### Building on the Kaival Fund I experience that identified and supported the emerging energy access ecosystem, Kaival Fund II has identified three thematic veritcals that represent the new landscape for off-grid investments:

- **Energy Transition:** Firms driving on global and national energy transition strategies through rapid deployment of renewable energy technologies (RETs) in the power sector, including solar photovoltaic (“PV”) markets, residential mini- and micro-grids, financial services for renewables, and waste-to-energy solutions.
- **Productivity:** firms focusing on low-carbon productivity and efficiency measures including low-cost internet access, “smart” green manufacturing, recycling / circular economy, clean cooking, and agro-processing.
- **Mobility & Logistics:** firms involved in the rapidly evolving mobility & logistics markets where unique electrification opportunities exist. These include electric vehicles (EVs), battery storage solutions in fragmented markets and creating new opportunities for climate resilient public and private transportation.

### Kaival Fund II will focus on late-stage / early growth stage companies with ticket sizes of $1-10 million and on its core expertise of scaling businesses. The Management team sees the company-building role of Kaival Fund I as a key driver in supporting the transition to a low-carbon economy, especially in Africa.

The larger fund and ticket size reflect the evolution of the investment landscape in Africa since Fund I was launched in 2015. While there has been significant progress on scaling up clean energy deployment. There remains a significant gap in equity funding at the stage of growth across African markets where a company has launched and started to gain traction, but still needs capital to reach commercial scale.

Kaival Fund II will be structured as an Accredited Entity (“AE”) under the Green Climate Fund (GCF) framework. Kaival Fund I intends to access the same stages but will have the ability to invest slightly larger tickets, which will allow it to better support companies in scaling up their operations and products.

### Kaival Fund II builds on the track record and experience of Acumen as an Accredited Entity (“AE”) with two GCF-supported funds under management – the Kaival Ventures Fund and the Acumen Resilience Fund. The Acumen Resilience Fund has invested $10 million in 25 companies across Africa, Asia, Latin America and the Caribbean.

Kaival Fund I or “Kaival” was one of GCF’s first investments; a $674 million venture fund focused on enterprises that are addressing climate change and Resilient Key Advancements of Kaival Fund I as at 30/06/2020 have been:

- **Disbursements:** $1.5M in 10 companies across Africa, Asia, Latin America and the Caribbean, paying BtGAS technology, consumer financing and C&I energy.
- A project in Kenya (the Kiva Fund) targeting Kenyan and Rwandan women below the poverty line. Kaival Fund I’s portfolio has actively promoted gender diversity and inclusion with a diverse portfolio comprising 87% women and training over 4,500 women in the larger ecosystem.

Kaival Fund I has cumulatively achieved 1Mt CO2eq in Kenya and 2 Mt CO2eq in Rwanda.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 6

## Leveraging public with private capital: KawiSafi Fund I has leveraged approximately $540M additional debt and equity capital to its portfolio (14% investment; 47% GCF capital).

### As a result, the Fund is working closely with the Green Climate Fund (GCF) as an implementing company and is working with the companies to support subsequent equity raises and/or exit planning.

KawiSafi I has a robust data and impact tracking framework which reports on impact metrics to its shareholders. The Fund is also working closely with the GCF in terms of capacity building, collaborating extensively with the GCF portfolio management team on the APRs and ensuring that methodologies and impact reporting are aligned across all investments. The Fund will continue to work with the GCF and other stakeholders to develop and improve its impact processes on an ongoing basis. An independent evaluation is due in October 2023 under the terms of the KawiSafi Fund I project implementation plan.

## Climate results benefits

1. **KawiSafi Fund I** focuses its efforts on private investment pathways identified in priority NDCs to achieve both mitigation and adaptation impacts and benefits in the highest impact countries listed above. The focus of our investments is on energy, transport, agriculture, water, and land use sectors where we can provide capital and support lower income communities to adapt and become more resilient to climate impacts.
2. **The Fund** aims to invest in a portfolio of companies that can deliver the highest CO2e mitigation and population resilience outcomes per $1 invested.

The Fund is targeting an average of 29.5 million tCO2e through its investments in energy, productivity, and mobility. Further, The Fund’s interventions seek to drive increased MW of low-emission energy capacity installed across the continent. Finally, the Fund aims to support at least two million electric three- or four-wheeler electric vehicles (EVs) across various markets on the continent aimed to increase the use of low-carbon transport and reduce emissions from fossil fuel-based transport in urban and peri-urban areas.

The Fund is also targeting reduced vulnerability and strengthened resilience of approximately 3.5 million people directly and indirectly as effects of climate change. This is expected to be achieved through enhancing their resilience and adaptive capacity by 1) reducing reliance on climate vulnerable generation such as hydropower, 2) increasing access to renewable energy sources, 3) improving rural livelihoods making and climate-response planning through expansion of internet connectivity and supply chains in rural populations, and 4) promoting the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices that can improve the resiliency of climate change. Specifically, investments in innovations in the agriculture-energy nexus such as solar-powered irrigation and off-grid storage are expected to increase farm communities’ resilience to changes in precipitation as well as enhance food security through reducing post-harvest losses linked lower food loss.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B1. Climate context by region (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

#### 1. Africa: The continent most vulnerable to climate change impacts under all climate scenarios above 1.5 degrees Celsius.
Despite having contributed the least to global warming and having the lowest emissions, sub-Saharan Africa is projected to be one of the regions most affected by climate change. This will have severe implications for investments, water and food systems, public health, agriculture, and livelihoods, threatening to undo its development gains made over decades.

According to the IPCC, temperature increases in sub-Saharan Africa are projected to be higher than the global mean increase. The African Development Bank (AfDB) projects that by 2050, temperatures will have increased by an average of 1.7°C above pre-industrial levels, with some regions experiencing a temperature anomaly at least below 1°C; regions between 15% and 15% are projected to experience an increase in hot nights, as well as longer and more frequent heatwaves. Increases would be even larger for the global mean warming of 2°C above pre-industrial levels.

The African Development Bank expects that 10% of Africa's population will be displaced by climate change by 2050. The World Bank projects that under the Paris Agreement goals, it will push nearly over 100 million people into extreme poverty by 2030; with Sub-Saharan Africa being one of the regions most affected.

The AfDB expects that by 2050, sub-Saharan Africa's average temperature is projected to increase by at least 2.8°C above pre-industrial levels. The AfDB projects that the continent will experience an increase in extreme temperatures and intensity of rainfall in all Sub-Saharan regions. The African Development Bank expects that 10% of Africa’s population will be displaced by climate change by 2050.

The World Bank projects that under the Paris Agreement goals, it will push nearly over 100 million people into extreme poverty by 2030; with Sub-Saharan Africa being one of the regions most affected. The AfDB expects that by 2050, sub-Saharan Africa's average temperature is projected to increase by at least 2.8°C above pre-industrial levels.

The AfDB projects that the continent will experience an increase in extreme temperatures and intensity of rainfall in all Sub-Saharan regions. The World Bank projects that under the Paris Agreement goals, it will push nearly over 100 million people into extreme poverty by 2030; with Sub-Saharan Africa being one of the regions most affected.

The AfDB expects that by 2050, sub-Saharan Africa's average temperature is projected to increase by at least 2.8°C above pre-industrial levels.

2\. In selecting focus countries, the KawGafi Team conducted a rigorous research and analysis exercise considering in particular:

I. Assessing country’s expertise and prior experience across different markets in Africa;

II. KawGafi’s track record of investable opportunities; and

III. Country needs and climate priorities.

This exercise led to the identification of priority countries for KawGafi II: Cote d’Ivoire, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Kenya, Nigeria, Rwanda, Senegal, South Africa (NOLs at advanced stages). KawGafi II has further segmented these countries into two groups based on their level of readiness to receive investment. The team intends to pursue a differentiated investment strategy to have mitigation and adaptation impact across each of these countries. The types and sizes of investment in each country will vary depending on the archetype and accompanying market conditions.

In East Africa, which contributes approx. 1.43% of global GHG emissions[1], climate variability will likely have significant impacts on the region’s ability to achieve its development goals and contribute to sustainable economic growth, livelihoods, food security, health, and ecosystems.[2][7] With a population of over 450 million, the region is one of the world’s most rapidly urbanizing areas; however, 70% of the population still live in rural areas. The region has experienced significant increases in agricultural productivity since independence, but productivity in much of the region is expected to increase power and food insecurity. Warming temperatures and increased rainfall variability are projected to reduce crop yields by up to 50%,[8] with water stress remaining a critical source of livelihood and economic activity across the region. Increasingly severe flooding and droughts, in addition to changing vegetation zones and shifting rainfall patterns, will likely devastate East Africa.[9][10]

[1] (GGO). GHG Emissions FastTrack East Africa. Of 28 ten countries included in the East Africa Regional mission, GHG emissions data are available for eight counties. Excluding DRC and South Sudan; for which GHG emissions data are not available. The Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) is included as it has a significant share of the total East African population.

[2] (FAO). Climate change and food security in Africa: A review of the evidence. FAO, Rome 2013. Available at <https://www.fao.org/3/a-i3456e.pdf>

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 9

**B**

Atmosphere quality and reduce water availability, impacting sanitation and human health as well. The region, which GHG emissions grew faster than the world average (42% compared to 38%) between 1990 and 2011**, is expected to be one of the most vulnerable regions in Africa to climate change impacts. The carbon footprint of the region has not been developed and intensified in East Africa, Kenya, Rwanda and Uganda (NDCs included). Examples of how some of these countries needs align with the Kenyan NDCs are as follows:

- **Kenya**: Droughts and floods are the main climate hazards in Kenya, whereas variable rainfall is also a major concern. The country’s economy is highly dependent on climate sensitive sectors such as agriculture, energy, manufacturing, and tourism. Climate change will have significant impacts on these sectors. In recent years, economic losses resulting from droughts and floods are estimated at 3% of Kenya’s Gross Domestic Product (GDP). The Kenyan government has committed to reducing its greenhouse gas emissions by submitting a Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) under the Paris Agreement. Kenya plans to reduce its carbon intensity by 20% below business as usual (BAU) scenario, and by 30% relative to BAU by 2030 to become the business as usual (BAU) scenario. Kenya has committed to burn 21% of the energy it uses from renewable sources in 2030. The Kenyan government is seeking international support for the remaining $53.9B investment costs. Among the priority mitigation activities are: increasing the share of electricity generated by renewable energy, including hydropower on fossil and non-sustainable biomass fuels, enhancing energy efficiency, achieving a tree cover of 10% of land area and clean transport. Adaptation focus includes promotion of climate smart agriculture in Kenya. The country’s agricultural sector is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as temperature increase, precipitation changes, and extreme events. Dry weather conditions in 2017 led to a decline in the production of maize, wheat, rice, and beans. In addition, livestock losses were high with slaughter being done by farmers and pastoralists prior to cushion their losses. Recurring droughts have forced an estimated 3 million Kenyans into food insecurity every year. The country’s fisheries sector is also at risk as fish that are moving from inshore to deeper water areas and inland fisher communities lack the technological capacity to adapt to these changes. Kenya has committed to increase energy access, increase production and enhanced resilience of the agriculture systems. Kwale II intends to invest in renewable energy sources such as solar energy, which support increased agricultural productivity, increased water harvesting & storage.

- **Uganda**: Uganda submitted its initial NDC in 2021 with an increased ambition in its emission reduction goals. Uganda emits ~4.9 million tons (or 0.2 per capita) of carbon dioxide per year. Combined, the transport and energy sectors account for approximately 58% of total emissions (37% from road transport and 21% from electricity generation). The country’s low adaptive capacity means that it continues to be vulnerable to climate change impacts such as temperature increase, precipitation changes, and extreme events. Uganda has committed to achieve a 40% reduction in its carbon intensity by 2030 relative to BAU scenario, and to reach net-zero emissions by 2050. The government is also seeking international support for the remaining $18B investment costs. Among the priority mitigation activities are: increasing the share of electricity generated from renewable energy sources (including hydropower on fossil and non-sustainable biomass fuels), enhancing energy efficiency, achieving a tree cover of 10% of land area and clean transport. Adaptation focus includes promotion of climate smart agriculture in Uganda. The country’s agricultural sector is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as temperature increase, precipitation changes, and extreme events. Dry weather conditions in 2017 led to a decline in the production of maize, wheat, rice, and beans. In addition, livestock losses were high with slaughter being done by farmers and pastoralists prior to cushion their losses. Recurring droughts have forced an estimated 3 million Kenyans into food insecurity every year. The country’s fisheries sector is also at risk as fish that are moving from inshore to deeper water areas and inland fisher communities lack the technological capacity to adapt to these changes. Uganda has committed to increase energy access, increase production and enhanced resilience of the agriculture systems.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 10

## Uganda’s National Climate Change Policy April 2015: The Uganda Green Growth Development Strategy 2017/18 – 2020/31

### Republic of Congo: Despite efforts in the last decade that improved energy access rates from 11% to 19% in 2015, the DRC’s 68 million people is the second largest population without electricity. The country has a high potential for renewable energy resources, with an installed capacity of approximately 15,000 MW, equivalent to an 8-10% decrease in forest cover since 2000, leading to 7,170 Gt of CO2 emissions. The DRC’s low electrification rate and high poverty levels are linked to limited access and security issues and poor transport infrastructure – has led to underinvestment in the national grid thereby increasing the country’s vulnerability to climate change. The DRC is also responsive to the NDC’s adaptation needs, as it aligns with the NDC’s prioritization of energy access as a key component for achieving its mitigation commitments. The DRC’s adaptation plan identifies the sections of water resources and land use that are most vulnerable to climate change. In the agriculture sector, for example, climate change will likely lead to a reduction in the overall productivity of crops due to increased evaporation rates at higher temperatures, reduced rainfall[1]. Given the sections dependent on rainfall, the country’s adaptation plan identifies opportunities to implement water management strategies that leverage low carbon technologies such as solar irrigation to improve resilience to the sector.

### Republic of Congo: Despite efforts in the last decade that improved energy access rates from 11% to 19% in 2015, the DRC’s 68 million people is the second largest population without electricity. The country has a high potential for renewable energy resources, with an installed capacity of approximately 15,000 MW, equivalent to an 8-10% decrease in forest cover since 2000, leading to 7,170 Gt of CO2 emissions. The DRC’s low electrification rate and high poverty levels are linked to limited access and security issues and poor transport infrastructure – has led to underinvestment in the national grid thereby increasing the country’s vulnerability to climate change. The DRC is also responsive to the NDC’s adaptation needs, as it aligns with the NDC’s prioritization of energy access as a key component for achieving its mitigation commitments. The DRC’s adaptation plan identifies the sections of water resources and land use that are most vulnerable to climate change. In the agriculture sector, for example, climate change will likely lead to a reduction in the overall productivity of crops due to increased evaporation rates at higher temperatures, reduced rainfall[1]. Given the sections dependent on rainfall, the country’s adaptation plan identifies opportunities to implement water management strategies that leverage low carbon technologies such as solar irrigation to improve resilience to the sector.

### Republic of Congo: Despite efforts in the last decade that improved energy access rates from 11% to 19% in 2015, the DRC’s 68 million people is the second largest population without electricity. The country has a high potential for renewable energy resources, with an installed capacity of approximately 15,000 MW, equivalent to an 8-10% decrease in forest cover since 2000, leading to 7,170 Gt of CO2 emissions. The DRC’s low electrification rate and high poverty levels are linked to limited access and security issues and poor transport infrastructure – has led to underinvestment in the national grid thereby increasing the country’s vulnerability to climate change. The DRC is also responsive to the NDC’s adaptation needs, as it aligns with the NDC’s prioritization of energy access as a key component for achieving its mitigation commitments. ## B

### Tanzania:
While the Country is targeting universal electrification by 2024, it is estimated that over 80% of the country still rely on biomass for cooking leading to significant deforestation in the Country. As a result, Tanzania has been identified as one of the countries with the highest potential for scaling clean cooking solutions in the country. Climate assessments project that the mean annual temperature across the country will increase by 1.4°C and the duration of heat waves is projected to increase by 7–22 days. The projected increase in frequency of flooding events is expected to reduce numbers of landbirds. Over the past 30 years, the frequency and intensity of extreme events such as floods have increased significantly across Tanzania. These are particularly pronounced in the high altitude western and northern provinces. This increases vulnerability of key socio-economic sectors including agriculture (food losses due to lack of access to markets), Kigwaf’s investment in Climate Resilient Agriculture (CRA) projects, which is a key sector for food security, will lead to the development of sustainable transport network systems and that are resilient to climate change. The existing road infrastructure is prone to damage and undermining from flooding, whilst increased wind speeds can damage control structures, leading to loss of service. Increased rainfall intensity is expected to damage control systems, leading to lack of service. Around 50% of Rwanda’s electrical power generation capacity is located in the north-western part of the country where climate change is projected to see a decrease in annual rainfall which will result in a decrease in river discharge and reservoir storage. The Fund seeks to increase Rwanda’s adaptive capacity to any of these events by supporting the development of climate proof infrastructure as highlighted in the Country’s Climate Programme launched in 2021.

### Across West Africa’s 17 countries, climate change has been referred to as a “threat multiplier” as intensifying heat and rainfall patterns are expected to exacerbate existing environmental stressors and alter land use patterns for farmers, herders, and their livestock. The World Bank believes a situation of deteriorating environmental conditions and weak governance could tip some fragile countries into the conflict. The region currently has 10 million people displaced by climate change and this number is projected to double in the next decade.

### Kenya:
Kigwaf Fund II intends to focus on: Core-4 Nodes (Nigeria, Nigeria II second tier), and it also aims to align NGLI with the Kigwaf Fund II. The following are some of the key areas of focus for the fund that modality are highlighted below:

* **Nigeria:** Is Africa’s largest economy and is projected to become the world’s third most populous country by 2050, home to over 186 million people. Changes in Nigerian diets were driven by climate change and forest sector (loss of forest land) as well as the energy sector which accounted for ~42% of the country’s GHG emissions. Nigeria has a large population with limited access to electricity and power supply only meets an estimated third of demand, while 60% of the population lives in rural areas. The Nigerian government aims to reduce its carbon intensity by 37-51% from its 2010 levels by 2030 and achieve a 45% reduction in GHG emissions per unit of GDP by 2030, which is equivalent to reducing Nigeria’s GHG by 2050, amounting to between US$100-480 billion, as climate hazards like flooding, droughts, heatwaves and wildfires are expected to increase. The government has developed its National Climate Change Policy (2020), where it aims to prioritize low carbon, carbon-resilient, and high-green circular economy. The Nigerian government has set various goals including committing 67% of household energy consumption from renewable sources by 2030, increasing the share of electricity generated from renewable energy sources to 50%, and increasing and restoring forest cover, among others. The National policy includes the energy sector as a key target for climate action with the aim of reducing carbon intensity in the energy sector through promotion of a diverse energy mix with an increasing proportion of renewables and clean technologies, plus the development of a national renewable energy strategy. In addition to this, the government aims at promoting the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices across all sectors including the agriculture sector in Nigeria which is one of the sectors most sensitive to climate change. Under a BAU scenario, the country’s emissions from the agriculture sector are projected to increase by 10% over the next decade. However, the decline in yield on rain-fed agriculture could be as much as 50%. This will, in turn, impact GDP, reducing it by as much as 4.0% by 2030, this will likely be coupled by a decline in agriculture’s

## Côte d'Ivoire:

- The country is also a leader in renewable energy development, with wind and solar being favored in an energy mix that includes renewable energy (35%) and natural gas power plants.
- Its ambition to reduce emissions by 20% below business as usual through domestic mitigation measures, of which 27 are unconditional. This is reinforced in its 2021-25 National Development Plan (NDP).

### B

**proposals include improving affordable electricity access, increasing the use of renewable energy sources, improving energy efficiency, enhancing vehicle fleet, and promoting mass transportation. In terms of adaptation, the country has implemented a number of measures to reduce vulnerability and increase the risk of water stress. Côte d'Ivoire has ambitious mitigation targets, aiming for 89.6% reduction in total GHG emissions by 2030 compared with business as usual (BAU). The country is unprepared to meet its pledges at a compliance level up to 550 MCO2. The estimated total cost to achieve this is $22 billion. The future climate projections of Côte d'Ivoire by 2050 show temperature increases, more frequent and intense heat waves, increased frequency and intensity of heavy rainfall events, change in length of dry spells from -8 to +1 days and sea level rise from +13 cm to +47 cm. The projected impacts on the economy: coastal fisheries could see a 26 percent reduction by 2050; substantial loss in surface area suitable for rice production is expected due to increased flooding, saltwater intrusion, and salinity; coastal erosion and loss of forest cover due to the use of woods for fire and dependence on charcoal. The estimated total cost to achieve this is $13 billion.**

### Southern Africa contributes approx. 2.19% to global GHG emissions - 63% of which are driven by South Africa and Angola. Angola is the largest emitting sector due to South Africa's high coal dependence. Across the region, climate change has led to increased temperatures, reduced rainfall, and more frequent droughts, increasing water scarcity, pest infestations, increased frequency and intensity of droughts and floods. Excessive grazing pressure on rangelands has also contributed to desertification and degradation of the environment, in turn exacerbating existing vulnerabilities in the region. The region faced its worst drought in 50 years during 2019-2020, with a total loss of 34 million tons of maize (Zambian DQL seconded), and 6 to obtain an NGL South Africa. Examples of how these countries could benefit from the Kawasil Fund II mandate are highlighted below.

* **Zambia:** has experienced a series of climate extremes since 2010, floods and droughts have impacted the country's economy. The government now shows the development process of the country and could cost Zambia up to US$3.8 billion in GDP. Supporting low carbon development pathways that can reduce emissions from agriculture, energy, transport, and industry sectors is critical for Zambia. All sectors of the economy has been a priority for Zambia leading to implementation of various plans and policies. The agricultural sector accounts for 25% of total GDP and employs over 70% of the population and is the primary source of livelihood of the predominantly rural population. Reduced productivity of crops due to climate change, coupled with increased pest infestations has led to reduced yields and likely to reduce the revenues that can be made from agriculture. Based on the current climate conditions droughts are expected in 2021-2022, which is projected to increase by up to 50% compared to the previous year. The number of people directly affected by droughts annually is projected to increase from 3.2 million to 6.9 million over the next decade. In addition, the country has experienced a significant decrease in rainfall precipitation in the period 2008 - 2019 is projected to decrease by a median value of up to 30.2 mm for the period 2050-2079. This will have an adverse impact on maize and sorghum production as crop yields are under pressure year on year, thereby threatening food security in the country. Zambia is projected to be among the countries most affected by climate change and weather variability due to the high dependence of agriculture on rainfall. The country has a total emission reduction potential of 380,000GtCO2e which translates to 4% against the 2019 baseline.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## Green Climate Fund Proposal V3.0 | PAGE 14

### Adaptation actions with ~75% expected to come from external sources.

In the updated NDC proposal submitted in July 2021, Zambia added transport and liquid waste to its overall mitigation strategy to focus on energy and energy efficiency. This is in recognition of the significant contribution of these sectors to climate change in Zambia. Zambia has one of the lowest rural electrification rates on the continent, estimated at ~5% compared to an urban connectivity rate of 76% in 2019. The country also aims to achieve a peak in its emissions by 2030 and reduce its carbon intensity by 48% below 2010 levels as per its commitment under the SDG7 National Energy Compact. The country also targets deploying 5 GW of renewable energy capacity, including 2 GW from solar power, by 2030. This is in line with a reversal of deforestation currently estimated at 276,000 hectares per annum due to high reliance on charcoal production and firewood for cooking.

### South Africa: The country facing the impact of climate change

The national average temperature has increased by about 1°C since 1950. South Africa’s electricity generation system is one of the most coal-dependent in the world (81% in 2017) with a large stock of high-carbon capacity, including 4 GW of coal-fired power capacity and 36 GW of nuclear power capacity (of 42 GW current operating). Of coal-fired power capacity by 2050. South Africa’s Low Emissions Development Strategy 2050, built upon its National Development Plan 2030, outlines its goal to achieve peak emissions in the period between 2018 and 2024 with a target of reducing emissions by 60% below 1990 levels. Other goals include establishing an economy-wide carbon price and implementing zero emission buildings by 2030.

### II. Climate Finance Context

#### 1. The above climate context indicates the significant climate finance needs in Africa.
It is estimated that African countries need ~$2.8 billion by 2030 to successfully implement their NDCs. Collectively, this represents more than 5% of total public and private sector development financing for Africa. In addition to these financial resources, about 10% of the total cost, $2.5 million will come from international public sources and the domestic budget. The remaining 85%, or $34 billion, is estimated to be required in private sector funding. Private capital being up to seven times higher in emerging markets and real perceived risks being too high for commercial banks.

#### 2. On the adaptation side: adaptation funding to African countries was significantly insufficient when compared to the needs over the previous decade.
According to research by Stockholm Environment Institute (SEI),

The transition cost in the region was estimated to be $7.15 billion USD per year by 2020; actual funding towards adaptation initiatives was only 6.18 billion USD from 2014 to 2018, translating to merely $5.5 billion USD per year. ### III. The Proposed Solution: KwaSafi Fund

1. Finding ways to drive low-carbon investment through blended finance structures is critical to achieve climate change adaptation and mitigation goals. The Green Climate Fund has allocated $200 million in climate-focused venture capital targeted at supporting inclusive green growth across Africa. The Fund will have a blend of structured debt, equity, and grants that will be used for investments in the private sector. This structure was not previously perceived the region and industry as high risk. This structure is designed to expand the pool of private climate investment by leveraging public funds with private capital.
2. KwaSafi Fund is an innovative blended finance vehicle that seeks to address these challenges through a unique combination of debt and equity, and bilateral development donors. Investing at an injection point for company growth and scale, KwaSafi Fund will provide a flexible financial structure that can be tailored to the needs of each project. The fund will have a dual equity layer that allows for additional debt leveraging. KwaSafi Fund has to date leveraged approximately $30 million in debt and equity capital to its portfolio companies (13% in investment). Should the success of KwaSafi Fund be replicated, it could potentially unlock billions of dollars of private sector funding on the continent.
3. Building on KwaSafi Fund’s impact investing supports the evolution of the grid and energy ecosystems. KwaSafi Fund is expected to invest in three core sectors namely: (1) Energy Transition; (2) Energy Productivity and (3) Mobility & Logistics. These are highlighted below (and further detail is included in Section B.3 of this document).

3. **Energy Transition:** The Fund will seek to continue investing in opportunities captured in the first Fund (renewable energy, energy efficiency, electrification of rural grids, solar PV hardware, waste-to-energy, clean cooking, energy finance) and additionally look for opportunities in other areas such as electric vehicles at scale through financing/leasing/e-commerce as a service model. This is expected to have significant mitigation and adaptation impacts.

- Increase energy access through investment in decentralized renewable energy for residential use, including electrification of rural grids (Mitigation)
- Increase access for reliable, low carbon energy for industrial growth in Africa (Mitigation)
- Support development of energy efficiency measures (Adaptation)
- Support development of climate-resilient infrastructure (Adaptation)

**Adaptation impact being targeted:** A lack of rural electrification in West Africa (Nigeria and Senegal). a. Direct adaptation impact on the most vulnerable people and communities through climate-resilient technologies b. Direct adaptation impact on infrastructure and build environments through adoption of decentralized energy systems

Climate risk vulnerability: Energy Transition in rural electrification Access to energy is still a major gap, for example in Nigeria and Senegal. Providing access for off-grid households to electricity can have significant co-benefits beyond the obvious, with significant signification benefits to those that are otherwise dependent on traditional, “dirty” fuels. Use of traditional fuels contributes to deforestation, air pollution, and other health risks. The lack of energy infrastructure in rural areas is a major barrier for economic development. Infrastructure in certain regions is vulnerable to climate shocks such as floods and other weather events, and the lack of access to electricity can have significant impacts on people’s livelihoods. Energy systems resilience is also important for adaptation purposes, especially when it comes to protecting households due to indoor air pollution and respiratory illnesses. Improved health increases resilience in the face of climate change.

Adaptation impact thesis: Energy Transition in rural electrification An investment in climate-resilient energy systems addresses the vulnerability in a climate change context by:

a. Improved recovery and/or resilience of energy system post extreme weather events b. Individuals who benefit from expanded energy access in other ways, for example through access to the internet and mobile phones (Adaptation) c. The adaptation co-benefits of more resilient health and education infrastructure, through their improved reliance on electricity d. The adaptation benefits of businesses receiving a more reliable power supply, notably the enhanced resilience of small-scale manufacturing and other energy-intensive industries e. The improved resilience of the energy network through the use of more energy-efficient appliances

4. **Energy Productivity:** Firms providing innovative solutions that leverage renewable energy, energy efficiency technologies, or energy storage to increase productivity in their operations will be supported for increased use of energy across African economies. This includes uses of energy in sectors such as agriculture and food value chains, manufacturing, transport, and information and communication technology (ICT) among others. Mitigation and adaptation impacts expected from this segment include:

- Increased energy efficiency of manufacturing and other energy-intensive industries in Africa (Mitigation)
- Support for development of low-carbon urban infrastructure solutions
- Increased energy efficiency of buildings through the use of solar or wind - through energy efficient cooling; waste to energy innovations and provision of climate related information (Adaptation)
- Support for increased access to water resources, including through investments aimed at increasing capacity of agriculture dependent rural populations to water availability, changes in temperatures and rainfall variability (Adaptation)

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 17

- **Adaptation impact being targeted: A look at the agriculture sector**
    - Direct and indirect impact on the most vulnerable people and communities through resilient agriculture technologies that achieve food security as well as health, well-being and water security outcomes.
    
## Climate risk vulnerability: Agriculture sector
Agriculture is one of the sectors most vulnerable to changes in the climate. Across the continent, yields are expected to decline by 30% or more over the next few decades due to increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, which threaten national economies, not to mention threatening food security. In Africa, major grain producing wheat, maize and sorghum areas will be most affected. For example, in South Africa’s Western Cape province, notably wheat, is expected to experience a yield loss of 17%. There is a major need for innovative solutions to offer farmers the ability to adapt to climate change, especially smallholder farmers such as those using solar powered irrigation systems.

### Adaptation impact thesis: Agriculture sector
An investment in climate smart agriculture technologies addresses the vulnerability in the climate change context by:
- Increasing crop output (yields per hectare) and dairy productivity (liters per cattle head) compared to baseline levels.
- Improving water use efficiency, increasing farmers’ ability for farmers to do multiple crop cycles within a year, given year-round access to water sources.
- Reducing in operation costs, vs. diesel fuel, for those farmers already using diesel powered water pumps and irrigation systems.
- Increasing income and take-home pay for farmers and their households, by increasing the volume of marketable produce and reducing operating costs.
- Increased produce available in the local market for consumption within vulnerable communities.

### Enhancing resilience: Agriculture sector
Investment in climate smart agriculture technologies will:
- Increase food security and reduce poverty levels among smallholder farmers.
- Reduce vulnerability to climate change impacts, especially for those living on or near the edge of subsistence farming.

## 5. Mobility and Logistics: Companies involved in the rapidly evolving electric mobility and logistics markets across Africa. Mitigation and adaptation impacts expected from this segment include:
- Increased access to reliable and affordable transport services.
- Support increased data collection and efficiency in urban transport and logistics systems – through electrification and investment in innovative shared public mobile business models (Mitigation and Adaptation)

### Adaptation impact being targeted: Transport Sector – Transport network technologies
- Direct and indirect impact on the most vulnerable people and communities through climate-resilient technologies.

## Climate risk vulnerability: Transport sector
African urban and intercity transport systems are vulnerable to multiple types of climate impact: sea level rise and flooding, increased frequency and intensity of extreme wind and storm events, increased precipitation intensity, extreme heat and fire hazard, overall warming, and changes in average precipitation. These impacts will result in:
- Increased vulnerability for the most vulnerable people and communities.
- Reduced year-round availability of critical transport services; roads are damaged more often or are more costly to maintain due to climate change impacts such as flooding and increased frequency of extreme weather events like storms and surges, resulting in a short-life cycle and capacity than they were originally designed for. Critical infrastructure is also at risk from the effects of climate change, including damage from heatwaves that last decades ago, given the current trend of increased climate risk. For Sub-Saharan Africa alone it is estimated that climate change will continue to increase road maintenance costs by 20% in no action taken.
- The transport sector has sub-national and national data collection challenges; cities with some of the largest cities may not have the capacity to collect any transport data or provide basic information to users. This makes it harder for local authorities to plan and operate efficient transit networks, journeys and makes it even harder for local authorities to plan and operate efficient transit networks.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

## Adaptation Impact thesis: Transport Sector

An investment in productive technologies addresses the vulnerability in a climate change context. We anticipate the following impacts from our investment in these productive technologies:

a. Vulnerable populations will have better access to early warning systems and emergency response mechanisms, enabling them to evacuate more safely and effectively when extreme weather events occur.

b. Urban communities will be able to plan transport networks with greater flexibility, taking into account routes and evacuation channels where congestion is most likely to occur.

c. Public service providers will have better ability to offer emergency healthcare and other critical public services in the event of extreme weather events by optimizing transport infrastructure for efficient use.

d. Municipal or local governments will be better able to monitor transport infrastructure in real-time, responding to maintenance needs quickly and ensuring infrastructure is climate-proof. Data-driven approaches can help identify areas that require more frequent inspections and more cost-effective maintenance to limit the damage caused by extreme weather events.

e. Municipal or local governments are able to plan transport networks more efficiently to reduce congestion and ensure good access to evacuation routes - critical in the face of higher probability of extreme weather events.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0: PAGE 19

## B

### Background

**Background**

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is a global mechanism for financing climate action in developing countries. It was established under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and aims to support low-emission, climate-resilient development.

### Key Challenges

#### Energy Access
- **East Africa**: Lack of access to energy.
- **West Africa**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **South Asia**: High costs and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **East Asia**: High costs and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **Latin America & Caribbean**: High costs and unreliable energy for industrial growth.

#### Energy Efficiency
- **East Africa**: Lack of access to energy.
- **West Africa**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **South Asia**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **East Asia**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **Latin America & Caribbean**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.

#### Energy Productivity
- **East Africa**: Lack of access to energy.
- **West Africa**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **South Asia**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **East Asia**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **Latin America & Caribbean**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.

#### Energy Storage
- **East Africa**: Lack of access to energy.
- **West Africa**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **South Asia**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **East Asia**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.
- **Latin America & Caribbean**: High cost and unreliable energy for industrial growth.

### Mitigation Actions

#### Energy Access
- **East Africa**: Investment in off-grid solar, commercial renewable energy, and grid-connected solar.
- **West Africa**: Investment in small-scale hydroelectric power plants.
- **South Asia**: Investment in mini-grids and distributed generation.
- **East Asia**: Investment in large-scale hydropower projects.

#### Energy Efficiency
- **East Africa**: Investment in energy-efficient lighting and appliances.
- **West Africa**: Investment in energy-efficient manufacturing processes.

## B

### Key Highlights

1. **KawiFund's Ticket Size**: The ticket size is $500M & investments are at late venture/early growth stage, which is different from the GCF’s focus on early-stage projects.
2. **Investment Structure**: KawiFund will seek to invest across all segments but with a strong focus on late venture and early growth stage projects.
3. **Alignment with GCF's Private Sector Strategy**:
    - Promote a conducive investment environment for combined climate and economic growth activities.
    - Contribute to the development of an ecosystem through capacity building, market access, and technology transfer.

### Equity Investments
- KawiFund will seek to identify replicable business models in these segments and invest in companies that are at late venture/early growth stage.
- This should be a complement to other GCF-funded projects such as the Universal Green Energy Access Programme (UGEAP) and the Africa Renewable Energy Initiative (AREI).
- Examples include green bonds, green sukuk, green project revenue bonds, green receivables financing, credit enhancement guarantees, and technical assistance to support growth of diversified renewable energy sources.

### Support for GCF's TA Grants
- KawiFund is raising a technical assistance facility (the "TAF") alongside KawiFund to complement its equity investments with focused grants to both:
    - Enhance the impact of existing TA facilities in Africa.
    - Leverage regional and local partnerships to drive successful implementation, deliver value for money, and ensure country ownership.

### Alignment with GCF's Private Sector Strategy
- KawiFund is closely aligned to the key principles set out in GCF’s Private Sector Strategy (19 May 2022):
    1. Promote a conducive investment environment for combined climate and economic growth activities.
    2. Contribute to development of an ecosystem through capacity building, market access, and technology transfer.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 21

## B

### Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words; approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

#### B.2.a.

**1. KawiFund II aims to address the twin challenges of poverty and climate change. In Sub-Saharan Africa, people lack access to essential goods and services while facing increasing risk and volatility driven by climate change. The private sector has a critical role in addressing these challenges. The companies directly address these challenges. The targets companies directly offer essential goods and services to individuals and households. These companies also provide financial services that increase the level of access to these essential goods and services and has resulted in income inequalities which historically have had the poor, women, children, and people with disabilities and rights – especially among women and girls.**

**2. The KawiFund II model of change is around the following three elements:**
- **IF:** KawiFund II unlocks capital for carbon productive businesses.
- **THEN:** New ecosystems for investment in and growth of such businesses should be catalyzed and accelerated, resulting in adoption of clean smart technologies at scale, reduced GHG emissions, and strengthened resilience to climate change.

**3. Because KawiFund II: Its demonstration effect of the financial and impact potential should lead to more available funding and more low-carbon productive businesses emerging in Africa.**

The theory of change is around three barriers:

* **Barrier 1:** Limited availability of flexible equity capital for late venture early growth companies in low-carbon and climate-smart sectors.
    * Limited access to finance is one of the most significant barriers hindering the development of low-carbon and climate-smart businesses. In Africa, there are limited institutional investors with a mandate to invest in green projects. There is also a lack of late venture and early equity growth capital to catalyze capital crowding-in.
    * There is not enough local institutional capital available with a mandate to invest in green projects. There is an urgent need for innovative financial instruments, such as blended finance vehicles, pool funds at scale and provide the much-needed equity capital for renewable energy companies in Africa.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 22

## Barrier 2: Nascency of many clean technologies in focus countries and lack of both consumer awareness and technical expertise.

Many of the critical technologies required to facilitate low-carbon transportation, including electric vehicles (EVs), charging infrastructure, battery storage, and electrical vehicle charging infrastructure. Similarly, the lack of proven models, technical capacity, knowledge gaps and limited consumer awareness hinder scaling the sector, especially with regards to do’s redesigning policies, regulatory frameworks, financing structures, and business models that align with international standards as well as innovative business models – all things that are necessary to design and scale up comprehensive solutions. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) will seek to address this barrier by providing technical assistance and facilitating technical support (through the TA) intends to serve as this add-on service.

## Barrier 3: Lack of alignment between adaptation and mitigation efforts, how to integrate adaptation practices into target sectors, understanding of methods and data tools for tracking.

Across the continent, governments have initially initiated mitigation and adaptation targeting setting in line with their NDCs. However, there is a need to ensure that these two are aligned. In addition, while progress remains a further dialogue linking the integration of mitigation and adaptation practices into target sectors. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) will seek to address this barrier by providing technical assistance alongside equity investing into these sectors should support enhancing climate and ESG performance, reduce financial risk, increase resilience, improve long-term sustainability, reduce adverse impacts, and promote best practices in climate impact knowledge and data.

## Barrier 4: Technical and STEM-related climate technologies are often gender exclusive and women are less likely to participate in the design of solutions that align with development goals and improving social well-being. The benefits of increased access to modern and cleaner energy services often fail to accrue meaningfully for women, who are disproportionately affected by energy poverty. Women’s lack of participation, control over decisions, assets and resources, capacity, and skills. Therefore, there exists an opportunity to address this barrier by ensuring that the design of solutions is inclusive of gender inclusivity and transformation in climate smart solutions. These should in turn improve the resilience of women and girls.

## 2. KfWd Fund II will seek to address these barriers as follows:

### First: Through investment of risk capital in the scaling of businesses providing renewable distributed energy and storage solutions, KfWd Fund II will seek to support the rapid acceleration of supply of these essential products and services. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) will seek to address this barrier by assessing suitability of these products and commercial viability of these businesses. KfWd Fund investment expects to be critical in scaling up the production, distribution, and deployment of these products and services.

### Second: To accelerate the shift to low emission transport, KfWd Fund II will seek to supplement public sector efforts by investing in segments ripe for private sector intervention such as bus and three-wheeler EVs and public charging infrastructure. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) will seek to address this barrier by financing business models being piloted to unlock affordability and access to electric transport in Africa. At scale, these investments are expected to reduce the carbon intensity of road transport by 50% or more for nearly a quarter of all energy-related CO2 emissions. The innovative EV financing structures such as Battery as a Service (BaaS) will be critical in scaling up deployment of these products and increase residual value for vehicles which is expected to generate new revenue streams.

### Further: KfWd Fund II will seek to invest in businesses that utilize innovative pay-as-you-go models to build out the charging infrastructure. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) will seek to address this barrier by building capacity to the effects of climate change. Increasing access to these products will likely also empower smallholder farmers who are most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change and whose livelihoods depend on these women’s ability to continue farming, especially through revenue via addition from solar-powered grinding or milling of farm produce. Given the nascency of this segment, KfWd Fund II activities will seek to address.

## The Fund activities intend to directly contribute to making wider options for low carbon productive use available and accessible to lower income people, ensuring companies have necessary liquidity and capital to scale up their climate solutions and mitigate environmental and social risk management.

In turn, these drive the following long-term outcomes:

- **Outcome 1: Reduce energy-related GHG emissions from renewable energy and energy storage solutions**
- **Outcome 2: Reduced transport and logistics-related GHG emissions from electric and shared mobility solutions and greening freight fleets. Finally, Africa can reduce industrial GHG emissions through green production solutions, including energy efficiency, agriculture-water-energy waste solutions, materials**

Africa is home to some of the world’s fastest-growing economies and populations, and the continent must prioritize reducing its greenhouse gas emissions, particularly those related to energy production and consumption. This will require a significant shift towards clean energy technologies and low-carbon energy storage solutions, which can significantly reduce energy-related emissions. Additionally, Africa must also focus on improving access to sustainable transport options for all citizens by promoting the use of electric and shared mobility solutions, as well as greening freight fleets. Finally, Africa can reduce industrial GHG emissions through green production solutions, including energy efficiency, sustainable agriculture, water and energy conservation, waste reduction, and environmentally friendly materials. By embracing these solutions, Africa can mitigate

## Impact on the environment and contribute to a sustainable future for all: KweKaSafi’s target businesses are expected to displace polluting sources of energy and increase efficiency across the continent. The proposed investment in the sector is expected to provide access to clean, affordable, and reliable sources of energy and water, as well as increased access to goods and services and additionally should contribute to the targets set by the UNFCCC for 2030*. Adoption of low-carbon technologies at the household, and business level is expected to yield positive environmental impacts. The proposed investment in the sector is also expected to mitigate state change. Since inception, KweKaSafi Fund I has availed ~72m tons of CO2eq emissions through its portfolio companies. This represents a reduction of 10% from the baseline scenario and avoids 28 million tonnes of CO2eq emissions through the investments it makes across the energy transition sector.

### Outcome 4: Improved resilience of lower-income populations due to better adaptability to climate change

The overall lower income populations is improved, through access to more resilient energy and transport infrastructure, strengthened food security, and access to a broader range of reliable climate proof opportunities and tools for employment, income generation and economic opportunities. A combination of these factors will help to reduce the vulnerability of lower-income populations, especially women. As described in section B.1 above, the region faces severe climate risks including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, such as droughts, floods, heatwaves, and wildfires.

By investing in the target energy and climate solutions, the businesses served will likely be more resilient to these climate risks. For example, by providing access to clean water through solar powered pumps, women can spend less time collecting water and have more time for income generating activities. By developing businesses that enable populations to diversify their incomes either by expanding climate sensitive agricultural production or by accessing new markets, lower-income populations will be better able to adapt to the changing climate.

### Outcome 5: Enabling Environment: Strengthened ecosystem for low-carbon productive businesses

As businesses in the energy and transport sectors move towards financial sustainability, business have available to them additional capital for investment in R&D and product innovation. This catalyzes their development of new products and services that support the energy transition. Further, this increased profitability is expected to increase employment, income, and gender inclusive opportunities for low-income people through low carbon businesses.

By 2030, it is estimated that the climate tech sector could support up to 15 million jobs in Africa (excluding South Africa), with 70% of these jobs located in West Africa (15%) and Central Africa (19%), where women led roles are expected to make up ~75% of those created. In addition, it is estimated that the climate tech sector could support an additional 275 million jobs across the broader economy (indirect jobs). Increased investment in this sector is expected to contribute to the creation of 30,000 direct jobs through its portfolio of 9 companies, with an average

### B.3 Project programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)
1. **Kawasil Fund** is a climate-focused fund, sponsored by Asian Development Bank and focused on low carbon technologies that meet the essential needs of growing Asian markets. The fund seeks to make a significant step-change in impact by investing at the nexus of climate and energy poverty. Its goal is to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from 10 million tonnes CO2e per year and benefit over 35 million people directly or indirectly due to effects of climate change and avoid 20.9 million tonnes of CO2e.
2. The Management team builds directly from its experience of deploying its predecessor Kawasil Fund I – a low carbon technology fund, which was the first private equity fund in the renewable energy ecosystem in East Africa. Areas where we can build from the successes of Kawasil Fund I notably include the development of a strong and diverse portfolio of companies, including those focused on solar (PV, C&I solar) since Fund I launch in 2016. The investment strategy for Fund II was explicitly intended to support the transition towards low carbon technologies and energy systems that are aligned with the SDGs line with that mandate and represent some of the strongest companies across the off-grid ecosystem.
3. Now that the sector has grown and evolved so substantially, the investment focus for Kawasil Fund II reflects this reality. The fund will continue to invest in the same areas as Fund I but will also expand the Kawasil Fund I strategy was very focused on East Africa, namely Kenya and Rwanda. We have observed a strong demand from investors for opportunities outside of East Africa and we are excited about the opportunities. Within our own portfolio, light, Bboxx and BioLite started from original core markets in Kenya and Tanzania but have now expanded to include other countries such as Uganda, Ethiopia, and Zambia, among others. The business models are tailored to local needs in each case, with distinct partners and distribution channels. We will continue to focus on these areas of opportunity, the strategic focus for Fund II.
4. We also note the strong performance on lives impacted and carbon emissions averted or avoided per dollar invested. This is an important metric that we have continued to track as our focus for Fund I. While residential solar has driven strong human impacts, we see the need to deepen our measurement of impact in other areas such as energy access, health, education, and climate adaptation and resilience benefits.
5. We have demonstrated strong mitigation impacts across renewable energy projects and are now looking at how these can be scaled up towards larger targets.

**Co-benefit:** Kawasil Fund II will likely also benefit in line from the changes and observations that Fund I faced. We note that Fund I received backing from supporters of our impact mission but did not benefit from wide scale institutional investment, which is a key difference between Fund I and Fund II. The fund types of capital into the sector. The first tier capital in Fund II is designed to provide much-needed risk premium capital for companies with strong growth potential. This will allow us to focus on and avoid dilution in fast-growing companies. Finally, we note that our companies face a wide range of challenges, while they build their companies from managing integrated business models, operational

## B

### Key Areas of Focus

#### 1. Energy Transition
Firms delivering on global and national energy transition strategies through:
- **Renewable energy (RE)**: Solar, wind, hydroelectric.
- **Clean energy technologies**: Carbon capture and storage (CCS), carbon capture utilization and storage (CCUS).
- **Industrial ("CAIT") markets**: Renewables-based mini- and micro-grids, financial services, grid management/energy ecosystem Internet of Things (IoT), and waste-to-energy circular economy models.

#### 2. Productivity
Firms focusing on low-carbon productivity and efficiency markets including:
- Low-cost energy solutions.
- Recycling: Circular economy, clean cooling, and agro-processing technology.

#### 3. Mobility & Logistics
Firms involved in the following areas:
- **Urban mobility**: Electric vehicles (EVs), shared mobility services.
- **Low-emission transport**: Micromobility, last-mile delivery.
- **Business models and software solutions**: These are driving rapid innovation in low-income markets and creating more climate-resilient, low-emission transportation systems.

### 5. KawSafi Fund II
Will focus on late/venture - early growth stage companies with ticket sizes of $20-$10 million and on its core expertise of scaling businesses.
- **The Management Team** sees the company-building landscape as a key opportunity for investment in Africa, especially given the post-COVID 19 world. The larger fund and ticket size reflect the evolution of the investment landscape in Africa over time.

### 6. Investment Pipeline
The KawSafi Fund II experience demonstrated the market opportunity for investments in the target areas. In the investment pipeline, the KawSafi team has developed an advanced pipeline of 40+ investment opportunities with an aggregate deal value of $500 million across 10 focus markets.
- **Focus markets**: Renewable energy (RE), clean energy technologies (CAIT), low-emission transport (LIMAZ), business models and software solutions (BMAS), most vulnerable people and communities (VRAP), health, well-being, food, and water security (HAWA).

### 7. Eligibility Criteria
Points 3 to 9 below set out the Eligibility Criteria for investments from KawSafi Fund II.

3. Partial contribution: KawiSafi Fund II will invest $1–10 million per company, in an expected 15-20 companies. No single exposure will exceed 20% of the total committed capital. In line with private equity best practices, KawiSafi Fund II will have appropriate limits on investments and industry concentration to ensure risk diversification.

4. Financial instruments: KawiSafi Fund II shall invest a mix of equity, equity linked instruments (including warrants and preference shares) as well as convertible and/or non-convertible securities depending on the investment opportunity.

5. Geographies: KawiSafi Fund II will invest only in companies that have a substantial sales or operating presence in one or more of the focus countries where NOUs have been obtained: Côte d’Ivoire, Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Kenya, Nigeria, Uganda, Zambia, and Zimbabwe. As at 15th June 2023[1], KawiSafi Fund II will not exceed exposure of more than 30% of investable capital in any single country.

6. Technologies sub-sectors: KawiSafi II will invest in low-cost/option productive business deploying climate technologies in the focus countries. This shall primarily include the following types of businesses:

| Segment / sub-sector | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Energy Transition |  |
| Distributed renewables: residential solar | Solar home systems for households (e.g., systems ranging between 40Wp-1kW) |
| Distributed renewables: mesh grid, mini-grid, or other renewable energy | Small scale renewable power generation interconnected to a distribution network (e.g., systems ranging from one group of businesses and household customers (e.g., systems ranging between 500Wp-2.5kW) |
| Distributed renewables: commercial & industrial solar (+ storage) | Solar PV installations (rooftop-mounted or grounded) for commercial and industrial buildings, with battery energy storage where appropriate (e.g., >50 kWp or larger) |
| Energy storage (primarily battery energy storage systems) | Energy storage solutions for consumers and businesses, including residential and commercial-scale battery energy storage solutions (e.g. home energy storage) |
| Biomass energy | Clean technology solutions to produce energy from renewable biomass resources such as wood pellets, briquettes, etc. |
| Battery recycling / 2nd life models | Recycling/upcycling of used Li-ion and other batteries for reuse of component materials or repurposing for the 2nd life batteries |
| Distributed energy resource management systems (DERMS) | Integrated software and smart device systems for remote monitoring and control of distributed energy resources, with reduction in energy consumption. |
| Smart metering | Smart meters, including advanced and off-grid distribution networks, to make power grids more capable of efficiency and reliable operation. |
| Renewable energy financing | Financial solutions for renewable energy companies and projects (including AvA and credit algorithms for) |

## Productivity

### Energy efficiency
Platforms, technologies and equipment that require less energy than baseline alternatives (e.g., energy efficient lighting, heating, cooling, appliances, and industrial processes)

Target improvements in energy efficiency must be at least 20% against a conservative baseline.

### "Green" manufacturing
Manufacturing processes and equipment that use less raw materials or energy compared to baseline alternatives

### Sustainable packaging and other materials
Alternatives to traditional packaging and other materials that are more sustainable (e.g., those with lower carbon footprints for use or disposal) – e.g. bioplastics, multi-use plastics, recycled paper and cardboard.

## Green building materials
Materials such as cement, wood and insulation foam that have smaller carbon footprints during production, transport, use or disposal.

## Waste management
Solutions that avoid waste generation (e.g., composting, recycling) or reduce the amount of landfill waste (e.g. landfill management), including plastics recycling

## Agriculture-water-energy
Renewable-based water pumping, including drip irrigation systems, and other technologies that reduce energy consumption during processing, such as milling, threshing, tilting, harvesting, mechanized planting, etc.

## Sanitation
Technologies and processes that reduce GHG emissions associated with sewage management – e.g., variable speed pumps, anaerobic digestion, composting toilets, biogas capture.

## Waste to energy
Pyrolysis and other energy recovery processes that utilize waste conversion technologies to generate heat and electricity from non-renewable sources

## Cold chain / clean cooling
Clean, efficient cooling technologies (clean gases, use of renewables) for agricultural cold chain & storage, and medical cold chain & storage, including refrigeration systems in health and agriculture supply chains.

## Clean water / wastewater treatment
Technologies that significantly lower the carbon footprint of the provision of clean water and also lower the carbon footprint of waste water treatment

## Renewables based internet access
Renewable power for mobile connectivity, including ESCO models for solar-powered mobile towers

## Mobility & Logistics

## E-mobility
- Electric vehicles (cars, bikes, etc.) and associated business models to help shift energy demand from cars to electric alternatives for fossil fuel solutions.

## EV charging
- Measures to improve the efficiency of EV charging networks, including fast-charge technology, better swap models, and software that optimizes charging infrastructure.

## EV financing
- Financing solutions for electric vehicles and fleet transition to EVs such as asset finance, pay-as-you-drive, etc.

## Shared mobility
