## 7. Minimum threshold criteria by Technology/ Sub-sector: KfW Green Fund II applies certain minimum thresholds for a company to be eligible for investment which are specific to different technologies / sub-sectors:
- **Solar PV installations:** KfW Green II will invest in solar installations of different capacities. These solar installations will likely use as back-up either an accompanying battery storage system, and/or the grid. The technology must have a minimum efficiency rate of 15% and a criterion that the solar installation aims to reduce diesel consumption at site by at least 80%. This criterion recognizes that in many countries, diesel is still used for power generation and that companies are usually economically viable to install battery storage at site. OCF funds will not be used to finance the purchase of existing financing for new diesel generators as back-up to solar installations and mini-grids.
- **Energy efficiency:** KfW Green II will invest in companies selling energy-efficient products/services. In order to qualify, these products must comply with standards where the improvements in energy efficiency are at least 20% efficiency gains against a conservative baseline. The KfW Green II will conduct an independent third-party verification of the company’s claims and will provide validation and validate the potential of the company’s product/service to deliver the minimum threshold criteria.
- **Clean cook technology:** KfW Green II may invest in companies manufacturing, financing, and/or distributing clean cookstoves and/or producing, distributing clean fuels for household cooking or industrial use. The technology must be based on renewable energy sources or other low-carbon technologies, such as improved biomass, biogas, electric, and biofuels. KfW Green II will not invest

In fossil-fuel based cooking technologies, such as LPG.

**Cold chain/storage & solutions:**
KawSafi II is aligned with the transition to low Global Warming Potential refrigerants. Any cold chain/storage companies which KawSafi II invests in will be for the purpose of replacing HFCs with alternatives. The following are the criteria met by KawSafi II will promote the use of climate friendly cooling equipment/applications commercially available in respective local markets: (i) use energy efficient refrigerators (e.g.) fans and passive cooling solutions; and/or (ii) use refrigerants that are aligned with the above criteria and most relevant safety standards, as commercially available in respective local markets.

**Biofuels:** KawSafi may invest in companies producing biofuels for household/industrial use or for alternative energy applications. Such investments will be made only where the production of such biofuels is not a substitute for fossil fuels. The following are excluded: (i) Waste oil/fat; (ii) Seeds or crops that are cultivated in dedicated plantations and used as feedstock for biodiesel production; and/or (iii) Biofuels produced from agricultural residues.

KawSafi II will only invest in such companies where their activities do not contribute to deforestation, land degradation, biodiversity loss, water pollution, soil erosion, or GHG emissions. In making this assessment, KawSafi II will consider the criteria set out in its climate mandate for the production of biofuel and AMS III.AK., Biodiesel production and use for transport applications.

**Asset finance & financing platforms:** KawSafi II includes in its mandate companies that offer financing to smallholder farmers and other micro-enterprises. This is done recognizing the critical importance of asset finance to unlock growth in low-carbon sectors. At the time of KawSafi II’s investment, such companies will have a clear business model and will have already agreed that such financing shall be used to fund activities that are in line with KawSafi II’s climate mandate. Such financing may or may not be provided by the company in the event KawSafi II is no longer an intermediate investor (after the KawSafi II investment has been used by the company in line with KawSafi II’s climate mandate).

In doing so, KawSafi Fund I applies the IFC exclusion list but will not invest in companies that have investments or activities in any of the excluded areas. The full exclusion list is found in Annex 6: ESMs.

### Key

- **KawSail's Investment Process**: Guided by three main policies: (1) Impact Policy, Impact Measurement and Management Procedures; (2) Responsible Investing Policy; (3) the ESG Framework (included in Annex 6). These policies elaborate how the KawSail team evaluates and ensures positive climate impact and the approach to managing environmental, social and governance (ESG) risks and performance in its investments.

- **KawSail's Investment Process**: Invests exclusively in low-to-medium risk and high-impact low carbon projects. KawSail is applying the IFC Exclusion List and has created a list of Excluded Investment activities that the KawSail will not use as screening criteria for investment opportunities. The KawSail team will not invest in a company or will accept investment from investors with business activity that is included on this list.

- **Screening and Eligibility**: In this stage, KawSail will identify investment opportunities and select them based on their ability to achieve the following three criteria:

  - Financial/Commercial assessment: KawSail assesses the information provided by the company applying for investment. This includes financial projections, business plan, and other relevant documents.
  
  - Climate-related risk assessment: KawSail evaluates the climate risks associated with each project or activity for aspects such as unit economics, management team experience, fit for the local market, potential for regional expansion, poverty and climate impact. It will also assess the viability of the business based on its ability to contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and infrastructure to support EV business models.
  
  - Climate-related opportunity assessment: KawSail evaluates each firm against the following to assess progress towards the SDG targets:

    a) Mitigation Impacts: Leveraging global standards (such as CDM methodologies) to calculate emissions reduction impacts, potential investments are analyzed for their contribution to reducing greenhouse gas emissions. The KawSail mitigation calculation methodology estimates future potential to make a significant contribution to KawSail's ESG overarching climate targets.
    
    b) Adaptation Impacts: The KawSail team will conduct an assessment of each investment decision to progress based on the outcomes of this assessment.

The eSG risk screening will be undertaken based on initial assessment of the potential project opportunity. ## Investment Approach

1. The team’s experience leading transactions and managing investments has generated important insights in identifying investment targets, negotiating appropriate terms, and managing investments to scale and ultimately exit.
2. Identify companies with potential to become market leaders – Seek companies with demonstrated traction and replicability in their markets (product-market fit), proven technology that are poised to scale with KawiFund’s capital injection. These companies have a strong management team, a clear vision for growth, a competitive value vision to build a regional / global leader and a demonstrative ability to attract low-cost capital.
3. Develop a robust pipeline of investment opportunities – The team has developed an extensive network by rights from a unique private transaction database based on its direct exposure to ~200 private investment opportunities in Africa over the past 15 years. This allows KawiFund to adopt a more structured approach to valuation, using contingent / variable mechanisms to bridge any gaps in expectations when the opportunity warrants.
4. The team led 9 of its 10 investments from IFC is used to developing its own thesis and identifying opportunities that are not yet on the radar of other investors. This allows KawiFund to drop out of deals when the terms become unfavorable.
5. Support companies with a strong track record – Focuses on deep market and operational expertise in de-risking companies for impact at scale, having extensive experience as operators, investors, and advisors in the African energy sector. The team has developed a unique set of skills and capabilities that are tailored support to its investment focus timing and resource where no marginal value is added.
6. The team’s experience in leading transactions and managing investments has generated important insights in identifying investment targets, negotiating appropriate terms, and managing investments to scale and ultimately exit.

### Key Outcomes from Component 1

- **Outcome 1:** Reduce unabated GHG emissions from renewable energy and energy storage solutions
- **Energy transition** encompasses the different pathways to achieve transformation of the global energy sector from fossil-based to zero-carbon, including renewable energy generation (solar, wind, hydro), battery energy storage.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 39

## B

### Energy decentralized energy systems, grid optimization, biofuels, and energy efficient technologies.

In Africa, the energy transition equation is unique. More than 40% of the population still lacks access to the grid and per capita energy consumption is on average only 1/5th of energy consumption in Europe. Unfavorable and inconsistent policies, coupled with a lack of investment, have hindered progress towards a sustainable and climate-changing climate. African countries need to significantly increase their power output in the coming decades to meet the needs of growing populations.

This presents an opportunity for an accelerated energy transition. Africa has already shown a rapid deployment of renewable energy generation over the past decade. Many African countries are now generating more than 50% of their electricity from renewables, and many have set targets to promote adoption of renewable energy by households and businesses alike. This is expected to have significant mitigation impacts.

- **Increase energy access through investment in decentralized renewable energy for residential use (Mitigation)**
- **Increase access for reliable, low carbon energy for industrial growth in Africa (Mitigation)**
- **Support reduction in changes in land use – especially deforestation (Mitigation)**
- **Support increased deployment of energy efficient technologies and appliances (Adaptation)**

### Outcome 2. Reduced transport and logistics: limited GHG emissions from electric and shared mobility solutions, and greening of private fleets.

Mobile phones and the internet are transforming Africa into a “distributed infrastructure” that moves people and goods enabling market transactions and allowing economies to flourish. The World Bank’s Logistics Performance Index (LPI) shows that Africa has made significant progress in recent years in terms of trade facilitation and logistics competence. This creates a drain on most people in Africa and particularly on poorer populations who are often unable to access these services due to lack of infrastructure, high costs, or limited availability.

New business models incorporating advanced data and sensor technology as well as more efficient vehicles create opportunities for increased energy efficiency and reduced emissions from transport and logistics.

- **Increased deployment of low-emissions transport infrastructure (Mitigation)**
- **Support the development of a sustainable urban mobility system – through electrification and investment in innovative shared public mobile business models (Mitigation and Adaptation)**

### Outcome 3. Increased household & industrial GHG emissions from “green” production, including energy efficiency, agriculture water-energy, waste solutions, materials.

Productivity growth is a key driver of economic development and clean technology access for households and enterprises can be harnessed to help grow economies and reduce poverty across Africa. Productivity gains are particularly important in the agricultural sector where smallholder farmers participate for the poor. It presents income diversification benefits from economies that are vulnerable to climate change, for instance farmers adopting solar irrigation to become more resilient to adverse weather patterns affecting their crops.

In the industrial sector, there is a significant opportunity to increase energy efficiency and reduce emissions in the informal sector – through micro-enterprises and small enterprises serving poor and emerging middle-income households. This can be achieved by providing access to clean technologies such as biogas which has a significant impact on productivity and pay high costs for back-to-power. There is a widening Productivity Gap in the informal sector, where there are opportunities for increasing businesses and individuals often lack the opportunity to utilize this energy for productive ends.

The productivity gap is a challenge across agricultural, commercial, and industrial sectors where energy access and clean technologies can be used by households and enterprises to create value. Mitigation and adaptation impacts expected from this segment include:

- **Increased energy efficiency of manufacturing and other energy intensive industries in Africa (Mitigation)**
- **Support for development of low carbon urban infrastructure (Adaptation)**
- **Increased deployment of energy efficient technologies – through energy efficient cooling; waste to energy innovations and provisions of climate related information (Adaptation)**
- **Support for increased access to clean cooking solutions (Adaptation)**

## Key Objectives and Activities under Component One

1. The investment team brings a combined 40+ years of experience investing in energy and climate technology in Africa and other emerging markets. The team has a deep understanding of renewable energy technologies, including solar photovoltaic (PV), wind power, hydroelectricity, and grid-interactive battery access to deal flow. Through KwaSaF! the team has built up an unparalleled reputation as a leading energy and climate investor in Africa. As a result of this experience, the team is well positioned to lead renewables transformation in Africa. KwaSaF! will should benefit from the continued, insights, expertise, and market reputation of the team that has led to the ongoing success of KwaSaF!

2. Output 1: Wider access to energy services, including clean and productive use assets and services by households and businesses.

3. Output 2: Wider, access to and use of clean energy, clean water, and other productive use assets and services by households and businesses.

The KwaSaF! team targets to deploy equity capital to scale up carbon and climate sensitive SMEs across Africa. The focus is on scaling up the adoption of low-carbon technologies at household and business level. The efforts here have directly to the fund outcomes of i) reduced energy-related GHG emissions from electrification, ii) reduced water-related GHG emissions from improved water supply and sanitation models that deploy low-carbon technologies in an inclusive and sustainable way and that enables people to participate in the economy, iii) reduced agricultural GHG emissions from improved agriculture-water energy waste solutions. Materials below we list the anticipated activities that are delegated to the team.

### 4. Activity 1.1: Fundraising, Market Screening, Pipeline Development, and Equity Investment

#### Fund Formation and Fundraising
The KwaSaF! Fund is an ambitious $200M Fund intending to utilize a blended finance structure to unlock private sector climate investment into the African region. The Fund seeks to leverage its experience in energy and climate technology investments in Africa by deploying models that deploy low-carbon technologies in an inclusive and sustainable way and that enables people to participate in the economy.

- **Sub-Activity 1.1.1: Fundraising, Establishment of the Fund entity and all partnership terms agreed**
    - Sub-Activity 1.1.2: Fundraising, Engagement with development finance institutions, institutional investors, and family offices achieve close final of the $200M Fund.

#### Market Screening and Pipeline Development
The KwaSaF! team has deep experience and expertise in distributing equity capital to energy and climate technology companies across Africa. The team has screened and tracked the success of over 300 Africa-focused businesses comprising over $1B in aggregate investment, including a number of successful exits and investments into lower income markets. This represents a qualified pipeline for Fund II investments.

- **Sub Activity 1.1.3: Research and securing:** The KwaSaF! team has a high-skillset presence to in-depth research and secure deals through its network of advisors, accelerators.
    - The Management team builds on a two decade long experience of Acumen and directly leverages this experience to identify early-stage market where technology tailwinds were driving systemic cost reductions. In the past 12 months alone, the team has identified over 300 companies that have invested with over 20 investors, crowning in more than $400M on follow-on debt and equity, collaborated with leading companies to scale up their business models, and supported a number of early-stage venture/funding growth in climate sectors. The team meets companies in the earliest stages and provides them with guidance and support as they navigate through the high-risk/low-risk assets to the leading companies.
    - When a particular segment is of interest, the team also provides access to its network of leading companies in that segment and pursuing those with the most compelling propositions. The team has privileged access to the energy and climate technology investments in its markets.

## Equity Investment via investment decisions to most impactful, fast growth companies, KewaSafi follows a rigorous and structured investment process from sourcing through evaluation, due diligence and transaction execution.

### Sub-Activity 1.7: Initial Diligence
The initial diligence phase is focused on information provided by the company, tested against the team's market knowledge and research. If it's company's business model, competitive landscape, financials, and other key metrics are aligned for local market; potential for regional expansion; poverty and climate impact, it advances to the next stage. The team then compares KewaSafi's investment proposition with that of its competitors, comparing to other early-stage businesses in the sector, and export networks leveraging local and international entrepreneurial and financial track records.

### Sub-Activity 1.7: Due Diligence
The primary investment memo is the first stage at which an opportunity is presented to the Investment Committee. KewaSafi's Investment Committee consists of a panel of experienced investors with diverse backgrounds, including local and international private equity, development finance and operational experience.

### Sub-Activity 1.7: Climate Impact and Commercial Diligence
The DD process and company assessment includes a positive assessment of its expected contribution to climate change adaptation and mitigation.

### Sub-Activity 2.0: Investment Decision
Following an approval based on the Preliminary Investment Memo, the team proceeds to full commercial due diligence (CDI) with the support of an independent consultant. The team comprises company representatives, customers, competitors, technical experts and others to fully inform the team about the business.

The team utilizes due diligence checklists informed by prior transaction experience, and follows each process to address the unique risks and opportunities of each potential investment. The IC member leads the CDI process with a focus on key areas such as market opportunity, which acts as an ESG Action Plan and, where applicable, a 100-Day Operational Plan.

### Sub-Activity 2.1: Final Investment Decision
The final terms and conditions are formally documented with external counsel advising. Investment documentation includes impact, gender, and ESG considerations. The team then submits the investment proposal to the Investment Committee for consistency with all regulations and governance agreements, and then may proceed to follow-up.

### Activity 1.2: Post-Investment Engagement
KewaSafi is an active investor, taking a board seat and otherwise being actively involved in the oversight and management of its investments. The team's deep experience in the sector allows it to provide valuable insights into market trends and opportunities. The investment team's perspective on industry best practices and local context are sought out by emerging companies as they navigate some of the most challenging business of a fast-growing businesses in emerging markets, and specifically in Africa.

KewaSafi's experienced team is deeply rooted in the rapidly evolving market which has allowed it to build strong relationships with key stakeholders. The team's deep experience provides an unparalleled advantage based on its proximity to managers, understanding of context, political and execution realities, competitive landscape, and the quality of management teams. In addition, the Fund's reputation for international leadership and value add beyond pure capital has given it a powerful competitive advantage in both pricing and portfolio.

Fund is focused on late venture stage (Series A-D) companies and provided up to $5m tokens to support market expansion and scaling growth. The KawiSat II team has led and underwritten most of its investments through a combination of direct investing and setting terms. Typically, it has led club rounds involving other, less proximate global investors.

Recognizing the extraordinary challenges of early-stage growth and capital efficiency, the team has developed a unique approach to support companies in their early stages of development. The KawiSat II approach:

* **Focuses on Financial Management:** Companies are selected based on their financial management capabilities.
* **Supports Impact & ESG Reporting:** Companies are required to report on their projects’ impact and ESG performance.

Annually, and at action plan assessment dates, the team also receives annual financial statements and an annual report on progress against the action plans. Information received from the portfolio companies is used to prepare the quarterly and annual reports provided to investors.

Sub-activity 1.2.2: Board Meetings The KawiSat II team typically requires board representation in portfolio companies, which is driven by its view on corporate governance. The team places the highest priority on adherence to best practice corporate governance policies and strict enforcement of these policies through board meetings.

Sub-activity 1.2.3: Ensuring compliance with the Shareholder Agreement The KawiSat II team oversees compliance with the shareholder agreement for each deal and works closely with its legal counsel to ensure that all agreements are properly executed, signed, and filed. ## Activity 1.3: Leverage Capital and Managed Exits
At the core of KawiFund’s business model is to drive sustainable development in Africa by leveraging capital and managed exits. The Fund will invest in a portfolio of companies (which are held for up to 6 years). Fund's exit strategy is based on selling shares through a company sale to strategic investors, or through an initial public offering (IPO) or secondary market listing. Alternatively, the Fund may choose to manage the business itself as a “management buyout,” or alternatively though a secondary sale of shares to a later-stage investor.

## Component 2: Technical Assistance Facility (TAF)
The TAF is designed to support capacity and knowledge gaps in ecosystem development. It will focus on three key areas:

1. **Mitigation and Adaptation**: The TAF will help mitigate the impact of climate change by supporting companies with adaptation measures.
2. **Gender & Diversity**: The TAF will promote gender equality and diversity within the portfolio companies.
3. **Technology Innovation**: The TAF will support technology innovation to enhance the sustainability of the portfolio companies.

The TAF is designed based on a benchmarking exercise conducted by the team, which included 10 leading TA facilities in climate-focused sectors. The TAF will be implemented through meetings, calls, and country webinars.

### Coverage of TA Services and Opportunities for TA
- Most TA programmes benchmark tend to have a broad climate impact scope and offer numerous services.
- KawiFund’s TAF will focus on specific areas such as energy transition, energy productivity, mobility & logistics in the medium term.
- Provision of TA is either light touch and prescriptive while supporting a broad base of companies or focused but often limited to investment readiness and business development services. There is an emphasis on building strong management systems across all thematic areas.

## Opportunities for more focused TAF for early-stage entities to foster accelerated innovation and deployment of new clean technologies.

### Increased focus on engaging with global advisory entities with a local presence, as implementing partners or TA managers to leverage their extensive market intelligence, high caliber skills, and wide geographical footprint for speed to implementation. This does require a more structured approach to TAF delivery.

#### Partnerships - Opportunity for TAF to broaden its partnership base to extend the impact of the TA:

- **Ecosystem level actors** - these are actors primarily working at the ecosystem level e.g. policy formulators, regulators, and other key stakeholders in the energy sector who can influence market channels. Fostering alignment via two-way feedback loops can optimize the relevance of ecosystem-level interventions that affect the success of businesses operating in those environments. TAF can offer real-time support to these actors to ensure their strategies are aligned with TAF’s business interventions
- **Specialized service actors** - these actors offer a suite of specialized TA services typically targeted at more mature businesses and are able to rapidly crowd-in resources and expertise within their areas of specialization.
- **Market systems actors** - these are actors focused on stimulating pipeline development and early market entry for new technologies, products or services. These actors to simplify TAF activities, especially in new target markets to optimize for speed to impact.

For new target markets for TAF, working closely with partners with traction in these markets should help TAF to access the right expertise and resources needed to support the development of a market strategy that is fully pertaining to beneficiary needs and key local partners that can be leveraged for TAF interventions. This would need to be intentional and proactive in mapping areas of alignment at a programmatic level with different actors, including those outside of the TAF portfolio.

## 5. Eligible TAF beneficiaries and types of support: TAF beneficiaries shall include both KawiSafi II investee companies and other eligible beneficiaries. Other eligible beneficiaries shall include:

- **Ecosystem-level actors** - these are actors primarily working at the ecosystem level e.g. policy formulators, regulators, and other key stakeholders in the energy sector who can influence market channels.
  - Companies not ready for KawiSafi II investment, but with innovative solutions/business models in KawiSafi II focus countries
- **Research institutes** and non-profit organizations advancing climate goals in focus countries;
- **Private sector actors** (e.g. private equity firms) that are aligned to TAF’s business interventions.
- **Training and capacity building organizations** (e.g. ESON) doing skills development for e.g. female technicians, or other groups of interest.

The Eligible Criteria for companies supported under the TAF shall be the same as for Component 1 Equity Fund, as set out in a B3 above in detail. The companies supported under the TAF must be under the technology readiness level (TRL) 4 or higher and have a clear business case for investment.

Beneficiaries under the TAF will receive support in different forms depending on the context and the needs of the beneficiaries, and alignment with the TAF mandate. This support can include:

- **Grant funding** for gender & diversity, climate adaptation, or technology & innovation pilots or initiatives.
- **Technical assistance** in the form of professional services / expert consulting provided by third parties; or
- **Market studies** or research to be made publicly available.

For many of the sub-activities, we would typically need to procure external services of consultants, experts, trainers, and other service providers as part of the implementation. Procurement of these services would be either managed by the Entity (which is an EE), or by the implementing partner (which would be a KawiSafi II entity). The procurement process will follow the TAF application manual in Annex 2 of this RFP. Procurement will always follow the TAF policies.

The KawiSalt team targets to deploy the Technical Assistance Facility (TAF), alongside the fund to scale-up impact (mitigation and adaptation). Below are some of what we see as the anticipated activities that we believe will lead to these expected outputs and co-benefits.

### Activity 2.1-1: Operationalization of the TAF

This includes establishing the key operational structures for the facility (e.g., governance structure, staffing), in addition to the ongoing constitution of the Technical Assistance Committee and approval of the operational manual. It will also include conducting a needs assessment to identify eco-system gaps and portfolio company baseline performance. Depicted below are some of what we see as the anticipated activities that we believe will lead to these expected outputs.

#### Sub Activity 2.1-1.1: Set up of the Technical Assistance Facility

This includes closing of STM in addition to grant agreements with co-financing partners, appointment of the Technical Assistance Committee members and officers and approval of the operational manual.

#### Sub Activity 2.1-1.2: Needs assessment of portfolio and eco-system gaps

This will include identification of partners and development of an approved service provider guide. This will also include engagement of consultants (such as ESG) to carry out a baseline assessment of portfolio company performance and needs assessments for ecosystem gaps to further inform TAF interventions.

### Activity 2.1-2: Technical assistance for enhancing climate (eco.) adaptation and ESG impact through learnings, best practices and benchmarking

The objective of this sub component is to provide support to businesses in deepening their understanding of their impact (through focused research and surveys), identifying how they can improve their performance and develop new products and services that are aligned with ESG action plans (through targeted impact initiatives). Businesses can meet their targets in Research, Development and Innovation (R&D) by developing new products and services for customers (contributing e.g. to scope 1 and 2 emissions reductions), improving the resilience of supply chains (contributing e.g. to scope 3 emissions reductions), increasingly adopting circular economy principles, and building more sustainable and resilient supply chains (contribution e.g. in scope 3 emission reductions). Increasingly, companies are recognizing that ESG performance is a key differentiator for attracting investors and customers as a precursor to investment. Investment in this approach is equipping companies to deliver on their climate commitments, while also ensuring they are not just meeting targets, and failing to deliver on their impact commitments. For the TAF to deliver the impact it desires, it must ensure that the investments made through the facility are having real and true positive impacts for the communities they serve. This goes beyond merely having ESG policies, but having an impact embedded in business operations and lived through the organization.

In particular, there is a need for more focused, dedicated support around climate adaptation - given the lack of data and research, and the fact that climate risks are rapidly evolving

### 9. Activity 2.3: Technical assistance for climate solutions and outcomes that are gender inclusive and transformative

The objective of this sub component is to address inequality on the basis of gender, affecting women's capacity to adapt to and address climate change challenges, as well as ensuring that women have access to resources and services needed to support their adaptation and behavior change. This is through providing services and funding to facilitate mainstreaming of all gender dimensions in climate solutions and outcomes.

#### a. Kwakwili’s gender assessment indicated a high vulnerability to climate change for women in the focus countries with more than 60% of the female population in climate-vulnerable employment sectors. Women are also disproportionately affected by climate change, highlighted between 77% (Kenya) and 91.5% (DR Congo) of self-employed. This indicates that women are more vulnerable to climate change because they are often excluded from the impacts of climate change and have lower adaptive capacity to climate events or hazards and disasters. Women also face a higher risk of poverty, food insecurity, and malnutrition, exacerbating existing gender inequality, worsening the outcomes for women’s livelihood.

- Female access to credit remains low with data indicating this at 24% as 24% of the female population in the focus countries are accessing credit. This is due to lack of collateral and the realization of entrepreneurial aspirations. There are too few female managers and professionals in the financial sector, which is reflected by the gender pay gap. Kenya has the highest % of female professionals at 38% and 41%, respectively, while Congo D R only has 12%. Female professionals recorded in DR Congo are 0%.

- Kwakwili’s gender assessment also indicated that Kwakwili does not have a strong gender commitment, including robust Gender Action Plans and a Gender component of the Kwakwili Fund I technical assistance. As the Fund is corporately managed, it is important to ensure that adequate TAF support is required in order for us to show strong progress on gender goals. The TAF activities will be focused around the goals to address the financing gap for women-led enterprises and women-owned start-ups in focus markets, including access to finance, capacity building, skills development, and mentorship.

- The activities will be delivered for the benefit of female jobseekers, employees, leaders, entrepreneurs, and women-owned start-ups. The TAF activities will also seek to promote gender equality through the provision of technical assistance to Kwakwili’s portfolio companies in terms of carbon ventures. Activities include:

  * **Sub-Activity 2.3.1:** Support activities to promote skills development and leadership representation for women in climate-related sectors - TAF will seek to identify critical skills gaps in key climate sectors and work closely with local and global partners, support the delivery of skills training and capacity building programs, and provide mentorship opportunities for women in the sector. TAF will also seek to facilitate increased representation of women within STEM fields and other areas that are critical to climate change adaptation and mitigation.

  * **Sub-Activity 2.3.2:** Support initiatives to increase entrepreneurial participation of women in the climate sector - TAF will seek to provide access to finance, technical assistance, and/or mentorship support for women-owned start-ups in focus markets. This will include supporting women at all levels including: understanding market trends, securing financing, etc., recruitment of women on boards and advisory committees and senior leadership roles, as well as company management team level.

### 10. Activity 2.4: Technical assistance for accelerating the deployment of new technologies and innovative business models

#### a. The importance is knowledge and skills driven. In the last decade, rapid innovation has spurred the development of low-carbon technologies and the evolution of business models that have enabled commercialization. For example, the global cost of solar energy decreased by 80% between 2010-2017, while wind energy costs fell by 45%. Consumer uptake of renewable energy solutions, particularly solar, has been fueled by progress in technology and innovation, as well as supportive policies, financing and entrepreneurship, consumer education campaigns, household incentives, and accessible methodologies.

#### b. The pace of change required to meet the global net-zero emissions reduction targets is such that without collaboration, cross-pollination of ideas, information exchange, and sharing, the world will not be able to achieve these goals. The objective of this activity is to boost the pace of innovation and adoption by enhancing ecosystem collaboration, knowledge creation, and productive partnership through curating, collating, and disseminating knowledge.

#### c. The activities will be delivered for the benefit of entrepreneurs, policymakers, regulators, business leaders, and other stakeholders in focus countries.

#### d. Outputs under this sub-component are bolstering the evidence base of climate business models and building more technology use cases. In Cluster A countries this will be largely case studies and examples of successful business models that have been developed or tested that focus on similar or focus country. In Cluster B countries, the evidence base will likely be enterprise-level projects and/or pilot programmes.

### 11. TAF mandate and sizing

Acumen’s experience with KawGall Fund I informs the size and activities of the proposed TAF. The design and implementation important lessons learned from our experience in collaboration with KawGall I portfolio companies and the broader venture ecosystem over the past 5 years.

The proposed $10M Technical Assistance (TA) Facility is 5% of the total fund size of $200M. We consider this allocation to be appropriate given that, historically, TA facilities have been used for a variety of purposes including capacity building and technical assistance. The use of TA facilities on average was ~12% of the investment fund amount. Further, a 2014 World Bank policy report states that “technical assistance provided to investee companies of private sector development funds is typically less than capital commitment levels between 7-15%.” This is consistent with our experience in collaboration with KawGall I portfolio companies and the broader venture ecosystem over the past 5 years. The TA Facility funding-to-fund size ratio ranges from around 11% based on an analysis of the KawGall I portfolio.

### 12. Technical Assistance experiences and learnings from KawGall Fund I

We have identified the following key insights and lessons learned from KawGall I fund, which have informed design of the TAF:

- **Role of local implementing partners**: involvement of local partners not just in delivery but in the design of TA strategies and action plans, as well as enhanced cooperation during implementation would extend the impact of the TAF. This is further underpinned by the expanded geographic focus that will be achieved through the TAF.
- **Leveraging local partners can not only foster greater industry but also optimize the relevance and effectiveness of TA services**.
- **TA will seek to proactively map relevant local partners in the respective geographies and leverage their expertise and networks for TA delivery**.
- **Knowledge sharing and exchange will be key to optimal partnerships in order to foster cross-pollination of ideas and best practices**.

- **Ability to drive climate impact and ESG value addition**: From our KawGall I experience, the team has acknowledged the significant cost of managing both the impact and ESG workstreams. In

Further, the broader geographical scope of KwaSefi will likely need to deploy further resources to support the identification of best practices and lessons learned from other countries where they were interested in progressing on the recommendations provided by the ESC assessment but had insufficient capacity to do so.

### Flexible and responsive TAF delivery approach (COVID-19 was an unanticipated shock to global markets, with a significant impact on the business environment for many small and medium-sized enterprises)

By virtue of FMO of more than 60% MSMEs (a majority from sub-Saharan Africa) showing that this has been one of their most challenging years in terms of business operations, the pandemic has undermined their adaptation efforts to the pandemic. This underscores the important role of responsive TAF delivery and support for MSMEs in navigating through these unprecedented challenges on a changing market environment.

- **TAF Delivery:** The TAF has focused on three themes; however, within that, it has been designed to support a breadth of activities thereby maximizing the responsiveness and flexibility to evolving necessary needs.
  - The TAF is intended to be delivered in partnership and close collaboration with beneficiary businesses and the TAF governance and communication approach builds in high levels of transparency and accountability.

- **Demand-driven TAF delivery:** One of the key learnings from KwaSefi Fund is the importance of the unique value proposition that it brings to the market. The TAF has been designed to be demand-driven, with KwaSefi Fund well positioned to support the proactive identification of TA needs and filter these through its network of partners and stakeholders. This will ensure that the TAF delivery is in mitigating potential adverse impacts on the business. As the geographic scope of the TAF broadens, the synergy between the TAF and the KwaSefi Fund will be critical to ensuring that a proactively surfaced market-specific TA needs, especially those that may likely impact a cross-sectoral nature are captured and addressed in an efficient manner.

- **Funding:** The TAF is designed to be demand-driven and flexible mechanisms mechanism between the TAF and KwaSefi Fund to ensure emerging TA needs are swiftly filtered through to TAF in informal meetings, workshops, and other events. This will ensure that the TAF delivery is in mitigating potential adverse impacts on the business. As the geographic scope of the TAF broadens, the synergy between the TAF and KwaSefi Fund will be critical to ensuring that a proactively surfaced market-specific TA needs, especially those that may likely impact a cross-sectoral nature are captured and addressed in an efficient manner.

### Focus on continuous improvement: A learning orientation is key to ensuring TAF support continues to respond effectively to the evolving needs of its beneficiaries. The TAF should be open to new delivery approaches such as proof of concept that foster learning. Continuous adaptation should be entrenched in the TAF delivery approach to allow the TAF to enhance its solutions building on from existing work and lessons learned.

- **Short-term TAF initiatives:** Short-term TAF initiatives should be built into programme delivery and will be informed by lessons learned from longer-term TAF initiatives, as well as insights from various channels forums in order of both validate and transfer knowledge.
  - The TAF should also ensure that the lessons and learnings from projects are captured and reviewed for future project design and planning. This should include collating feedback from beneficiaries on how they have used the TAF support to inform their business.

- **Optimize for speed of deployment:** TAF expedient TA deployment is critical to ensure alignment with fund priorities, as well as to avoid unnecessary delays in providing technical assistance activities due to a misalignment with the emerging needs on the ground. The energy sector in particular is increasingly dynamic and doing all enable to timely provide support is key.
  - A streamlined process for TAF delivery should be put in place that can help unison context specific barriers.

- **TAF will seek to work closely with GCF to support expedient delivery of relevant proposals**

## 13. TAF Impact Framework:

### TAF & Theory of Change

Below is a "sub" theory of change for the TAF, which fits within the broader theory of change for the Fund. The TAF three interrelated theories of change are as follows: IF THE TAF IS ABLE TO MEET ITS MANDATE, THEN THERE WILL BE A PARADIGM CHANGE IN CLIMATE MITIGATION, ADAPTATION AND LIVELIHOOD OUTCOMES FOR HOUSEHOLDS AND COMMUNITIES ACROSS SUB-SAHARAN AFRICA BECAUSE THE GROWTH OF CLEAN TECHNOLOGY AND CLIMATE-SMART BUSINESSES WILL BE ACCELERATED.

| Outcomes | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Climate & Ecosystem Resilience and Adaptation | TAF investments will contribute to the resilience of ecosystems, which in turn will lead to increased ecosystem services. |
| Economic Growth and Livelihoods | TAF investments will contribute to economic growth by creating jobs and increasing incomes for households and communities across sub-Saharan Africa. |
| Technology and Innovation | TAF investments will contribute to the development and adoption of new technologies that are climate-smart, which in turn will lead to increased productivity and efficiency. |

The Investment Committee is the final decision-making body in the investment process.
- It will assess the business model for its financial viability, social impact and climate contribution and make a final decision to approve or reject investments.
- The IC will review/approve transaction requests at key decision gates in the investment process and at exit.
- Review and approve transactions at key decision gates in the investment process and at exit.
- Review and approve transaction requests at key decision gates in the investment process and at exit.
- Review investment and portfolio performance against annual goals and recommend potential corrective actions.
- Review and provide guidance on investment and portfolio management strategy.

KawiFall II will have an IC of up to 5 members. The IC members will comprise experts with a combination of (i) investment experience, (ii) climate change expertise, and/or (iii) social impact expertise. The Manager is currently recruiting at least 2 additional IC members in line with this profile.

The IC will be governed by a terms of reference and engagement acceptance letter which each IC member shall sign. Example language is here: You acknowledge that you have been supplied with, read and have had the opportunity to discuss this document with your legal advisor.

## 6. Impact Review Process

The Fund’s contribution to climate mitigation and adaptation for each investment will be presented as part of the Investment Memo to the Fund’s Investment Committee for consideration and approval. The Fund’s contribution to impact is achieved through the provision of capital as well as through Management support and strategic advice. The Fund’s Investment Committee will review the impact assessment in its Investment Agreements for ESG, KawiGall Fund II will articulate its impact expectations in its Investment Agreements.

## 7. Impact Management Plan

The Impact Management Plan will include (but not be limited to) the following contents:

* Articulation of the Impact Thesis
* Reference to relevant academic literature or primary company data in support of the Impact Thesis
* Identification of the key impact metrics (and any secondary metrics) for the investment using the attached KawiGall Fund II Impact Metrics Framework
* Assessment of the potential risks and opportunities associated with the identified impact metrics, covered by metrics including: GCF Results Areas, lives impacted with access to energy-enabled services, employment creation, etc.
* Projected annual and aggregate impact across the impact metrics (with sensitivity analysis based on business case assumptions)
* Assessment of any risks of negative impacts from company’s business, and how to mitigate, including potential for support from the TAF, particularly with respect to gender, consumer protection and ESG
* Plans for monitoring and reporting on progress towards achieving the identified impact metrics

## 8. Limited Partner Advisory Committee (LPAC)

KawiGall Fund II will have an advisory committee (LPAC) to serve as an advisor to the Manager and the General Partner of KawiGall Fund II concerning the Fund activities and operations, including, in particular, with respect to the identification and resolution of conflicts of interest.

## 9. Governance for the TAF

The TAF shall be a separate entity that exists independently from the KawiGall Fund II, with separate management and governance arrangements.

For the implementation of TAF, the AE shall sign a Management Agreement, in form of a grant agreement, with the TAF (the “Management Agreement”). The TAF shall develop and present for approval by the Trustee Board (the “TBF”) a TAF Manual governing the operations and grant-making processes of the TAF (“TAF Manual”).

## 10. Experience and track record of the AE and EEs with respect to the activities (sector and country)

KawiGall Fund II launched in 2016, is a first-of-its-kind vehicle that invests in venture stage companies in the district energy sector in Sub-Saharan Africa. The Fund has invested in 3 companies across the district energy sector to make a substantial impact in electrification rates in Sub-Saharan Africa through ambitious and innovative solutions. These investments have resulted in over 10 million people gaining access to off-grid solar today, many of whom did not have access to modern energy sources before. There is now a $17.5 billion market for off-grid solar products and services. KawiGall has been at the forefront of this trend, having invested in companies such as Off Grid Electric (OGE), Solar Home Systems (SHS) and BioLite to name just across Africa and, in some cases, into Asia.

As of 30/06/2022, KawiGall Fund II has achieved:

* 170 million people impacted by electrification globally, of which 43 million lives are impacted in East Africa (the primary geographic focus of Fund I). This represents a 3.3X the Fund’s impact target in East Africa.
* 2 million tonnes CO2 equivalent emissions avoided. This represents a 5.6X the Fund’s impact target

## Acumen as a Accredited Entity

12. **Accredited Entity:** Acumen brings experience as a direct investor since 2001 - having invested a total of $16 million of capital into 154 companies. Acumen currently has $84 million in investments under management and $379 million committed. Acumen's local presence in East and West Africa is aligned with the KawGII Fund I target countries.

13. **Acumen became a GCF Accredited Entity in 2016:** Acumen now serves as GP to two funds with GCF funding: KawGII Fund I and the Acumen Resilient Agriculture Fund (ARAF). ARAF also serves as Acumen's own fund.

- **ARAF is an equity fund focused on African agribusinesses that help smallholder farmers adapt to climate change:** The fund invests in companies with a strong track record of delivering social impact and financial returns, including GCF, SIF, Soros Economic Development Fund, Proglio, The Children's Investment Fund Foundation, and others.
- **EMRF, managed by Social Investment Managers and Advisors (SIMA), provides relief capital in the form of equity to smallholder farmers affected by climate change:** EMRF has committed $10 million to date with disruptions brought by COVID-19. EARF closed at $95.4M above its target of $86M in total commitments from GCF, DFIs and other investors.

## B.3 Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Funding from GCF will enable the launch of KawGII Fund II

16. **KawGII Fund I was launched in 2015:** focused on providing energy and climate solutions to smallholder farmers in Sub-Saharan Africa. The fund has already achieved significant results with over $34 million invested, generating $87M in sales revenue and $9M in financial outcomes. An anchor investment of $25M from GCF, including $40M for first loss capital for the Equity Fund, $10M senior capital for the Equity Fund, and $24M grant for the TAF, is expected to be catalytic in scaling up the fund's impact. The fund has already demonstrated its ability to scale beyond the initial scale needed in our target sectors.

### KawGII Fund II

- **The first fund was launched to catalyze a nascent market segment and move it toward maturity:** Several companies have scaled out from pilot and proof stage to become strong regional companies.
- **The energy and climate sectors in Africa are expected to grow significantly over the next decade:** with many private sector players and philanthropic organizations having invested time and resources to support and promote high-potential companies. However, these companies still face significant challenges in scaling up their operations due to later stage strategies that benefit from longer company track records and/or the ability to enter into long-term contracts.
- **The fund has already demonstrated its ability to scale beyond the initial scale needed:** with several companies having tools to de-risk the investments and deploy larger ticket sizes, thereby attracting more institutional capital. The fund's success in scaling up these companies is a testament to their potential for later-stage growth.

### KawGII Fund II Strategy

17. **KawGII Fund I's theory of change is to mobilize capital from large institutional investors:** such as global asset management firms, pension funds, and foundations like Rockefeller, TIAA, and others.
- **The fund has already raised $250 million in committed capital:**
- **Including $148M from DFIs such as AKA and Goldings, as well as $102M from private investors.**

### Key aspects of KawGII Fund II

18. **KawGII Fund I's theory of change is to mobilize capital from large institutional investors:** such as global asset management firms, pension funds, and foundations like Rockefeller, TIAA, and others.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 55

## Introduction

The feedback from private institutional investors has been clear. While they are excited about KawiSafi Fund II and see it as well aligned with their climate targets and objectives, they still perceive the expected returns to be too low for them to invest in the fund. To address this concern, we propose leveraging GCF funding as Junior Equity. While DFIs and concessional funders are important partners for KawiSafi, their financial resources are limited compared to private institutional investors.

## Target Size and Segments

The target size and segments are designed to address these challenges and build sustainable scaling companies. To meet that need, we need to attract capital from commercial investors, who have announced billions of dollars in commitments over the past year for the same purpose as a commercializing market in general and Africa in particular. These asset managers need the capacity to reach targeted markets and are looking for opportunities where they can deploy their capital more efficiently than with traditional investment strategies and desire to deploy large amounts of capital in few transactions. In our market, we have seen that this is best achieved through structured investments which are increasingly utilized for risk management in the energy and climate sectors.

## Equity Commitments

We request a total $50 million equity commitment from GCF, including $28 million of Junior Equity and $13 million of Senior B. In addition to these commitments, we are requesting $2.5M of Grant capital for the Technical Assistance Facility. The Junior Equity is designed with two features: 1) first loss protection (to manage downside risk) and 2) a return structure that provides upside potential in excess returns.

## Differential Performance

The differential performance between the Junior Equity and Senior B tranches is structured as follows:

- If the Fund does not achieve positive returns, the Junior Equity participates in any losses of the KawiSafi Fund II.
- If the Fund achieves positive returns, the Junior Equity participates in a lower preferred return, and then each would ratably share in Fund excess returns.

The Senior B tranche is designed to benefit only private investors in the Senior A tranche, not development finance investors. Concessional capital providers are expected to participate in a separate, Senior B tranche.

## 6. B. Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Key Aspects of Exit Strategy:

- **KawiGulf Fund II’s Business Model:** The fund aims to drive strong exits upon investment maturity.
- **Sustainability Focus:** The fund seeks to build sustainable scalable businesses with demonstrated impact mandates and seeks out potential buyers who share the commitment to continue such a mandate.

## 7. Key Considerations for Exit Strategy:

### Investment Climate Eligibility Criteria:
- **Climate Change Mitigation:** Projects must align with GCF's strategic objectives.
- **Adaptation and Resilience:** Investments should contribute positively to adaptation and resilience in target sectors.
- **Negative Impacts:** The fund aims to ensure that its portfolios do not have any unfounded negative climate impacts.

### Investment Climate Impact Methodology:
- **Validation of Climate Impact Methodology:** GCF's influence and support are vital, as many other DFIs and investors seek to invest only in projects with validated climate impact methodologies.
- **Adaptation and Resilience Resources:** The fund aims to build leading edge methodologies to track our adaptation outcomes.

### Investor Participation:
- **Proper Risk Management Policies:** Active investor participation ensures that proper and adequate risk management policies and procedures are in place.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

1. **Overall impact targets:** KawiSafi Fund II will seek to identify investments that promote radical affordability of clean energy and water solutions for all households and businesses in Kenya. These investments are expected to increase the resilience of approximately 3.5 million people directly and indirectly to effects of climate change and supply up to 10,000 tonnes of CO2e, demonstrating that the focus African markets can transition fossil fuel grids to clean technologies.

2. **Under mitigation:** KawiSafi Fund II is expected to achieve the following:

   - *Through investments in energy efficiency:* KawiSafi Fund II will invest in projects that reduce energy consumption by 30 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (t CO2e) during the fund’s lifetime.

# D2.1 Potential for scaling up and replication

KawiSafi intends to build on KawiFund I (the world’s first climate change fund focused on SMEs that serve BoP populations in developing countries). KawiSafi Fund II will seek to demonstrate the scalability and replicability of the model, with a focus on scaling up and replicating the lessons learned from KawiFund I. The Fund will build upon the lessons learned in deploying the first fund and (with continued GCF partnership) allow KawiSafi to demonstrate how it can be scaled up and replicated across multiple geographies. This will provide a blueprint to a clean-energy future that advances GCF’s goals and that can be replicated in multiple geographies.

The Fund's investments will seek to develop, prove, and scale innovative clean energy technologies and products that all promote low-carbon growth, and support lower income people to live a life that is more dignified and productive. The Fund will also seek to build the capacity of companies to attract private sector investments which should attract further private sector capital into these companies and others, increasing the social and environmental impact.

By investing in for-profit companies, KawiSafi II, GCF, and other investors will seek to create financially sustainable enterprises that continue to operate through internal profits and follow on investment even after the Fund’s initial investment period has ended. The Fund will also seek to build a strong track record of operations within the specific countries of focus. Scale built through the Fund's investments will seek to generate growing entry points for others who may further turbo-boost mobility of the African energy landscape.

KawiSafi Fund II will seek to invest in business models whose operation and scale are directly correlated to climate change mitigation, including those that avoid or remove 5 million tonnes (directly and indirectly) to the effects of climate change and avert 20.9 million tonnes of CO2eq during the lifetime of the Fund. These investments will also seek to build on existing business models that can leverage local knowledge, and strengthen local supply and distribution channels in their countries of operation.

Energy investment is critical for nascent sectors such as the agriculture-energy companies that are still providing energy services to rural communities. The role of private sector investors in these areas will likely be important to: 1) further catalyze private sector investment by signaling commercial opportunities, and 2) provide a source of capital to help finance the development of the infrastructure and closing low-cost debt required to achieve scale for wider income and productivity benefits for smallholder farmers.

# D2.2 Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

Acumen has a 20-year track record of investing in emerging markets. The Fund has been a market leader in driving impact investing, and is a key player in the impact investing ecosystem. As a pioneer in impact investing, Acumen played a key role in shaping the sector’s approach to measurement. Specifically, Acumen was one of the founding members of the Global Impact Investing Network (GIIN), the Aspen Network for Development Entrepreneurs (ANDE) and the Global Impact Investment Network (GNI). 60 Decibels (previously LeanData), one of the leading impact advisors, was spun out of Acumen and has grown to become a global leader in impact measurement. The Acumen team has also been active in publishing well-received reports on energy featuring the work of its Pioneer Energy Investment Initiative (PEII) and its role as a founding member of the Global Alliance for Solar Energy Access (GSEA). Fueling the Future: Which Next? On Off Grid Energy; and Lighting the Way: Roads to Exit in Off-Grid Energy.

Both Acumen Fund and KawiSafi Fund II held numerous open webinars to share and co-create/mouseover knowledge on various innovative approaches to driving strong performance and impact. The KawiSafi team has been active in sharing its lessons learned from KawiFund I with other investors, including accelerators, early-stage companies, entrepreneurs, and others, including its November 2019 event that resulted in a white paper titled “The Future of Energy Access in Africa: A Roadmap for the State of Charge of Early Stage Businesses in Kenya.” In May 2021 the team hosted a virtual panel titled "Beyond the Tipping Point: The future of battery storage and its implications for low-carbon energy access in East Africa." The Funds publish annual Impact and Community reports which provide additional

### Measuring adaptation benefits from interventions is harder than mitigation benefits, as quantifying an increase in adaptive capacity or reduction in vulnerability is harder to do than a reduction in GHG emissions. The GCF has been working with partners such as the Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) and the Equity and Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) by bringing more definition across various indicators in the new TAF framework. This will help to better understand how adaptation benefits can be measured.

### Through KfW/KaSaF, the GCF team is committed to working with partners to develop an evidence base for the quantification of adaptation benefits from the energy and transport sectors which has been limited so far.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 67

## Economic co-benefits: Solar PV is generating opportunities across the globe, creating new jobs and growing income.

Recent research in East Africa found that 34% of households report an increase in economic activity since solar power was installed. In Kenya, 81% of households report an average increase of $60 or more monthly income – equivalent to 14% of per household national monthly earnings. Solar PV is also creating new jobs and business opportunities for the rural economy: 75% of respondents say that solar systems have created new business systems for business or income generation; 81% of which this resulting in increased revenues.

Improvements in energy access are also leading to improved health outcomes, with 90% of households reporting an increase in their ability to cook food and its impacts. Through these investments, KawiSafi II expects to reduce vulnerability approximately 3.5 million people from the effects of climate change and over 20.9 million tonnes of CO2eq emissions over the life of the fund.

1. KawiGulf Fund builds on the Kawafi-Gulf Fund I’s local presence and expertise. A strong understanding of the region from a local perspective drives the investment strategy, network access, pipeline development, and broader stakeholder engagement.

Acumen has local offices in each of the regions where it invests (East Africa, West Africa, India, Pakistan, and Latin America), staffed by local employees who are deeply embedded within their respective communities. The KawiGulf Fund builds on Acumen’s teams across the world with relevant local government, NGO, and private sector partners to ensure that investments are aligned with national priorities.
3. KawiGulf Fund builds on the Kawafi-Gulf Fund I’s geographic scope, intending to invest in multiple countries across Africa and Asia. The KawiGulf team will build on its experience from the first fund by using a ground-up process of investing with Fund I, working with companies as they scale regionally, and identifying new opportunities for scaling up. This approach is designed to ensure that investments are aligned with local scale and replicate certain business models, and the need for new approaches based on local context.
Based on the lessons learned from Kawafi-Gulf Fund I, the KawiGulf team will also look out of a wider opportunity pool of just under US$2 billion across the continent. The KawiGulf team has invested time and resources to understand the landscape in each country and region where it invests. This includes building these networks to identify best-in-class local companies that are meeting the needs of individuals and businesses.
As highlighted in the market study, each of the activities identified are aligned with countries’ national strategies and priorities. The KawiGulf team has also built a strong network of partners who have been active discussions with local investors, industry associations, early-stage accelerators, and entrepreneurs across Africa and Asia. This network is reflected in the KawiGulf Fund I’s engagement through its Stakeholder Engagement Plan. The team has traveled on mission to Cote d’Ivoire, Ghana, Kenya, Nigeria, Pakistan, and Vietnam. The team has also engaged with over 100 investors and entrepreneurs aggregate. The team has also entered into MOUs and NDAs with organizations within the focus countries for the purpose of building a strong network of partners that will be leveraged in the future. ### D. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

1\. Acumen is requesting that the GCF invest $50M Equity capital into the Equity Fund and $2.5M Grant capital into the TAF. This vehicle, and GCF's anchoring investment, will enable KweKaSif to raise additional private sector co-investment of up to $37.5M. The total equity investment will therefore leverage GCF's investment capital on a 4:1 ratio, targeting a $200M fund. We further believe that GCF's $2.5M of grant capital could be leveraged on a 3:1 ratio, leading to a $10M Technical Assistance Facility.

2\. Given the high risk profile of the target market (exacerbated by the perception of higher risk in emerging markets, particularly Africa), traditional private commercial lenders are unlikely to provide co-investment. The proposed investment vehicle will allow GCF's commitment to take the form of Junior Equity, providing a first loss cover for senior, commercial capital providers. This structure is designed to enable the mobilization of up to $37.5M in private equity capital, but rather catalyzing investment by private capital managers seeking to achieve social impact and financial returns.

3\. Acumen and KweKaSif have strong track records of deploying and returning capital. The appropriate internal controls are in place to monitor investments and report to investors.

4\. The Fund seeks to generate positive financial returns for investors. This desire promotes sustainable business models, which are more likely to be market fit, and appropriate pricing and credit policies, to ensure strong businesses achieve significant scale. In turn, it also builds the case that private capital is a viable source of funding for climate action.

5\. Based on our proposed investment vehicle and the resulting reduction cost of $13.67 per tCO2eq ($3.21 per tonne CO2e), we believe this proposal to be competitive amongst other interventions that have aimed to reduce CO2 emissions.

6\. EPA and other federal agencies use the social cost of carbon (SCC) to estimate the climate benefits of reductions in greenhouse gas emissions, including carbon dioxide (CO2). The SCC is defined as the present value of the net damages associated with a one metric ton increase in atmospheric concentrations of CO2 emissions, conventionally one metric ton, each year. This dollar figure also represents the social cost of a reduction in CO2 emissions (the inverse of the social cost of a reduction in CO2). The EPA estimates the social cost of carbon to be between $14 and $38.

7\. Market pricing for carbon credits has resulted in a range of pricing. Recent transaction activity in the Kwale County project area indicates that the price per tonne of CO2e is approximately $50 as at 2023. We note that this pricing is affected by several factors, particularly regulatory environment, and has been subject to significant volatility.

8\. We believe our proposal provides a cost-effective means of reducing CO2 emissions while also having significant mitigation and adaptation impacts for low-income countries and beneficiaries (including those living below the poverty line).

<ref>1</ref> As of 15th June 2023, we received six (6) NOLs from Cote d'Ivoire; Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Kenya, Nigeria, Rwanda, Uganda and Zambia. The AE is in advanced discussions with several other countries, including (without limitation) Ghana, Senegal, South Africa, Tanzania, Togo, and Uganda. Additional countries may be added to the program after initial Board approval, provided they submit an NOL by the publication deadline.

<ref>1</ref> <https://www.epa.gov/sites/default/files/2016-12/documents/social_cost_of_carbon_fact_sheet.pdf>

## Component I: Equity Fund

### Activity 1.1 - Fundraising, Market Screening, Pipeline Development, and Equity Investment

- **Target fund size is $200M**.
- **Marula LLC (Investment manager)** identifies pipeline opportunities with strong climate impact and innovation potential, aligning with the strategy or platform building strategy.

#### Sub-activity 1.1.1 - Fund formation activities
- Pipeline of investable opportunities across all verticals: Energy transition, Productivity, and Mobility & Logistics.
- Initial review of opportunity against investment criteria.
- Pre-final diligence report, risk assessment, and recommendation.
- Final diligence report, including due diligence on financial, legal and ESG matters including recommendations.

#### Sub-activity 1.1.2 - Fundraising activities
- **Marula LLC (Investment manager)** follows a rigorous and structured investment process from sourcing through to execution and transaction documentation.

#### Sub-activity 1.1.3 - Research and sourcing
- Initial review of opportunity against investment criteria.
- Pre-final diligence report, risk assessment, and recommendation.

### Impact Reporting

To ensure that the Fund is achieving its climate objective, the KawiSatF II team will work with each investee company and an external impact expert to develop and track a number of proprietary indicators that will measure progress at the fund and portfolio levels. The Fund will also initiate various Lean Startup activities to test assumptions about the business model and market conditions for the proposed activity, including improved income levels, income stability, access to climate information systems, and other metrics. The Fund will consolidate data for analysis and own insights at fund level on a regular basis. This will help track progress against set objectives and enable the Fund to provide timely feedback to the investee companies.

Given that KawiSatF II will not invest directly in beneficiaries, the team will seek to ensure that investments are made into Companies which

aligned on impact thesis and whose goals are aligned directly with the generation of both resilience and mitigation climate impacts in the countries of focus.

* **Beneficiaries will be assessed by the portfolio companies,** who are required to report data to KaesGill II on a quarterly basis. The KaesGill team will conduct periodic surveys and audits to ensure the data is accurate and up-to-date. Importantly, KaesGill II ensures that it invests in companies that are committed to transparency and accountability.

* **On a quarterly and annual basis,** KaesGill II shall track reported impact, with support from independent impact experts to verify impact performance. The evaluation process is aligned with leading industry standards for the sectors of focus including the energy transition, productivity and other mobility sectors.


### 2. ESG Reporting

* **KaesGill II investors will receive an annual report on the ESG performance of its investee companies,** KaesGill II will actively monitor ESG matters in portfolio companies from point of investment onwards. This will typically include (i) monitoring development of ESG risk areas, (ii) assessing progress against targets and (iii) identifying opportunities for improvement.

* **On an annual basis,** the Fund will conduct an independently run ESG review of each portfolio company using an ESG expert. The purpose of the annual review is to assess ESG performance of the portfolio companies, identify those companies facing significant challenges implementing ESG actions, and provide recommendations on how best to support these companies in overcoming their challenges. This will include a detailed discussion amongst the investment team and the IC to determine how to best support the portfolio companies going forward. Further, the Fund will review the progress made by each company against its targets with the adoption of GCF gaps during deliberation at the diligence stage and/or additional gaps/inputs/additional initiatives to close on action items.


### 3. Mid-term evaluation

* At the time of the Mid-term evaluation, it is not expected that significant development results will have been achieved, since the portfolio will not have achieved full maturity. Therefore, the implementation of the investment strategy will be the focus of the evaluation and will also cover aspects like the number of portfolio companies invested by the Fund, GHG avoidance, energy production, installed capacity, private sector capital catalyzed and total amount of people served/impact.


### 4. Final evaluation

* For the final evaluations it is expected that the fund will have achieved maturity. The end of program evaluation of the Fund will comply with the GCF evaluation policy. An independent evaluation will track key performance indicators and results, as well as gender and other inclusion targets.

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

As an investment fund focusing on innovative businesses in developing markets, KawiSall Fund II and the portfolio companies in which it plans to invest, may face a host of challenges. In line with Acumen’s Risk Management Approach, the following are identified as key risk factors:

1. **Regulatory: Workplace safety; AML and Compliance; Tax matters; SEC; etc.**
2. **Asset Risks: Financial performance; Liquidity; Market risks; Credit risk; operational risks;**
3. **Geographic: Regional security; Political and regulatory stability; Culture; and**
4. **Conflicts and Operations: Governance; Business continuity; Government donor complexity; Fund oversight,**

Identified risks are prioritized based on estimated likelihood and combined magnitude of impact of a given risk event (on a scale of 1-3), with the highest priority being given to those risks that have both high likelihood and high magnitude (3.0) of an adverse event and fewer resources and less focus are dedicated to those risks which have low likelihood and low magnitude (1.0). The prioritization is based on the best available knowledge of each risk, informed by available qualitative and quantitative data, but be ultimately subjective.

Risks identified to respond to can range from continued monitoring to expert consultation, insurance, or proactive prevention measures. The KawiSall Fund II Board of Directors will review all risks and make recommendations, approves the final action plans, and is ultimately accountable for monitoring their implementation.

The Managing Director plans to regularly evaluate the overall risk of KawiSall II and its investments. She will also present a quarterly report on the status of identified risks in a written report and include procedures to mitigate and address such risks. A business risk assessment will be provided to the KawiSall Fund II Board of Directors for their review and consideration.
