# Regulatory Uncertainty

## Description
KawSat will invest in companies providing energy solutions. Energy is a regulated industry and in some countries, governments have the ability to reduce the cost of power to end users. This has the discriminatory effect of crowding out private solutions and creating an even greater gap in generation capacity. Subsidies and other interventions make it more difficult for independent power producers (IPPs) to compete with state-owned enterprises (SOEs). The Fund can assess the impact on pricing and whether a certain market is attractive for investment. Therefore, the main risk is changes to subsidy and other policies that could reduce revenues and may deter investment.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
The Fund will assess regulatory risks in the markets where it operates and will consult with local regulatory agencies to understand the nature of these risks. The Fund will also monitor any legislative reviews across its verticals and ensure potential adverse impacts are mitigated. The Fund will invest in distributed energy strategies that have greater flexibility to move assets to adapt to adverse local conditions as necessary.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

### Probability: Medium
### Impact: Medium

## Exit and Valuation Risks

### Description
KawSat will invest in companies in markets which have limited options for liquidity events / exits due to lack of developed secondary markets, including lack of an abundance of private equity and venture capital firms. Many of the countries in which KawSat Fund II seeks to invest control, to some extent, the repatriation of investment income, and may not allow foreign investors to hold a majority stake.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The Investment Team expects to maintain ongoing relationships with potential strategic acquirers of portfolio companies and will monitor market conditions for opportunities. The Investment Team will be mindful that KawSat Fund II will carefully monitor investments and utilize regional teams to stay abreast of changing markets and company conditions.

KawSat will require its portfolio companies to have annual financial audits to improve transparency of financials and accounting systems.

### Description
In addition to investing equity, Green Climate Fund may utilize alternative investment structures reflective of market conditions. In addition to investing equity, KawSat Fund II may structure debt instruments and equity-like instruments that have a pre-determined maturity date with the option for early redemption in certain circumstances. The team has also used vehicles such as dividends and warrants to help offset the unpredictability of markets.

## Technical and operational Medium Description

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

### Human rights risks in the supply chain

The human rights risks are primarily related to labor practices in the production of solar cells. Four of the world's five largest polysilicon producers are based in close proximity to one another. There is a significant risk that these companies will use forced labor in polysilicon-producing markets.

The increased demand for polysilicon due to the energy transition has led to ongoing investments in capacity at these locations, which could further exacerbate human rights risks and jeopardize the positive impact of the sector and affecting the effect of investment into low-carbon initiatives.

### Mitigation Measures (None)

Please describe how the specific risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of realizing it? If so, to what extent?

It is important to note that due to the expected size and nature of Kaua'i Fund's investment portfolio, Kaua'i Fund does not have a direct role in addressing these risks or their influence they can have over supply chain practices. However, it is imperative that this issue be assessed and addressed to the extent possible.

The team will conduct an initial due diligence research (as with Fund 2). The team will engage its portfolio companies that are either operating in, or sourcing from certain geographies under scrutiny (or other countries where there may be significant human rights risks) to understand their policies and practices within their supply chain. Specifically:

- Kaua'i Fund's investment team will conduct ESG due diligence prior to investment as well as annual ESG monitoring. The analysis includes desk-based review, direct engagement with key individuals within the company, and where possible, third-party verification.
- The team will consider the following factors in its assessment: 1) organizational context and geographic footprint to gain a high level sense of the extent to which the company is exposed to human rights risks; 2) alignment with international standards for supply chains; 3) aligned to core ISO standards on labor; 4) addressing the protection of Human Rights, especially in relation to forced labor; 5) alignment with relevant industry codes of conduct and association; and 5) ethical sourcing policies and practices; among others.
- The team will also engage with third-party ESG service providers, including through its Stakeholder Grievance Procedures — including training of staff, clear explanations and channels on company websites, and in HR Policies, and an anonymous reporting option.
- The team will also work to ensure that the portfolio companies are aware of their human rights and labor policies, including support for strengthening supplier auditing and monitoring practices for Companies at risk. This will be done through engagement with the companies as well as through ESG management, as well as embedding strong requirements in the ESG Action Plans and related reporting.
- The Kaua'i team will report annually on the implementation of the above practices.

The team will also continue to support the work of GOGLA, an international industry group for the off-grid sector. Kaua'i Fund will continue to support the public consultation process with GOGLA in relation to its Code as well as supporting a country body develop strong supply chain traceability protocols, which can help prevent human rights violations.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 104

## Specific Risks

### Grid Solar Waste

Off-grid solar waste represents only a fraction of the total sum of e-waste produced, even in countries with a mature off-grid solar market. For example, Kenya, off-grid solar waste is about 3% of the total e-waste generated annually (100,000 tons), while China and India generate 25 million tons per year respectively. While data on solar waste is not available, it is important to note that Africa contributes the lowest amounts of e-waste per capita globally, estimated at 1 kg/person/year compared to Asia's 4-8kg/person/year.

### Challenges

The challenges of distributed solar products in Africa include a shorter life span of components such as batteries and inverters, which are often not locally manufactured or recycled. This results in low recycling rates and thus difficulty in the collection of the waste components.

### Hazardous Components

The hazardous components found in off-grid solar panels include cadmium, arsenic, and polychlorinated biphenyls which are harmful to the environment and human health if improperly managed. In Africa, a lack of legal frameworks for the management of e-waste coupled with informal recycling practices and waste handling not formalized. Informal handlers trying to exploit the economic value contained in the products by dismantling them without regard to safety or environmental processes and little regard to emission control lead to adverse effects on the environment and public health.

## Mitigation Measures

### Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurring? If so, to what level?

The Kawakii Fund team will manage this risk through its portfolio company efforts and its ECOefficent ecosystems. The Kawakii Fund team will also work with its portfolio companies to integrate circularity into their business models – these include waste-to-energy businesses; recycling businesses; and businesses that use recycled materials in the production of new products. This is expected to reduce the projected scarcity of these resources as global energy transition efforts continue to scale.

The Fund will also support its portfolio companies through its ECOefficent ecosystems by providing product management. This could be through take-back and collection mechanisms, repurposing and refurbishment efforts as well as product design to enhance recyclability. The team will then continue to monitor the progress of these initiatives.

At an ecosystem level, the Kawakii Fund team will participate in industry association efforts. Specifically, it will work with the Global Alliance for Incinerator Alternatives (GAIA) and the consumer protection committee that works on promoting best practice in e-waste across the sector. GAIA is in active discussions to develop a global standard for the recycling of off-grid solar products.

The Fund also expects to support best practice efforts through the Technical Assistance Facility under which it will provide technical assistance with respect to consumer protection standards, quality assurance, and best practices across the industries of focus.

Please describe the risk to the best of your knowledge at this point in time.

Risk of Conflicts in Fragile States

Two countries identified by the KawiSafi Fund – the Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) and Nigeria are ranked 6th and 10th on the Fragile States Index respectively. The index reviews twelve conflict risk indicators including insurgency, terrorism, civil war, political instability, economic collapse, human rights violations, internal flight and brain drain, state legitimacy, public service, human rights and rule of law, demographic pressures, refugees and internally displaced persons (IDPs), and access to basic services. Both countries are experiencing high levels of conflict, violence, ongoing conflict and has been a long-running humanitarian crisis. Nigeria on the other hand ranks low on human rights and rule of law, but still has high levels of IDPs and refugees. Other countries of Focus rank between 25th to 100th on the Fragile States Index reflects different levels of perceived risk by country.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Please describe how the identified risk will be mitigated or managed. Do the mitigation measures lower the probability of risk occurrence?

At the portfolio level, the KawiSafi team will seek to ensure country diversification to limit significant exposure to any one country. The team will also ensure that all investments are in countries where the team can carry out country-based risk assessments which will include both formal and informal political economy factors driving security of the investment; internal power relations and their impact on potential business partnerships; and the ability to navigate these risks with experience navigating these risks in highly fragile countries.

G.1 ENVIRONMENTAL AND SOCIAL RISK ASSESSMENT (MAX. 750 WORDS, APPROXIMATELY 1.5 PAGES)

IFC categorization: Fund II is classified as 1-2 by IFC's Revised Environmental and Social Policy (2021), requiring medium level of intermediation.

Overview of KawiSall’s approach: KawiSall has a systematic approach to integrating environmental, social, and governance considerations into its core operations and investment activities, with robust policies in place. The principles and commitments stated in the ESG Policies are a cornerstone of all business operations of Fund II. Compliance with these principles is mandatory for all portfolio companies. The ESG Policy is divided into two levels:

- **ESM at Portfolio Company level:** Portfolio Companies will be required to adhere to the ESG requirements of the Fund. Each Portfolio Company will establish and maintain its own ESM to assess, address, and monitor ESG risks in line with the Fund’s ESM needs.

- **ESM at Fund level:** At a higher level, KawiSall will establish and maintain the KawiSall Fund II ESM to assess, oversee, and support the management of ESG matters by the Portfolio Companies, as well as overall compliance with the IFC Policy and Performance Standards.

The operational guidelines aim to provide the information needed to support the implementation and operationalization of the ESMs. They are based on the IFC Policy and Performance Standards on Social and Environmental Sustainability Management Systems (2019), which is aligned with the IFC’s Environmental and Social Principles and Standards. KawiSall II therefore intends to implement an Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS) in accordance with the IFC Policy and Performance Standards, as included herein as Appendix 6. KawiSall's approach is aligned with international good practice, including GCF policies and standards, and the ESMs will be consistent with the requirements of the IFC Exclusion List and Performance Standards. KawiSall has a Grievance Policy, aligned with GCF standards.

Agreement to ESG Due Diligence and Monitoring: In depth ESG due diligence is conducted for all transactions in accordance with the IFC’s Environmental and Social Principles and Standards, as well as an agreement upon and embedded in investment agreements. ESG risk and performance are monitored throughout the life of investments through a combination of monitoring tools.

Key risk considerations: Fund II will focus on commercial and residential low-income customers in Africa, and on technological interventions such as electric vehicles, cell towers, off-grid solar products, smart meters, and financing for energy-efficient appliances. KawiSall will encourage its portfolio companies to adopt ESG policies that contribute to climate mitigation and adaptation. Investment companies will be encouraged to manage their investments in a manner consistent with the IFC’s Environmental and Social Principles.

Sexual Exploitation, Abuse, and Harassment (SEAH) risk management is addressed in ESG assessments, which consider the impact of projects on local communities and company policies and procedures. KawiSall will take a positive effect on the lives and livelihoods of people living in poverty by establishing a SEAH policy in 2023.

Indigenous peoples are not likely to be directly affected through the activities of investee companies. KawiSall monitors its investments for any potential adverse impacts, aligned with GCGA’s monitoring procedures and post investment support, which will be continued in Fund II.

Community health, safety, and security, and the environment may be impacted by use and disposal of off-grid solar, ICT, and electric vehicle technologies, including emerging risks associated with radiation from cell towers and smart meters. KawiSall will monitor its investments for any potential adverse impacts on community health, safety, and security for manufacturing, and health effects of electromagnetic radiation. Fund I established strong consumer protection discharge aligned to GCGA’s monitoring procedures and post investment support, which will be continued in Fund II.

KawiSall is committed to improving the production of off-grid solar technology and minimizing e-waste and emissions from this sector. KawiSall will assess the environmental impact of end-of-life products to ensure that portfolio companies either have or will develop strong e-waste policies and procedures.

KawiSall will continue to strengthen its monitoring and management of supply chain ESG management. A particular

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 108

## Participation, control over decisions, assets and resources, capacity, and skills.

The Gender Action Plan further acknowledges that mitigation and adaptation initiatives are more sustainable, equitable, and more likely to achieve their objectives when gender equality is integrated, especially given the disproportionate positive impact of climate change on women. The team will work with KawiFund II to ensure that all investments are made at the household level.

### 2. Program Design

This Action Plan included reflections from KawiFund II’s performance to date and multiple discussions with the Accredited Entity (Amenco) on importance and best practice of integrating gender lens performance in venture capital fund structures. The team also reviewed gender-lens investing frameworks, including those developed by the Global Impact Investing Network (GIIN), which define a value chain specifically promoting female entrepreneurship: leadership, employment, and consumption of energy services. The team will work with KawiFund II to ensure that all investments are made at the household level.

### 3. The following activities are designed to drive integration of gender at KawiFund II:

#### a. Fund Level:
At the Fund level, the KawiFund II Team is committed to ensuring alignment to gender best practices.
- A minimum of 50% female representation in KawiFund II Team employment;
- Integration of gender lens investing practices across the investment process to achieve portfolio-level improvements.

#### b. Portfolio level:
At a portfolio level, we intend to
- Evaluate all our potential investments based on their potential to drive gender inclusion and increase women’s economic empowerment.
- Focus on companies that will primarily use our capital to bridge existing funding gaps for female owned and led businesses. It is currently estimated that only 10% of the venture capital invested in Africa goes to female-led enterprises, while female entrepreneurs are underrepresented in the sectors that attract the most financing; moreover research indicates that all female teams remain less likely to receive financing than male teams, and they are more likely to be rejected by investors. The team will work with KawiFund II to ensure that 50% of our investments are going to all-female teams².
- Build on existing gender-focused work at Amenco. According to studies, 35% of the workforce in renewable energy companies are women compared with 20–25% in the broader energy sector³. The team will use our capital to support our portfolio companies that aim for 40% and above.
- Business models provide women with access to energy services or other goods that disproportionately improve outcomes for women. For instance, a high reliance on biomass for cooking and heating is associated with poor health outcomes for women who spend an average of 1.4 hours/day collecting fuel. Greater access to energy services can improve the time available for education and income generation, which will enable their economic empowerment, thereby supporting the achievement of SDG 5.
- The Fund will also support portfolio companies in enhancing their gender inclusion and diversity practices by providing them with guidance on how to integrate gender considerations into business activities. This will be offered through development of Gender Action Plans for each investment made which are expected to include a gender action plan, a gender policy, and a gender finance structure.
- The team also seeks to support companies in developing policies and procedures to prevent SEAR and provide gender-sensitive and survivor-centred response teams. The team will implement this through direct participation of KawiFund II as board representative on the portfolio.

1) GAV (2020) Negatively Impact, Increasing Venture Capital to Women-led Businesses
2) World bank Blog (2021) As African Venture Capital Booms, Are Female Founders Losing Out? Link

Note: The Information Disclosure (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF funding activities. Under the IDP, project proposals will be subject to the terms of the GCF’s confidentiality policy as approved by the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Information provided in confidence is only to be used for the purposes specified and shall not be disclosed without the prior written consent of the disclosing party. This document does contain specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

* **No confidential information:** The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
* **With confidential information:** The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, are not to be disclosed in full and will only be disclosed in accordance with the terms of the IDP. Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP210

## Proposal name: KawiSafi Fund II

### Accredited entity: Acumen Fund

Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Overview

#### 1.1 Overview

The KawiSafi II funding proposal is an evolution of the KawiSafi Ventures Fund (also known as KawiSafi I), which was launched by the accredited entity [Acumen Fund] in 2016 and was one of the first funding proposals approved under the GCF’s Pilot Window. The fund has invested in low- and zero-carbon technology businesses in two countries: Kenya and Rwanda.

The fund is still in its investment period, and it intends to make one more new investment in 2023, after which it will be liquidated at the end of the management period. Remaining funds are expected to cover fees and expenses over the life fund. KawiSafi II aims to invest in later-stage opportunities and moderately small ticket sizes, ranging from USD 500,000 to 5 million.

- **KawiSafi II is a follow-on fund to KawiSafi I** and it expects to make more investments in terms of both number and size. The Acumen funding proposal aims for an USD 200 million venture equity fund utilizing a blended finance structure that is intended to leverage the GCF’s USD 52.5 million grant. The fund will target investments in companies at the late-venture/early-stage growth that have business models deployed across Africa and are focused on energy, mobility, and logistics.

As of the date of the funding proposal, KawiSafi II had received no objection letters (NOLs) from any of its target countries: Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda, Tanzania, Zambia. The Alem team expects to receive four additional NOLs by the thirty-second day after the submission of the funding proposal.

- **The requested contribution from GCF is USD 52.5 million**, of which USD 50 million is in the form of equity and USD 2.5 million is in the form of grant. The grand funding is to establish and implement a blended finance facility (BFF) that will invest in portfolio companies and the wider energy and climate ecosystem in the relevant African markets.

### 1.2 Impact potential

**Sector: Medium**

- KawiSafi II expects to invest in the energy, mobility, and productivity (clean cooling, agriculture-energy, green manufacturing) sector. According to the project sponsor, these sectors are projected to avoid emissions of 20.9 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO₂e) by 2030, which is expected to benefit approximately 3.5 million people.


---
**Page 129**
---

6. These sorts of prospective impact estimates for fund vehicles are difficult to verify as they involve many variables (e.g., nature of investee companies, execution by investors, timing of investments, and so on). In addition, it is important to note that GCF funds are not diversified in their nature are unable to provide certainty as to the ultimate composition of their portfolio. This noted, the fund has a clear mandate to support projects with high mitigation potential and market responsiveness that often ensures greater effectiveness over time in achieving climate impact targets. The fund also recognizes the inherent uncertainty associated with this level of uncertainty in assessing probable impact outcomes. In this regard, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) is responsible for providing advice on the overall portfolio strategy and a positive assessment including, but not limited to, (i) track record of the fund manager, (ii) target sectors and concentration limits, (iii) pipeline and illustrative investments, and (iv) the overall investment selection criteria.

7. In the case of KawSafi II, GCF can take some comfort from the track record of KawSafi I, which has invested in 10 projects with a total estimated mitigation potential of 25 Mt CO₂e emissions in Kenya and 2.4 Mt CO₂e in Rwanda. Additionally, the KawSafi I portfolio maps to an investment selection process that prioritizes business models with a clear mitigation and/or adaptation impact pathway. The fund manager has demonstrated its ability to identify projects that will carry over to the management of KawSafi II. Further, in reviewing the KawSafi II funding proposal, the TAP found that the proposed investment selection criteria were consistent with the overall portfolio strategy and that the investment selection criteria were reasonably aligned with achieving the impact target estimates made by the fund.

8. Moreover, ITAP notes that the impact potential of early-stage equity investment in emerging market climate businesses can be beyond first order mitigation and adaptation directly linked to the projects themselves. The fund manager has demonstrated its ability in developing countries so that they can grow and deploy climate-friendly technologies across their markets. This is because the fund manager has a deep understanding of the markets, and the networks necessary to transition emerging markets into low-carbon and resilient pathways. This is true for KawSafi II as well where the fund manager will be able to provide access to a network of service providers, local financiers, and project development professionals. The economic activity and technology adoption potential in these countries by businesses can engender in their own market conditions, which are not just for their success but also for others. This is evidenced by demonstrating that African markets can leverage fossil-fuel grids to deploy clean technologies.

9. KawSafi II also expects to make investments in commercial and industrial solar power generation; battery storage applications targeted at displacing diesel generators; and energy productivity improvements through the deployment of energy efficiency measures in cooling towers that are currently powered by diesel generators that result in significant CO₂e emissions.

10. Additionally, the fund expects to make investments in the mobility and logistics sectors focusing on scaling up companies that market electric vehicles (EVs), with a view to increasing the use of EVs as an alternative to fossil-fuel-powered vehicles for last-mile delivery services in urban areas. The focus of this aspect of the program is on funding electric two-wheelers. The ITAP does not have concerns about the overall investment selection criteria, which considers that assumptions concerning the number of EVs to be supported by the fund is overly optimistic, and the default values for distance driven and fuel saved are not conservative.


---
**Page 130**
---

In the case of public transport, ITAP noted methodological errors in the calculations. However, mitigation interventions are only estimated to account for a relatively small minority of total expected emissions.

12. The mitigation potential of the indicative investment pipeline provided by the AE is more significant than previously thought. This means that a greater proportion of portfolio companies will be able to reduce climate vulnerability and enhance the resilience and adaptive capacity of the geographic regions where they will operate. Acumen’s adaptation claims rest on the assumption that the investments will be made in projects that are resilient to climate change that will impact food security (e.g., surface water and groundwater changes), harvests, and other agricultural activities.

13. More credibly, the funding proposal for KawiSafi II includes specific examples of where it expects to see adaptation outcomes, including investments in resilient food systems that could help agriculture adapt to climate change. The indicative investment pipeline provided by the AE points to energy sector investments that can have impacts on resilience if applied to the agricultural sector. However, the indicative investment pipeline does not include this. Nevertheless, ITAP considers that the adaptation impact potential of KawiSafi II is particularly difficult to quantify because it depends heavily on how the investments are made and what outcomes they produce.

14. ITAP has reduced its estimate for the overall mitigation potential of KawiSafi II in light of the new information provided by Acumen regarding the expected adaptation outcomes. The ITAP encourages the AE and the GCF Secretariat to consider models that provide a more active role for the GCF in the assessment of climate impacts to better ensure alignment of interest between the AE, the GCF, and the fund manager. This is because the relationship between the AE and the fund manager is incredibly close, as in KawiSafi I.

15. ITAP proposes that the indicative investment pipeline provided by the African Energy Facility (AEF). The TAF will seek to assist both portfolio companies and the wider clean energy ecosystem in Africa. Portfolio companies will receive support to, among other things, develop the tools needed for climate risk management, access new markets, and manage financial and climate impacts, which Acumen notes is a current gap in the market. The ecosystem support will go toward developing the enabling environment for investment in Africa’s clean energy sector by industry associations and academia; support development and maturation while ensuring that the TAF shares the positive impacts of its activities among all members of society, especially women.

15. Overall, ITAP regards the proposal's impact potential as Medium.


### Paradigm shift potential

16. KawiSafi II intends to build on KawiSafi I by demonstrating the scalability and replicability of the approach. If successful, KawiSafi II will raise significant private sector capital into climate-smart agriculture in Africa. The indicative investment pipeline provided is intended to demonstrate the investibility of low-carbon businesses in Africa and support firms that need to grow quickly without any government or donor funding. This could unlock a large flow of funds to guide to deliver clean energy. ITAP considers that this paradigm shift potential lies in the ability of KawiSafi II to demonstrate that investing in an African clean-energy future is feasible and profitable.

17. The ITAP considers that Acumen has a moderate likelihood of succeeding. Its concerns rest primarily on the details of Acumen’s ratio for capital as KawiSafi I had more mainstream investors than KawiSafi II, which highlights the importance of having a diverse investor base. KawiSafi I relied extensively on investments from high-net-worth individuals and impact-first investors. KawiSafi II is expected to have a similar structure but with a greater proportion of institutional investors. The ITAP considers that this could lead to a paradigm shift. Based on its assessment, ITAP is not wholly convinced that this goal will be


---
**Page 131**
---

achievable at the scale sought by KawiSll. Concerns stem from (i) the status of investor outreach and the type of investors further along in discussions; (ii) KawiSll Investors re-apply, and (iii) the ability to attract new investors.

18. Although the KawiSll I track record gives comfort around impacts and the screening capacity of the GCF, there are concerns that the current level of funding may not be sufficient for the will matter to prospective investors. For instance, since 2016-KawiSll I has deployed under USD50 million and has only achieved one partial exit. Arguably this is in line with the initial funding levels, but it also means that KawiSll I has not been able to demonstrate a track record that KawiSll II can deploy much larger amounts of capital and ultimately provide liquidity for Acumen’s portfolio companies. The concern here is that KawiSll I may not be able to generate sufficient scale and quality, or it is unable to successfully develop bankable financing opportunities, which could result in the need to raise additional rounds of debt or equity capital.

19. Notwithstanding the above, ITAP does not consider it a lost cause. For example, Acumen’s other GCF-supported funds (excluding KawiSll I) have been able to attract a more diverse range of investors and have achieved exits in a number of cases despite the challenges during a very difficult economic period. The macroeconomic environment over the past 3-4 years has been challenging for many emerging markets, with some experiencing significant macroeconomic situation with rising debt, falling currencies and inflation. Bank value is no substitute for equity capital, but it can be an important source of liquidity. However, it does differentiate KawiSll II from other efforts in the market that have stalled in fundraising.

20. The KawiSll I team informed ITAP during the review that it is currently in advanced stages of discussions with a number of investors and expects to close deals shortly. However, ITAP takes some comfort from the AE’s claims that various institutional investors have confirmed to Acumen that they have successfully navigated their own investment committee approval process for KawiSll I and are now ready to commit additional capital. This provides financial resources into moving into commercial, legal, tax and environmental, social and governance (ESG) due diligence.

21. If KawiSll II can raise capital successfully, ITAP considers that investing in-for-profit companies that seek to scale clean energy technologies or promote low-carbon growth can contribute significantly towards the achievement of the SDGs and the Paris Agreement goals. However, paradigm shifts will be achieved only if the fund is able to build a track record performance and demonstrate its ability to generate returns for investors. The presence of smart owners and encourages new investment managers to structure new funds in this sector. Demonstrating commercial success can attract new actors and reduce the cost of capital into these sectors.

22. The investor composition that Acumen are interested is to be financially sustainable enterprises, which have a track record in generating returns for investors. Successful portfolio companies can attract additional investment in addition to the impacts that they make on the environment and society. If KawiSll II is able to generate an exit for its investors this could then have a significant paradigm shift potential by contributing to the credibility of the fund’s ability to deliver impact. This could be a virtuous cycle that can truly support a paradigm shift in development trajectories.

23. Overall, ITAP sees the potential for KawiSll II to effect paradigm shifts as Medium, due to the above-mentioned concerns around financing and adoption impacts.


1.4 Sustainable development potential
Scale: High


---
**Page 132**
---

24. The funding proposal presents a credible pathway for sustainable development impacts in addition to the potential for knock-on effects on market development and demonstration impact (for example, see section 1.3). The extent of these impacts depends on the ultimate makeup of the KawSII portfolio and the success of those investments. This is reflected by the Fund’s track record, investment thesis and indicative pipeline. However, the funding proposal and ITAP’s engagement with investors and other stakeholders in the early-stage climate investments will be in support of business plans and technologies that can have a measurable beneficial impact (i.e., not just speculative or theoretical).

25. Another important facet of the sustainable development potential of KawSII is its focus on African markets. The region has significant development needs, while it is expected to bear the brunt of climate change impacts. According to the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC), temperature increases in sub-Saharan Africa are expected to be higher than the global mean temperature increase by 2050 and will have a major impact on food security.

26. The ITAP also notes that Africa contributes only 3 per cent to global GHG emissions; however, it is disproportionately affected by climate change. In addition, many African countries are developing low rate of consumption. Approximately 55 million people in sub-Saharan Africa do not have access to electricity and must rely on kerosene and diesel generators for lighting, while another eight million use wood as their primary cooking fuel. This leads to deforestation and dangerous levels of indoor air pollution. Africa’s energy consumption and management is a major challenge that needs to be addressed if the continent is to create economic opportunities and jobs that can lift its people out of poverty. If the Paris Agreement targets are to be met, it will require a significant increase in renewable energy capacity. This will not only possible if the companies that will need to provide low-carbon services and technologies exist as a plan for Africa’s development. The KawSII funding proposal is an important contribution to developing this ecosystem of low-carbon businesses.

27. The ITAP notes the following specific potential sustainable development contributions:

| Environmental co-benefits: reduction of air and water pollution and improved resource efficiency in mining and crop applications. Reductions of kerosene, diesel, and other carbon-based fuels |
| --- |

28. Employment and income generation resulting from renewable energy projects could create 10 million jobs by 2050. The renewable energy sector has already generated 370,000 jobs in off-grid electrification as of 2019, with strong representation of women workers and young people. In addition to the economic benefits, the KawSII funding proposal seeks to invest in innovative companies that promote inclusive and sustainable industrialization. The KawSII funding proposal also credits a focus on increasing gender inclusion in the early-stage climate investments, particularly through business accelerators and incubators to increase the participation of women in their cohorts.

29. The funding proposal allows for investments in biofuels. The ITAP notes that investments in biofuels have a high risk of creating negative climate and sustainability problems


---
**Page 133**
---

if not well screened for and managed. First generation biofuels are generally produced from food crops, such as corn, soy, and sugarcane, which make them unattractive from a socio-economic perspective. The overwhelming majority of so-called wastes are already currently used for other processes (e.g., animal feed). In addition to the use of waste materials, other types of raw material (e.g. the waste "bagasse" is also used as input material for paper production; used cooking oil can be processed into biodiesel; and palm oil mill effluent can be converted back into edible oils, then revert to using primary palm or soy oil). Thus “waste” are end-of-life products but many of them have alternative uses that could be more beneficial than being diverted to biofuel production. Using such wastes can cause displacement emissions when diverted for biofuel production. Additionally, the use of expedient feedstocks can lead to indirect land-use change and deforestation, and potentially higher total GHG emissions compared with fossil fuels, plus other environmental and social problems.

The proposed funding proposal does not include a credible methodology for determining the direct as well as indirect GHG emissions from biofuels. Notwithstanding that biofuels are projected to play a small role in KawiSall II, lack of an effective screen against biofuel projects could lead to significant displacement emissions and other adverse climate impacts. This would be particularly problematic if the funding proposal needs to exclude financing of biofuel projects with GCF proceeds.

Overall, ITAP rates the sustainable development impact potential of KawiSall II as high.


### Needs of the recipient

Scale: High

1. KawiSall II requests investment from the GCF on the assumption that concessional funding is required to attract capital from private sector investors who may be risk adverse to direct investments in renewable energy.
2. The ITAP considers that there is a clear need for additional finance to support Africa's low-carbon sectors, particularly early-stage risk capital that can fund the scaling up of the low-carbon technologies and projects. However, the ITAP notes that the current funding gap in emerging countries continue to face persistent funding gaps regarding the need to crowd-in private capital into renewable energy.
3. However, ITAP notes that the prioritized countries represent very different economic realities and considers that KawiSall II will face a challenge in operating across 10 different economies with varying levels of development and institutional capacity. The operational challenges in originating deal flows across such varied economies and having sufficient know-how to manage them are significant.
4. In terms of the needs of the recipients, ITAP notes that some of the priority markets are starting to demonstrate improved conditions in attracting finance. It is clear that there remains a real need for additional concessional funding from the GCF to support these markets. With a tenor (10 years + 2-year renewal) of KawiSall II, there is a risk that GCF concessionality could contribute to crowding out private sector investment in some priority markets if it is assumed that other priority markets are tremendously underdeveloped, could potentially limit deal flow in these countries and put undue pressure on KawiSall II's manager to deploy primarily in the quickly improving markets.
5. The funding proposal has features that mitigate the above-mentioned risk, particularly the cap on investment per country and the focus on a limited number of projects. However, experience of Acumen as reflected in its management of multiple funds in the region.


---
**Page 134**
---

Cumulatively, Acumen has directly invested USD 149 million in 157 companies operating across 13 countries.

The GCF also takes comfort from the waterfall structure and preferred return structure that appears to be reasonable and within the current expectations around blended finance. Under this structure, the senior equity (private sector investors) will receive their returns first, followed by the junior equity (public sector investors). Additionally, the GCF will recover its equity investment prior to any distributions for the senior equity (private sector investors) preferred return. This mitigates the risk that the Kawasil II portfolio could be underperforming and not generating adequate portfolio performance. Additionally, the proposed preferred return for senior equity and junior equity is aligned with the blended finance principles of the GCF. The finalization of the fund structure, fundraising efforts will clarify whether the above senior preferred return (and hurdle rate) suffices or whether a higher hurdle rate is required to ensure that the fund remains sound. ITAP considers that the senior rate is not overly subordinated by the junior equity.

Nevertheless, as a general observation [not just limited to Kawasil II] the GCF Secretariat could benefit from further analysis of the blended finance structure and its implications which can raise concerns around over-subscription or market distortion impacts of GCF concessionality. Market Intelligence can be a useful data point in ensuring that GCF concessionality is fit for purpose.

Overall, ITAP rates the sustainable development impact potential of Kawasil II as High.


### 1.6 Country ownership Scale: Medium-High

Kawasil II is a global programme that will be implemented in 10 countries. To date, Kawasil II has received six NDA's in support of this proposal (Dote d'Orno, Democratic Republic of the Congo, Ethiopia, Kenya, Liberia and Nigeria) with four others (Ghana, Kenya, Senegal, and South Africa). The GCF expects to receive the remaining NDAs before Q3.

The Kawasil II team is based in Nairobi, Kenya, and plays an active role in the impact and investment communities there and travels frequently throughout the region. Acumen's office includes a Kenyan team, which is important for understanding consumer needs and the cultural context.

Kawasil II is intended to build on the geographical scope of Kawasil I by having a pan-African focus. The proposed Kawasil II portfolio will be focused in 10 countries with a footprint informed by the ground-up process of investing in the Kawasil I fund and from working closely with Acumen's existing portfolio companies. The total investment pool for Kawasil II has been identified, spread across most of the 10 priority countries.

The AII has provided credible information that each of its proposed investment sectors is aligned to the GCF’s strategic priorities and the SDGs. In preparing the funding proposal for Kawasil II, the Kawasil II team engaged public and private sector stakeholders in each country to ensure that the proposed blended finance interventions are aligned with individual country contexts. According to the stakeholder engagement plan provided with the funding proposal, the Kawasil II team is engaged in outreach to civil society organizations (CSOs) in Ethiopia, Kenya, Liberia, Nigeria, South Africa, Uganda and Senegal, as well as delivering online stakeholder webinars for each country.

The GCF Secretariat will provide quarterly reports on progress to the national designated authorities (NDAs) in the countries of focus across the following different channels: (i) Kawasil II will provide quarterly reports to the NDAs with comprehensive information on the fund progress and insights. (ii) The GCF Secretariat will also hold regular virtual or face-to-face calls with the NDAs to present the fund's progress and insights, as well as to hear of the latest

# Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: Medium-High

## The AE is requesting that GCF commit US$50 million of equity capital over a 10-year fund (up to US$25 million per year for up to two years). This vehicle, and GCF’s support investment, is intended to enable Acumen to raise additional capital from private sector investors. The proposed structure is as follows: the initial US$40 million of equity in this project structure will be tiered and will include a junior and senior equity layer: US$ 40 million on GCF funding would be in the form of junior equity; the remaining US$10 million would be in the senior equity. The fund will also have a debt component, which is intended to provide enhancements to attract capital from private institutional capital providers.

### Tiered Equity Structure

- **Tier 1:** Junior equity (US$40M): This tier is designed to manage downside risk and a differential preferred return (to improve upside potential). The first loss protection absorbs any losses of KawiFall if the fund does not achieve positive returns. The differential preferred return will be paid out before any junior equity holders receive their higher preferred return before the junior equity participants in a lower preferred return, and then each would share equally on any remaining profits.
- **Tier 2:** Senior Equity (US$10M): This tier is intended to benefit only private investors in the Senior A tranche, not development finance investors. Concessional capital providers are expected to participate in a separate, senior B, tranche.

### KawiFall II

- **KawiFall II also represents an additional US$25 million TFG, which is designed to augment the activity of the fund.** The funding provides revised leverage on GCF’s equity capital for the TAF. This implies that GCF’s US$ 2.5 million of grant capital could be leveraged on a 3:1 ratio, resulting in an additional US$7.5 million of private sector investment. The size of the target fund has now been set at US$400 million, which is in line with other similarly situated TAFs.
- **GCF concessionality is expected to leverage over the overall KawiFall II structure to attract additional private sector capital and support the fund’s activities.** The concessional funding will support company balance sheets to reduce debt and support follow-on equity rounds. If successful, this could have a significant mobilization impact of GCF’s investments along with commensurate social and climate impacts.
- **KawiFall II will use its capital to make equity, quasi-equity and convertible debt investments in the target companies.** The fund is expected to invest US$10 million per year over 10 years. The funds’ 10-year horizon is designed to provide KawiFall II’s future portfolio companies several years to grow their businesses and become more attractive for follow-on equity rounds, loans, and other similar financial arrangements can be impacted given the lack of equity finance in many African markets for low-carbon businesses.
- **Lack of access to early-stage investment capital is a well-documented barrier to scaling up clean energy projects.** KawiFall II is a fire-profit fund. For-profit funds have much greater impact potential in growing markets than grant-based facilities or other less commercial models. The need to


---
**Page 136**
---

generate a return (albeit a concessionally supported return) permeates through all aspects of capital provision from company selection to follow-on investment decisions, portfolio management and exit strategies. This is intended to provide an incentive for investors to participate and mitigate against reputative risk and effort. Additionally, only by demonstrating a return model can the Fund meaningfully affect the ability to mobilize additional funding into key geographies and sectors.

However, a for-profit fund only succeeds if the interests of the manager and investors are aligned. The Fund is designed with this in mind. A capital structure that provides for a realistic carried interest will typically struggle to raise capital from truly commercial investors. In the case of KawiSII, the Fund has chosen to provide for a 25% carried interest on fees over the life of the fund. In part this is due to the long tenor of the fund and arguably the continued need for the Fund to be able to make follow-on investments in companies where it considers that a reasonable account can be made for the fee structure sought by Acumen. However, it considers that the carried interest should not be so high as to create an undue sensitivity on fees and allow fees to seemingly be determined by follow-on private sector investors.

Broadly, ITAP views positively the cap of 25 per cent GCF funding in KawiSII and the linked conditions. The Fund has chosen a reasonable level of leverage for the capital raised. The funding proposal envisions a subscription agreement that ties GCF funding to a calculated return on invested capital (ROIC) over the life of the fund. This ROIC is also regarded as reasonable and the GCF veto rights on extensions are preferable. These conditions add to the overall efficacy of the approach. The ITAP generally encourages the GCF Secretariat to avoid any further discussion or negotiation with respect to the terms of the Fund, other than linkage to actual third-party funds raised. As noted above, it is difficult for the GCF Secretariat to actively participate in negotiations on the terms of a fund that has already been established and place a reasonable onus on the fund sponsor to deliver on what it promises through limits on the prospect of follow-on investment, downpayment shortcuts, short capital raise windows with GCF veto rights and so on.

The ITAP also views favorably the fact that both the GCF and the fund manager are contributing equally in terms of equity. The Fund has therefore been provided with a red flag for follow-on investors.

Further, ITAP views favourably the fact that GCF funding is structured in such a way that it would be difficult to replicate by private capital and does not involve other public or quasi public entities such as development finance institutions that may eventually participate in KawiSII. The Fund has chosen to provide for a 25% carried interest on fees over the life of the fund, giving the risk profile offered by the underlying KawiSII investments (incapsulated by the perception of the GCF) and the Fund’s own capital structure.

The ITAP notes positively that KawiSII emphasizes its focus in collaboration with its investment sectors, seeking to serve as lead investor and attracting co-investors to contribute further funding. The Fund has chosen to provide for a 25% carried interest on fees over the life of GCF concessionality to enhance the entire market ecosystem without diluting the return sought by investors. The Fund also provides for a downpayment shortcut in relation to KawiSII it views as acting as market makers and not just passive money takers. (The ITAP also appreciates that Acumen has agreed to provide a 25% carried interest on fees over the life of the fund.) According to the funding proposal, KawiSII’s investments helped to attract nearly 14 times the initial amount in further equity and debt into the investee companies.

As previously noted, the Fund is designed with the intention that it they typically provide more than just funding. Successful funds contribute both money and direct management support, often through active board engagement not in some capacities at the manager’s discretion or as a condition of investment. This hands-on approach helps to incentivize companies who can access new information and networks and to fund investors that are


---

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

Overall, this funding proposal has the potential to generate climate impact benefits as well as broader sustainable development benefits for the target countries through early-stage investments in biofuels businesses. The TAPP endorses this funding proposal with the following conditions:

### Conditions to be met prior to the effectiveness of the funded activity agreement for KawiBioF (II)

(a) **Delivery by the Accredited Entity to GCF:** In a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretary.

1. **Adopt an enhanced assessment methodology framework** to calculate the GHG emissions impact of potential investments into biofuels projects, which shall be applied when appraising such investments in accordance with and to indicate GHG emissions impacts of individual biofuels projects over their extended life cycle (the "Framework"); and
2. **Engage an independent third party** to review biofuels projects based on the results of the application of the Framework at the appraisal stage (the "AE");

(b) **Inclusion of the limited partnership agreement for KawiBioF if an appropriate provision requiring the general partner and manager of the fund:**

1. apply the Framework when appraising potential investments into biofuels projects;
2. in relation to potential investments into biofuels projects, invest only in biofuels projects that meet the EU; and
3. conduct a life-cycle review of such projects, as applicable, engage an independent third party to conduct an extended life-cycle reviews of the project, and make all reasonable efforts to ensure that the fund complies with each subject using the approach set out in the Framework, and submit the report of the review available to GCF on a prompt manner; and
4. apply the Framework when appraising potential investments into biofuels projects;

(c) **The AE shall ensure that the general partner and the manager:**

1.


---
**Page 138**

2. In relation to potential investments into biofuels projects that meet the IC, and

3. in relation to investments in biofuel projects, as applicable, engage an independent third party to conduct extended life-cycle reviews of the biofuel project, including a review of the GHG emissions from such project(s) using the approach set out in the Framework, and make the report available to GCF in a prompt manner.

60. Biofuel projects that have higher GHG emissions compared to the baseline plus resulting in environmental and socio-economic problems. Absent the inclusion of adequate safeguards to determine the direct and indirect GHG impacts of biofuels the risks from biofuel projects are too high.

61. Beyond the above conditions, IFAP has also identified certain challenges that KawSafi II will face; however, IFAP has identified sufficient capacity and planning on the part of Acumen to address these challenges.

# KawSafi Fund II: Annex B Gender Assessment and Gender Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Part I: Introduction

The KawSafi Fund II is an ambitious $200 million venture equity fund intending to utilize a blended finance structure to unlock private sector climate investment into emerging markets. The Fund will focus on five countries in Africa: Ethiopia, Kenya, DRC, Uganda, Rwanda, Ghana, Nigeria, South Africa, Côte d'Ivoire, Senegal, and Madagascar.

The Fund will address gender barriers at three levels discussed further below:

I. Barrier 1: Closing the Financing gap that exists in investing in women-led founded enterprises.
II. Barrier 2: Increasing participation and representation of women across the three areas of focus: (1) energy transition; (2) energy productivity; and (3) electric mobility;
III. Barrier 3: Increasing adaptive capacity of women to climate change across the countries of Africa.

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Barrier 1: Financing Gap in Investing in Women

There have been many recent advances on women's rights globally, including many changes in laws to promote equal rights for women; however, about $30 billion is needed on progress toward gender equality.

According to McKinsey, even for the countries with the highest female representation in parliament in Africa, there is a significant drop of -50% of the share of women in entry-level positions and only 12% as top leadership roles<sup>4</sup>.

The share of firms owned by women as top managers in South Africa has been reported to be as low as 16%.

The funding landscape mirrors this reality. It is estimated that only 7% of emerging market venture capital goes to women-owned businesses. Female founders are underrepresented in the sectors that attract the most venture capital<sup>5</sup>. Women-led teams receive less funding than all-male teams, and they receive smaller amounts if they do not all-male founding teams receive US$24 for each US$1 going to all-female teams<sup>6</sup>.

## Barrier 2: Increasing Participation and Representation of Women in the Renewable Energy Industry with a Focus on (1) Energy Transformation; (2) Energy Productivity; and (3) Electric Mobility.

The solar value chain is generating thousands of employment opportunities in emerging markets. In South Africa, for example, the solar sector supported ~370,000 Full-time Equivalent (FTE) jobs across East, West, and Central Africa, and across South Asia with over 50% of these jobs in sales and distribution. This is expected to grow to over 1 million by 2030.

While data on female representation in the African renewable energy sector is not available, (IRENA estimated 32% women's employment including both grid-connected and off-grid renewables<sup>7</sup>). The results of a survey conducted in Uganda<sup>8</sup> indicate that women in the Off Grid Solar (OGS) industry are represented in sales and distribution as microentrepreneurs, and less in technical maintenance.

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## World Bank Fund to Focus on Women in Energy Sectors

### Key Barriers to Female Participation in Renewable Energy

#### Barrier 3: Increasing the adaptive capacity of women to climate change across the countries of focus.

- **Climate change presents significant challenges to socio-economic development, especially for countries in sub-Saharan Africa where the majority of the economy depends on climate-sensitive sectors, including agriculture and forestry. Climate change is also expected to worsen existing poverty and exacerbate inequalities, especially for those disadvantaged by gender, age, and disability. Women are particularly vulnerable due to their limited access and control over productive and economic assets and resources that could help them address the threats posed by climate change.**

- **Gender inequalities remain among the deepest and most pervasive of all forms of inequalities. The intergenerational transmission of poverty is a major gender issue as one of the socio-economic factors that influence the capacity to adapt to changing environmental conditions. Nonetheless, women are not just helpless victims but powerful agents of change.**


---
**Page 145**
---

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Part II: Gender Assessment

To better understand these barriers across each country of focus, the KawSafi Team conducted a comprehensive gender assessment to understand the roles, rights, needs, and opportunities of women in relation to climate change. The assessment was informed by existing literature, ongoing research, feedback received through the stakeholder consultation process (on person or through online webinars) and learnings from KawSafi Fund II that invested in the distributed renewable energy projects (e.g., solar home systems, biogas digesters, and water pumps) across 180 countries.

The gender assessment was important to highlight the potential disparities in the vulnerabilities of men and women and how this is likely to be impacted/worsened by the effects of climate change. The assessment also aimed at identifying the key barriers to women's empowerment, identification of potential interventions and assessment of impacts generated at the beneficiary level.

The assessment reviewed the Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI), Gender Inequality composite indices as well as other country specific indicators. These are highlighted below:

### Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI)

The SIGI is a cross-country measure of discrimination against women in social institutions (formal and informal laws, social norms, and practices) across 180 countries. The SIGI covers four dimensions of discriminatory social institutions: spacing major basic economic areas that affect women's lives.

#### I. Discrimination in the family;
II. Restricted access to education;
III. Restricted access to productive and financial resources; and
IV. Restricted civil liberties.

The SIGI’s variables quantify discriminatory social institutions such as unequal inheritance rights, child marriage, violence against women, and unequal and unpaid property rights.

As highlighted below, the countries that KawSafi Fund II intends to invest in are ranked between 22 and 46 on the SIGI. In these countries, in general, labour force participation rate is lower for women than for men. Discriminatory social norms that confine women to reproductive roles also limit their access to education, training, finance, and networks.

In addition, biased perceptions of women’s abilities and discriminatory educational norms tend to prevent women from accessing decent work and confine them to specific sectors of the economy such as domestic services, informal sector, and agriculture. This leads to gender-based segregation in science, technology, engineering, and mathematics (STEM) fields – and thus accentuate gender-based segregation in the labour force. Furthermore, discriminatory social norms and biases related to women’s access to education, finance, training, and networks also hamper women's entrepreneurship in these countries.

Source: OECD-UIS 2019

### Gender Inequality Index (GII) - GII is a composite metric of gender inequality

A low GII value indicates low inequality between women and men, and vice-versa. GII reflects gender-based disadvantage in three dimensions—reproductive health, empowerment, and the labour market.

It shows the extent to which women are disadvantaged relative to men on these dimensions and how far they have achieved in these dimensions. It ranges from 0, where women and men fare equally, to 1, where there is complete disadvantage for women compared with men across all three dimensions. The rankings of the countries covered by KawSafi Fund II are highlighted below.

## Rwanda
Rwanda is a small but growing market, with a population of ~13 million people and a Gross Domestic Product (GDP) Current of $10.1 billion, according to the World Bank. Before the COVID-19 pandemic, Rwanda enjoyed strong economic growth, averaging over seven percent GDP per year from 2014–2018. The country’s economy grew at an even faster rate of nine percent in 2019 thanks to strong growth in industry, construction, services, and agriculture.

### Rwanda's Achievements and Commitments

- **Rwanda stands out globally for its efforts in advancing gender equality and women’s empowerment, particularly in labour force and political participation. Nearly two-thirds of its parliamentary seats and more than half of cabinet positions are held by women. Significant progress has also been made in many sectors.**

- **Rwanda's achievements include the decline of maternal mortality rates arising from women's improved access to health services; a 90% reduction in child marriage through the One Campaign targeting over 500,000 signatories and three commitments bridging the gender digital divide by 2020, triple girls' enrolment in TVET and eradicate Gender-Based Violence. More recently Rwanda has committed to the UN Women’s Generation Equality Platform for Action and Innovation for Gender Equality and committing to closing the gender digital divide by 2026.**

- **Rwanda's impressive results are due to strong political commitment and institutional level accountability, with a dedicated Gender Commission, the National Council on Gender, the National Gender Machinery, The Ministry of Gender and Family Protection (MIGEPFRO), the Gender Monitoring Office (GMO), the National Women’s Council (NWIC) and the Rwanda Women Parliamentarians’ Forum.**

- **Rwanda has also adopted a strong and effective mechanism for mainstreaming and monitoring of gender equality.**

### International Commitments

- **Signatory to several gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, and the African Union Protocol on the Rights of Women in Africa. The country has also adopted a number of gender-related policies and strategies, including the National Gender Policy, the National Strategy for the Reduction of Poverty, and the National Gender-Based Violence Prevention and Response Strategy.**

### Gender-based violence (GBV)

- **Gender-based violence (GBV) is a significant problem in Rwanda. According to a 2018 survey by the National Institute of Statistics of Rwanda (NISR), 54% of women have experienced some form of GBV in Rwanda. With more than half of women in Rwanda having experienced physical violence from an intimate partner, forced marriage is another form of GBV in Rwanda. Although child marriage has declined significantly since 2013, it remains a serious problem. According to the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF), 4% of girls in Rwanda were before the age of 15, and 22% are married by the time they reach their 18th birthday. Child marriage is associated with poor health, education, well-being, and can perpetuate cycles of poverty and gender inequality. Female genital mutilation/cutting (FGM/C) is also a form of GBV in Rwanda, although the prevalence is**

## Political participation

As highlighted above, Rwanda has done particularly well in increasing female political participation. Women now hold a female majority in Parliament, with 43.1% in the Chamber of Deputies and 26 female members in the Senate, ~36% in the Senate, as per 2021 reports.

Article 10 of the Constitution of Rwanda states: The State of Rwanda commits itself to upholding the following fundamental principles and ensuring their respect: building a State governed by the rule of law; promoting equality between men and women; and ensuring that women and men who are affirmed by women occupying at least thirty percent (30%) of positions in decision-making bodies, including the executive branch, the judiciary, and the elected legislature. Women also got one-third of all cabinet positions as well as the important posts of Supreme Chief Court Justice and Speaker of Parliament. Women initially held 39 out of 80 seats (49% of total) in the Chamber of Deputies at the 2010 election, rising to 56 out of 80 seats (70%) in the 2013 election, even though reserved seats for women were 30% as per the constitution*.

# Access to financial resources/knowledge and skills/other

Rwanda has made noteworthy progress towards increasing women’s financial inclusion and closing gender data gaps. According to a 2016 FinScope survey*, 86% of women have access to formal or informal financial products and services, as compared to 90% of men. Of the 85% of women who have access to financial products and services, 74% use them for business purposes (mainly driven by the growth of informal savings groups). This falls short of the target of 80% formal and 100% informal access for women. By comparison, 74% of men were found to have access to formal financial products and services, with 65% using them for business purposes (mainly through commercial banks) and 35% through non-commercial financial institutions such as microfinance institutions or savings groups. The 2016 FinScope also revealed that a higher proportion of women (24%) than men (17%) relied exclusively on informal sources for banking. There is also a significant gender gap in the use of mobile money, with only 38% of women using it compared to 64% of men.

The 2016 FinScope observed only a slight difference in savings rates with 16% of women not saving at all, as compared to 13% of men; and while women and men saved for similar reasons (to cover unexpected expenses or emergencies), women were more likely to borrow from informal savings groups. Additionally, women were more likely to borrow from informal groups or informal lending institutions.

*Access to Finance Rwanda, 2016: FinScope Insights: Gender and Financial Inclusion Rwanda: Promoting greater financial inclusion of women in Rwanda

friends and family, rather than through a commercial bank. The 2016 Fintech Show found that women's access to formal finance had increased significantly between 2013 (39%) and 2016 (45%).

- Rwanda has significantly promoted STEM education across all levels of study. For example, in 2019, Rwanda introduced the newly developed education curriculum referred to as the "New Competency-Based Curriculum" (NCC). This curriculum is designed to promote gender equality.
- The new curriculum underpins building a knowledge-based and technology-led economy through well-aligned curricula that are inclusive of all students. Despite this progress in promoting STEM subjects, despite gender parity at primary level in Rwanda, girls are consistently underrepresented in STEM subjects at upper secondary and tertiary levels. Any type of underrepresentation inevitably leads to reduced opportunities for women in the workforce.
- The causes of such underrepresentation are rooted in social norms, whereby mathematics and science are often seen as male domains. This is particularly true when it comes to girls at an all-too-frequent lack of female role models in STEM - especially in school settings - but also negative stereotypes around girls' mathematical abilities, and lower levels of confidence among girls than boys.
- The gender gap in STEM subjects is further exacerbated by the limited encouragement given to girls by teachers. This is partly due to a lack of awareness about the importance of encouraging girls among teachers and school leaders, meaning that girls do not receive the encouragement they need to pursue these subject areas.

## Barriers faced by female headed households, youth, the elderly and other traditionally marginalized groups in Rwanda

- Female headed households, Youth, elderly people, and other traditionally marginalized groups in Rwanda face several barriers in accessing energy and mobility. In Rwanda's case, the legacy of the 1994 genocide has left many women as heads of households with young children or school-age children.
- Limited financial resources and lack of access to credit are significant barriers that prevent female headed households from investing in productive assets such as solar panels, quality infrastructure, particularly in rural areas, limits their mobility and access to markets, healthcare, and education. In addition, limited access to information and education on available technologies and training opportunities further exacerbates the situation.
- Cultural norms and societal expectations often limit women's access to mobility and energy decision-making. There is a need to address these barriers through targeted policies and programs that promote gender equality in renewable energy sector, provide financial inclusion, and provide education and information to marginalized groups.

## Women in energy in Rwanda

Rwanda is a country that has made significant progress in promoting gender equity in the renewable energy sector. According to a report by the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA), women make up 32% of the country's total renewable energy workforce, which is significantly higher than the global average of 22%. One of the reasons for this high percentage is the government's commitment to gender equality in the energy sector. The Ministry of Gender and Family Promotion, in collaboration with the Energy Development Corporation Limited (EDCL), has implemented several initiatives aimed at promoting women's participation in the renewable energy sector through training, mentorship, and access to finance.
- Another effort to promote gender equity in the renewable energy sector is the establishment of the Rwanda Women's Renewable Energy Association (RWREA). The RWREA aims to support women's career development in the energy sector and to promote gender equality in the

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Workplace

The network provides mentorship, training, and networking opportunities for women in the energy sector.

The Rwanda Energy Group (REG) is actively developing a gender mainstreaming programme to increase the percentage of female staff. REG has a gender expert and gender focal points and plans to establish an all-female workforce for the power plant at the new Kivu North project.

While private sector data is not available, reports indicate that challenges still remain in mainstreaming gender in the energy sector:

I. The high cost of energy results in poor access to an energy supply and a lack of energy services. As indicated above, a lack of affordable energy access increases gender inequalities.
II. Inadequate disaggregated data: data is lacking on gender and energy in general. For example, women are less likely than men to have access to electricity for lighting, affordability, decision-making areas for entrepreneurship opportunities and new technologies.
III. Limited capacity and resources: energy-related institutions are ill-equipped to effectively analyse the gender needs for the sector and generate sufficient data.

## Women in Energy Productivity in Rwanda

The Productive Use of Energy (PUE) sector in Rwanda includes activities such as agriculture, agro-processing, and other income-generating activities that rely on access to energy. It also covers women's participation in these sectors, which is often limited by lack of resources, affordability, decision-making areas for entrepreneurship opportunities and new technologies.

Women play a critical role in these sectors; they often face significant barriers to accessing energy and power, as well as the skills needed to use it effectively.

According to a report by the International Finance Corporation, women in Rwanda are less likely to have access to energy services than men, with only 21% of women having access to electricity compared to 35% for men. This is due to limited financial resources, lack of education and awareness, and cultural norms that limit women's mobility and ability to participate in decision-making.

This lack of access to electricity limits women's ability to engage in income-generating activities and can have a significant impact on their health and well-being. Additionally, women often have limited control over energy resources within the household and are less likely to use them effectively, which can further constrain their participation in the productive use of energy.

To address these challenges, the Rwandan government has implemented a range of policies and programs aimed at promoting gender equity in the PUE sector. These efforts include the National Gender Policy, the National Gender Strategy Plan (2013), the National Gender Machinery Strategic Plan (2014-2018) and the Rwanda Gender Diagnostic Note: Addressing Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment for Inclusive Growth.
