# Women in Mobility in Rwanda

In Rwanda, particularly in the mobility and logistics sectors has been a priority for the government. However, women continue to face significant barriers and discrimination in accessing these sectors. According to a report by the International Trade Centre (ITC),13 women face challenges related to gender stereotypes, social norms and beliefs, institutionalized cultural biases, and inadequate skills and training with women occupying only 11% of jobs in the transport sector.

Women are underrepresented in leadership roles across all sectors, but particularly in the transport sector. Women are often concentrated in lower-paying and less skilled jobs, such as sales and customer service roles. Women are also underrepresented in leadership decision-making positions.


According to a report by the World Bank, "women in Rwanda are less likely to own and use motorbikes than men." This is due to gender stereotypes, social norms and beliefs that prevent women from using certain modes of transportation. Women face challenges related to gender stereotypes, social norms and beliefs, institutionalized cultural biases, and inadequate skills and training with women occupying only 11% of jobs in the transport sector.

In terms of two-wheeler motorcycle transport mobility in Rwanda, there are few women who own and operate motorcycle taxis. According to a study by the African Development Bank, women are underrepresented in leadership decision-making positions across all sectors due to perceptions that motorcycle taxi driving is dangerous and not suitable for women.


The root causes of these gender gaps are multifaceted and include cultural norms and biases that limit women's access to opportunities, lack of access to education and training, and limited access to financing and networks.

### Vulnerability to Climate change and needs of the beneficiaries

The high % of women in vulnerable employment in Rwanda and the low levels of secondary and tertiary education mean that women are disproportionately represented in the agricultural sector for most women means that women in Rwanda are likely to be disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change and have lower adaptive capacity to climate events or hazards.

Although data on gender and energy are still scarce, recent studies in Rwanda have provided some insights into the gender dimension of access to energy. The Gender Office has compiled available data in the "Gender Profile in the Energy Sector" report (GMO, 2018). The World Bank's Global Gender Data Portal also provides a wealth of information and relevant data on energy access for male and female-headed households (Koo et al., 2016). In 2020, the Power Africa East Africa Energy Programme published an assessment on women’s participation in Rwanda’s energy sector.

Women spend more time than men acquiring fuels for cooking. Nationally, this burden for women averages 73 minutes per day compared to 36 minutes for men. The time burden on women is even greater when they have to collect firewood. Women often need to spend twice as long as men on preparation activities to use the collected wood as fuel, such as chopping it into smaller pieces. Firewood collection remains a huge workload for women, which tends to engage in other productive activities. Scales of alternative

# South Africa

- South Africa had a population of 59.3M people (32% being rural), as of 2011. Poverty rate in 2015 was 18.9%. The GDP in the same year was $335.4B, or -7.0% real growth in the same year. Gini coefficient is 62%, which is one of the highest in the world.
- South Africa has the most advanced, diversified, and productive economy in Africa. The country covers 1.22 million square kilometres and is the world's largest producer of platinum, vanadium, chromium, and manganese.
- South Africa enjoyed relative macroeconomic stability but it faced increasingly strong headwinds due to the COVID-19 pandemic. The economic expanded by 0.8 percent in 2018 and by 0.2 percent in 2019. Inflation was at 4.5% in 2018, falling to 3.7% in 2019. GDP contracted to $320 billion in 2020, as a result, GDP per capita dropped to 2005 levels (US International Trade Commission).
- South Africa's Gender Inequality and Gender Index indicates a low level of gender discrimination in social institutions, with a 2019 value of 0.23. This indicates that strong laws providing equal rights for women and men are in place in the country – but some international standards have not been met yet.
- South Africa is a signatory of several gender-related international conventions including: Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW); Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action; International Covenant on Civil and Political Rights; Protocol to the African Charter on Human Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa.
- South Africa has ratified the International Labour Organisation (ILO) Convention No. 111 on Discrimination in Employment and Occupation, which prohibits discrimination on the basis of gender, colour, race or religion. The country also adopted a policy to address sexual harassment in the workplace. South Africa adopted the Code of Good Practice on the Prevention and Elimination of Violence and Harassment in the World of Work in 2020 due to rising cases of sexual harassment in the workplace mostly affecting people under vulnerable employment.
- Gender-based violence (GBV) is a major problem in South Africa, with women and girls disproportionately affected by physical, sexual, and psychological abuse as well as economic abuse. According to statistics from the South African Police Service, over 177,000 cases of GBV were reported in 2020, with many cases going unreported. Physical and sexual violence are common forms of GBV in South Africa. Domestic violence is a major problem, with women


---
**Page 155**
---

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

often experiencing physical and sexual abuse at the hands of their partners. Rape and sexual assault are also present, with many cases going unreported due to social stigma and fear of retribution.

- South Africa has one of the highest rates of violence against women in the world with between 40–50 reported rapes per annum. Police data showed rapes and sexual offences went up 13 percent from January to March compared to the same period last year, and there were more cases in the first three months of 2022 compared to the same period last year. Psychological abuse is another form of violence against women that can be as harmful as physical violence. Emotional manipulation, controlling behaviour that can have serious long-term effects on their mental health and well-being. Economic abuse is also a significant problem, with women often being denied access to money or resources by their partners. Women may struggle for basic needs such as food, shelter, and healthcare. This can make it difficult for women to leave abusive relationships and can exacerbate their vulnerability to other forms of GBV.

- The South African government has recognized the importance of addressing GBV and has implemented several policies and interventions to address this. These include: 1) The National Strategic Plan on Gender-Based Violence and Femicide; In 2020, the South African government launched a National Strategic Plan on Gender-Based Violence and Femicide. This plan outlines a range of interventions, including prevention, response, and support services for survivors of GBV. 2) The Sexual Offences Bill: This bill aims to strengthen the legal framework addressing sexual offences and to provide better protection for survivors of sexual violence. The law includes provisions for improved investigation and prosecution of sexual offences as well as enhanced protections for victims of sexual violence. There is also a Gender-Based Violence Command Centre which is a full-time helpline that provides counselling, support, and referrals for survivors of GBV as well as Thuthuzela Care Centers which provide services to survivors of GBV including medical care, forensic examination, counseling, and legal support. The centers are located in hospitals across the country and provide a safe environment where survivors can receive the necessary support.

## Political participation

In South Africa, women have the same rights to vote and stand for election as men, and the Constitution guarantees equal participation in political life. The country is often cited as a success story of women's representation in government with one of the highest numbers of women in parliament at the world at 43%, and a raft of progressive women-friendly laws passed by the legislature.

Like other instances of high female-political representation, such as Rwanda, women parliamentarians are not necessarily more supportive of feminist policies. In South Africa, some women parliamentarians have been openly critical of the feminist agenda, choosing instead to toe the party line.

## Labour force participation

- South Africa has a population of 60M, with a female labour participation of 46.2% (2021). The country's infant mortality rate is 34 deaths/100,000 live births (2021). The female population with at least some secondary education (age 25 and older) was 31%.

- Most of the employment is in agriculture and services, constituting 64% and 34% of the population in employment, respectively.

- 77% of women are employed. This is lower than the 80% of the female population estimated to be in vulnerable employment which is defined as contributing family workers and own-account workers as a percentage of total employment.

The gender pay gap – or the difference in wages between men and women for the same type of work – remains one of the major obstacles in achieving gender equality in South Africa. It affects women in the middle and upper wage bands the most.

Unequal monthly earnings persist in households with 38% of home-owners managed by women earning less than R10,000 per month compared to 25% for men. Also, 48% of female-headed households support extended family members compared to 23% of male-headed households doing the same. In addition, women face discrimination in the home and have a greater responsibility towards unpaid domestic work with power relations skewed in favour of men.

### Access to Financial Resources/Knowledge and Skills/Other
Access to finance in South Africa is not equal with race and gender being important variables in the lack of access. Women are disproportionately affected by these biases.
- The Broad-Based Black Economic Empowerment Act promotes "increasing the extent to which Black women own and manage existing and new enterprises, and increasing their access to economic opportunities." However, the Financial Sector Charter only specifies gender targets for staffing, considered to be low, and does not include consumption and female participation as consumers overall.

### Barriers Facing Women Headed Households, Youth, the Elderly and Other Traditionally Marginalized Groups in South Africa
Traditionally marginalized groups in South Africa face a number of barriers in accessing energy and modern services. These include Black South Africans, particularly those living in rural areas or townships, women, people with disabilities, and LGBTI+ individuals. One major challenge is the lack of access to public transport options for these groups across the country, which can make it difficult for people to access job opportunities, education, and other essential services. This is particularly true in rural areas where public transportation options are often limited or non-existent. Additionally, some traditionally marginalized groups may not have access to modern forms of energy, such as electricity and clean cooking fuel, which can limit their ability to engage in economic activities and improve their quality of life.

*PwC report, Accountants Today (February 2013)

# Gender Assessment and Gender as Social Inclusion Action Plan

* Other barriers may include discrimination and lack of access to financial resources, which can make it difficult for people to purchase or maintain vehicles, or to invest in renewable energy solutions.
* South Africa has launched the Renewable Energy Independent Power Producer Procurement Programme (REIPPPP) and the Green Growth Fund. South Africa may include government policies to promote investment in public transportation infrastructure as well as initiatives to expand access to modern forms of energy and increase financial inclusion.

## Women in energy in South Africa

At last year's UN Climate Conference, COP21, South Africa launched its US$ 5 billion Just Energy Transition Partnership (JETP) with the World Bank. The JETP is a follow-up to the Green Energy Transition Partnership (GETP) announced at COP20 the previous year. The JETP partners, which include France, Germany, the UK, USA and EU, have pledged to raise some of the required finance via grants and loans for South Africa's energy transition.

The investment plan earmarks funding for decommissioning coal power plants, expanding renewables capacity, and supporting the development of new industries investing in sustainable manufacturing, such as of new energy vehicles, and in skills and training.

* Given the importance of implementing the just transition in South Africa, the conversation is dominated by concern about how changes to industry will affect male employment. Yet women are affected too, particularly those working in informal sectors where they are more vulnerable to the changes wrought by South Africa's energy transition.
* With their male partners out of work or underemployed, women are already pushed to the margins of the economy, often working in the informal sector.

* If South Africa's energy transition is managed well, it then could increase opportunities for women. For example, the country's growing solar PV and (or) e-mobility sectors could provide opportunities for women who have been historically underrepresented or marginalized as noted above.
However, this will require awareness and capacity building.

According to a study by the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA), South Africa has one of the highest gender gaps in renewable energy. The gender gap is global average of 32%. This gender gap is attributed to several root causes, including limited access to education for girls, particularly in science, technology, engineering and mathematics (STEM) fields, as well as cultural and societal norms that limit women's participation in the workforce.
The South African government has recognized the importance of promoting gender equality in its energy transition. The Integrated Resource Plan (IRP) 2019-2030 includes a target to achieve an 8/50 gender split in the renewable energy sector by 2030. The IRP also includes a gender action plan and strategy that seeks to address the root causes of gender inequality in the energy sector.

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## South Africa

In South Africa, there are significant gender gaps in access to energy for productive use, which has implications for economic development and gender equality. According to the 2020 Energy Access Survey by the International Finance Corporation (IFC) and the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO), women in South Africa are often excluded from accessing productive resources such as land, credit, and training, which limits their ability to engage in agriculture and other income-generating activities. This exclusion is compounded when women are seen as a "male domain." As a result, women are less likely to benefit from improved productivity, however, they are more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.

### Women's Mobility and Logistics in South Africa

Gender equity in the mobility and logistics sectors in South Africa remains a significant challenge. Women face barriers in accessing opportunities within these industries. Women are underrepresented in this sector, particularly in decision-making positions, due to societal and cultural norms, as well as systemic gender biases. According to the World Economic Forum's Gender Gap Report 2021, South Africa ranks 85th out of 156 countries for gender equality in the workforce.

### Data on Women in Logistics

There is a data gap regarding women in the mobility and logistics sectors in South Africa. However, a study by the National Business Initiative found that women made up only 14% of employees in the logistics sector in South Africa in 2019. Additionally, women often face gender-based violence and harassment within these industries, which can lead to unequal pay.

### Two-Wheeler Motorcycle Industry

In terms of the two-wheeler motorcycle transport sector, there is a growing number of female riders. However, women still face challenges such as harassment and safety concerns in this industry. Organizations such as the International Transport Forum have highlighted the need for policies that promote women's rights and security in the transport sector, including the two-wheeler motorcycle transport sector.

### Vulnerability to Climate Change

South Africa has a high % of female employment in the services sector, indicating lower vulnerability to climate change. However, South Africa has a gender pay gap between 23% and 35%. Global average is 20%, which negatively impacts women's adaptive capacity in general.

## Kenya's progress towards gender equality

Despite significant achievements such as increased access to education, advancements in affirmative funds for women-led businesses, and increasing representation of women in public and elective office. Despite such developments, only 29% of women are considered empowered, according to UN Women. The country has made strides in reducing the gender pay gap; however, progress is still slow, hampered by inadequate implementation of laws, inadequate funding, weak accountability mechanisms, and slow transformation of discriminatory and patriarchal gender norms, attitudes, and practices.

- **Adult literacy rates and labour force participation** are still lower for women creating significant inequality in incomes.
- **Kenyan law protects against all forms of sexual harassment**, Section 4(2) of the Employment Act. Laws also stipulate that all employers with more than 20 staff are supposed to create a policy prohibiting sexual harassment at the workplace. Sexual harassment is an offence punishable under the Penal Code, and any person found guilty of committing an act of sexual harassment is liable to imprisonment for a term not less than three years or fine, or both.

## Kenya's Social Institutions and Gender Index ranking

The country also scored 95 out of 100 in the 2021 Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.489, placing it at rank 37 globally. The index highlights that women are more likely to be responsible for the family and low access to resources.

## Gender-based violence (GBV) is pervasive

Gender-based violence (GBV) is pervasive in Kenya, affecting women and girls of all ages, socio-economic backgrounds, and ethnicities. According to a 2018 survey by the Kenyan National Bureau of Statistics, 39% of women in Kenya have experienced physical violence at least once in their lifetime. The most common forms of GBV are domestic violence, sexual violence, and emotional manipulation. Women often experience violence at the hands of their partners or family members. Sexual violence is also a significant problem, with many cases going unreported due to fear of stigma, shame, and lack of access to justice.

## Kenya's response to GBV

Kenya has adopted a raft of critical legislations designed to address sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV). These legislations are welcome and have all the hallmarks of a country that is committed to facilitating gender equality. Kenya also pledged to end Gender-Based Violence by 2026 through full commitment to the implementation of GBV laws and policies as well as every other measure necessary for the achievement of this goal.

The National Strategy on Prevention, Protection, Response and Recovery from Gender-Based Violence was launched in 2014 and provides a framework for the prevention and response to gender-based violence. The strategy includes provisions for the establishment of GBV response centers, the training of service providers, and the development of GBV prevention programs; 2) the Sexual Offenses Act: This law was enacted in 2006 to strengthen the criminal justice system's response to sexual offenses. The law includes provisions for improved investigation and prosecution of sexual offenses, as well as better support services for survivors; 3) The Protection Against Domestic Violence Act: This law

## Political participation

- In Kenya, women have the same rights to vote and stand for election as men, and the Constitution guarantees equal participation in political life. Despite this, women remain poorly represented at all levels of government.
- The 2017 general elections held August 2022 saw the greatest number of female candidates across most positions with seven governors, three senators and 35 single constituency members elected were women.
- However, Kenya still has the least representation of women among the six East African countries (Ethiopia, Kenya, Rwanda, Uganda, Tanzania and Burundi). The Inter-Parliamentary Union Commission has recorded representation of – 24.36 per cent, far below the two-thirds gender principle.

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

* **Kenya has a population of 46.69M**, with a female labour participation of 30.2% (2021). The country has a maternal mortality rate of 342 deaths/100,000 live births (2021). The female population is at 51%, and the male population is at 49%.

* **Most of the employment is in agriculture and services**, constituting 64% and 34% of the population in employment, respectively.

* **77% of women are self-employed** (i.e., over 60% of the female population estimated to be in vulnerable informal employment), defined as contributing family workers and own-account workers as a percentage of total employment. It is reported that COVID-19 significantly increased the vulnerability of women, particularly in terms of access to education, health outcomes, representation in parliament, and participation in the labour market.

* **Even within employment**, significant disparities exist between women and men. Over the last three decades, efforts have occurred to close the gender wage gap in Kenya. Legislation, in the form of the Equal Remuneration Act (1984) and the Gender Equality Act (2013), has been enacted. In 2019, it was estimated that women earn 32% less than their male counterparts, compared to 27% for men.

* **Despite these efforts**, the sector lacks standard employment contracts, social protection, and adequate pay, meaning that women are unable to obtain a decent livelihood, let alone have the same level of income as men.

## Decision making and leadership

* **Generally underrepresented** at decision-making levels. In Kenya, women are underrepresented on company boards and senior management positions, as well as in policy making and governance. Under-representation and the absence of female role models is even more pronounced among young people.

* **The few women who do manage to secure top positions are often less well-connected with their peers than with their male colleagues**. Notwithstanding some changes over the last few decades, underlying cultural and social norms still shape the perceptions of men and women's abilities, needs and prerogatives.

* **While data at household level is not available**, we expect that similar to in observations in other sectors (such as sanitation)**, increased gender equity could potentially have spill over effects that translate into better health outcomes. This would be particularly important for adoption of cleaner or modern lighting and cooking solutions which is a significant opportunity.

## Access to financial resources/knowledge and skills/other

* **Driven by perceptions and misperceptions**, only a low percentage of female students choose to study STEM fields (science, technology, engineering, mathematics). The UNESCO Report, entitled Towards 2030 (UNESCO, 2015), offers the most recent statistics on women's participation in STEM fields. The share of women studying and working in these fields is very low compared with men. In other science is low in many industrialised and emerging countries alike. Gender imbalances among STEM students carry through to gender imbalances in STEM jobs – in the renewable energy sector as well as elsewhere. DfE's survey finds that women occupy 28% of STEM positions.

## Kenya STEM participation shows a clear gender disparity ranging from 30%-35%. Fewer women participate and even fewer complete their studies. In addition, their graduation scores are lower than those of men. The number of women earning bachelor's degrees in STEM fields declines as they move through the educational ladder; this is attributable to institutional and structural barriers such as lack of access to quality education, financial constraints, and family responsibilities. Gender biases were not only revealed in inequality in enrollment and completion but also in policies that favored male students in STEM disciplines.

### Financial Inclusion

In Kenya, 83% of the population has access to mobile money services; however, eight out of ten Kenyans (83.7%) have access to formal financial services. The gap between men and women in physical assets that would normally be held by men. According to a 3FC report[1] only 1% of women in Kenya own collateral.

## Barriers for Women in Kenya

### Marginalized Groups in Kenya

In Kenya, youth, the elderly, and other traditionally marginalized groups such as persons with disabilities, ethnic and religious minorities, pastoralists, and refugees who have historically faced social exclusion. These groups are particularly affected by the lack of access to reliable and affordable energy and transportation services.

Youth, for example, face challenges in accessing energy for education and entrepreneurship; while the elderly often lack mobility options and may have difficulty accessing health care facilities. Other traditionally marginalized groups such as women and people with disabilities may face barriers related to gender or disability discrimination. Women, for instance, may have limited access to financial resources and face discrimination in accessing employment opportunities. People with disabilities also face physical and infrastructure-related barriers to accessing transportation services.

These barriers contribute to a cycle of poverty and inequality for these groups, limiting their ability to fully participate in the economic and social development of Kenya.

## Women in Energy in Kenya

According to reports by the AEDB and ENERGIA[2], 35% of the total staff and 15% of the technical leadership positions in the Ministry of Energy's headquarters are filled by women. Kenya shows a strong commitment to gender equality, with 40% of its seats on the board of directors at all state corporations in energy; there is some representation of women at the levels of heads of department and managers, and a woman was heading the Kenya Electricity Generating Company

## World Bank only 7% of truck drivers in Kenya are women, and women are also less likely to own transport businesses. Women also face discrimination and harassment in the workplace, with many female transport workers reporting incidents of sexual harassment and gender-based violence.

However, according to a report by the International Labour Organization (ILO), women in Kenya are more likely than men to use non-motorized modes of transport such as walking and cycling. Women are also more likely to be employed in informal sectors where they face greater mobility challenges than men, which may be a result of social norms that restrict their movement.

- According to a report by the World Bank, women account for only 14% of boda boda riders in Kenya. This is partly due to societal expectations that consider this job as men's work, as well as discrimination and harassment faced by women who attempt to enter the industry.


## Vulnerability to Climate Change and Needs of the Beneficiaries

- The high % of women in vulnerable employment in Kenya, as well as their significant reliance on agriculture means that women in Kenya are likely to be disproportionately affected by the impacts of climate change and have lower adaptive capacity to climate events or hazards.

- Further, climate change is expected to affect women more negatively compared to men in five key areas: (i) food security; (ii) water availability and access; (iii) energy access and energy; (iv) climate-related disaster, migration, and conflict. This is expected to have an overall negative impact on income and employment.

- Kenya has a high % of female-headed households, estimated at 32.4% of households (indicating a need for a gendered approach to increasing product accessibility to these households).

- With the majority of the population using wood fuel as their main source of cooking fuel, access to clean energy services is still a major issue in Kenya. Related to the differences in energy needs, in Kenya women are responsible for collecting firewood and water, which can be time-consuming for women. Further, it is well understood that women and men have distinct roles in the energy systems, with women often being responsible for cooking (and thus the associated costs of business) for cooking. A situation made worse by fuel scarcity and negative health and safety impacts (such as indoor air pollution).

- Women bear the invisible burden of the human energy crisis – and spend significant time and effort to water pumping, agricultural processing, and transporting. They need modern and more

# Ghana

Ghana has made considerable progress toward democracy over the past 20 years under a multi-party system, and its independent judiciary has gained the confidence of the populace. Ghana consistently ranks among the top three nations in Africa for press and speech freedom (World Bank, 2019). Despite this progress, however, Ghana faces significant challenges, including economic growth approaching 7%, making it one of the world's fastest-growing economies. The country has also made strides toward gender equality, with women still managing to sustain a growth rate of 0.5 percent in 2020 and it bounced back with a growth of 5.4 percent in 2021 thanks to dynamic agriculture and services sectors.

Ghana is ranked as the 9th most peaceful country in sub-Saharan Africa, with a 2019 value of 0.365. The country was ranked 107 out of 165 in the 2021 Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.67. Ghana's ranking in the Gap Index was as a result of high performance in the female labour force participation and political empowerment indicators, but low scores on education attainment. However, gaps remain in income earned between the two sexes as well as political participation.

Ghana is a signatory of several international conventions related to gender equality and women’s rights including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Platform for Action, the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (also known as the Maputo Protocol) and the Sustainable Development Goals. The country has also taken steps towards promoting gender equality and the empowerment of women and girls. Ghana has also taken steps to integrate these principles into its development strategies and policies.

Ghana has taken steps to integrate gender-based integrated interventions into its national development policies and strategies. The country has developed a Gender Policy Framework which seeks to promote gender equality and women’s empowerment by mainstreaming gender perspectives in all sectors of the economy, including social services. Additionally, Ghana has developed a National Gender and Children Policy and a National Action Plan on Women and Girls (2017-2026) as well as a National Strategy for Empowering Women in the Economy.

Gender-based violence (GBV) is prevalent in Ghana with physical violence being one of the most common forms of GBV in Ghana, with women often experiencing violence at the hands of their partners or family members. According to a 2017 survey by the Ghana Statistical Service, 28% of women aged 15-49 have experienced sexual violence and 36% have experienced physical violence since age 15, and 9% have experienced sexual violence. Source: Ghana Statistical Service (GSS), Ghana Health Service (GHS), and the International Union for the Prevention of Blindness (IUB). The Ghana Demographic and Health Survey (GDHS) 2017, Rockville, Maryland, USA; GHS, GHS, and ICF. According to a 2019 report by the Ghana Police Service, it was reported that 15% of women aged 15-49 have experienced emotional violence, such as humiliation or belittlement, in their lifetime. The same report also indicated that 37% of women aged 15-49 were working from home due to COVID-19 and 20% had lost work or access to money. The Domestic Violence and Victim Support Unit (DVVSU) of the Ghana Police Service reported that there were 9,146 cases of domestic violence reported in 2019. Source: Ghana News Agency. (2020).

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

The Ghanaian government has taken steps to address the problem of GBV, including the development of policies and interventions aimed at preventing and responding to GBV. These include: 1) The Criminal Code Act (Act No. 235), which provides for the prosecution of survivors of domestic violence. The law includes provisions for restraining orders, counseling and support services, and other forms of assistance; 2) The National Policy on Gender: This policy was launched in 2015 to provide a framework for promoting gender equality and addressing GBV in Ghana. The policy includes provisions for the establishment of a national gender equality commission, as well as guidelines for implementing policies and practices; 3) The Domestic Violence Secretariat: This is a government agency that provides support services such as counseling, legal aid, and other forms of assistance to survivors of GBV.

Ghana does not have criminal penalties for sexual harassment in the workplace. However, Ghana has developed civil penalties in Labour Act, 2003, Secs. 43(1)(b), 64 and 575.

occupations across the world. In Ghana, that percentage of women graduating from STEM- oriented academic disciplines is only 20%. This gender gap has profound human rights implications for women in terms of access to education, employment, and empowerment of countries. Unequal access to education, gendered choices for academic programs is strongly influenced by cultural norms, where girls are more likely than boys to be enrolled in STEM subjects, and other social and cultural factors about opportunities for employment and implications for future life.

According to the 2017 FinStat, 45 percent of women in Ghana have formal financial accounts, meaning that there is an 8 percentage point gender difference. Efforts are underway to close this gap using digital financial services (DFS). While the policy environment is supportive of DFS, challenges remain such as low levels of access to mobile phones and smartphones; lower levels of digital and financial literacy as well as negative cultural norms that create pessimistic views of digital access.

## Barriers to Digital Financial Services in Ghana

- According to a report by the World Bank, women, in particular, are disproportionately affected, as they have lower levels of access to transportation and electricity than men. This is due to a variety of factors such as limited access to financial services, lack of entrepreneurial opportunities, limited access to finance, and inadequate infrastructure.
- Youth also face challenges related to digital financial services, including limited access to education, housing, and transportation sectors. The elderly, especially those living in rural areas, often lack access to reliable energy sources, which can impact their health and quality of life. Addressing these barriers requires improving access to electricity, expanding the reach of digital infrastructure, increasing access to finance, and address social norms that limit opportunities for marginalized groups.

## Women in Energy in Ghana

- Research has established that energy policies that do not explicitly target women often result in inequities in energy access for women and men. Cultural roles and social norms limit women's access to energy services such as electricity, cooking fuel, and water.
- The lack of gender equality in the energy sector is a significant barrier to achieving equitable access to energy. Inequities in access to energy will persist. It is important to involve women in all the processes, including the supply chain of renewable energy services as either entrepreneurs or employees.

## Low Capacity of Staff at the Energy Sector Government Agencies

- The low capacity of staff at the energy sector government agencies is also a major challenge in maximizing the impact of gender equality policies and programs. This is because it is difficult to translate knowledge and skills to mainstream gender into climate change stems from the fact that policy and program implementation is often not gender-sensitive.
- The inadequate staff strength compounds the challenge of low capacity, affecting the planning and implementation of gender inclusive energy programs for the sector.

## Limited Data Availability on Gender Equity in Renewable Energy Sector

- There is limited data available on gender equity in the renewable energy sector. However, it is important to note that the lack of data does not necessarily mean that there are no women involved in this sector.
- According to a report by the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA), women are

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Women’s Energy Productivity in Ghana

In Ghana, the productive use of energy, agricultural cooling, and manufacturing sectors face a significant gender gap, with women being underrepresented.

According to a report by the Ghanaian Ministry of Energy, only 18% of the energy sector's workforce are women. Women's participation is also low in the renewable energy sector, which is dominated by men. Women are concentrated in low-skilled jobs and often face discrimination and bias in hiring and promotions, limiting their career advancement and economic opportunities.

Root causes of gender gaps in these sectors include limited access to education and training, lack of financial resources for starting businesses or expanding existing ones, limited saving opportunities, and limited access to finance and productive resources. Women's roles as caregivers and household managers also often limit their ability to participate in the formal workforce.

Efforts to improve gender equity in the energy sector include policy and legal frameworks such as Ghana’s Gender Equality Act (2016) and the National Energy Policy (2017). The government of Ghana has launched the Women, Energy, Climate and Leadership Initiative in 2020 to increase women's participation in the energy sector. Additionally, civil society organizations like the Women’s Empowerment Network (WEN) and AFRONT provide training and support for women entrepreneurs in the manufacturing and agricultural sectors, helping to improve their access to finance and markets.

## Women in Mobility and Logistics in Ghana

Gender equity in the mobility and logistics sectors in Ghana faces some challenges, with women being underrepresented. According to a report by the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) and the Energy Commission of Ghana (ECG), women are underrepresented in the transport sector, constituting only about 10% of the workforce.

The study found that women in Ghana face social and cultural barriers to accessing transportation services and infrastructure. Women also face discrimination and bias in hiring and promotions within the transport sector. Women's roles as caregivers and their concerns for safety while traveling make them more likely to use informal modes of transport, which are often unsafe or unreliable.

# Zambia

Zambia has a population of 18.3M people (55% of that being rural), as of 2020. The GDP in the same year was $18.1B, with 3.0% real contraction. (World Bank) GHG emissions measured in the prior year totaled 91.4Mt.

Zambia has a score of 526 on the 2019 Social Institutions and Gender Index with a score of 0.348 and 46 out of 546 as the 2022 Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.723. Zambia also ranked 131 out of 162 countries on the Gender Inequality Index, with a score of .540. As of December 2022, 89% of women in Zambia were married or living together. Women’s perspectives are available, with gaps in key areas, particularly: unpaid work and domestic labor information and communications technology skills.

## Vulnerability to Climate Change and Needs of the Beneficiaries

While climate change poses serious environmental hazards to all, research has shown that perceptions of vulnerability and exposure to these hazards are differentiated by gender, age, educational status, and place-based variables, with women generally showing a lower level of awareness about climate change than their male counterparts.

The results indicate further that irrespective of age, educational attainment, and where people live, women are more vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. Exposure to similar levels of exposure as the men, by virtue of their limited access to productive resources, poor conditions of housing, lack of information on adaptation decision-making, as well as the heavy domestic responsibilities placed on them.

* **Ghana** currently relies heavily on hydropower, with three plants comprising 41 percent of Ghana’s installed electric capacity. Ghana’s primary energy supply comes from petroleum, biomass, and hydropower.
* In terms of cooking, biomass is the main source of energy for cooking and heating in households. Wood and charcoal are the dominant sources of cooking fuel for households in Ghana. Close to one-third (31 percent) of all households use wood as their primary cooking fuel. The second predominant fuel for cooking is wood, accounting for 33.3 percent of households. Other energy sources used by households include kerosene, LPG, charcoal briquettes, natural gas, solar, sawdust, and animal waste. Rural households primarily use wood for cooking (63.0 percent). Gas and charcoal are more prevalent in urban coastal (50.2 percent) and urban savanna (48.2 percent) zones than rural areas. The use of biomass as a fuel source is also more common in rural savannah households for cooking than in the other ecological zones. Wood is the most commonly used fuel, followed by charcoal briquettes and kerosene in all ecological regions. The depletion of forest wood from unsustainable exploitation means women and girls walk longer distances to fetch firewood for cooking and for charcoal production.

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Sector Plan

## Zambia is a signatory to various gender-related international conventions, including:

- The Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW)
- The Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (1995)
- The African Union Protocol on the Rights of Women in Africa
- The National Gender Policy. The 7th National Development Plan has a focus on gender equality and women's empowerment, with specific targets related to increasing women’s representation in decision-making positions and ensuring that women have equal access to resources.

## Additionally, Zambia has established a Ministry of Gender to lead and coordinate gender-related initiatives and ministerial gender in all government policies and programs. The country has also adopted the National Strategy for Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment, including the Girls' Education and Women’s Empowerment in Agriculture and Livelihoods (GEWEL) program, which aims to increase girls’ enrolment and retention in schools and improve economic empowerment.

## Gender-based violence (GBV) is characterized by high rates of physical, sexual, and psychological harm. According to a study conducted by the Zambia Demographic and Health Survey 2019-2020, 45% of women have experienced emotional violence such as being insulted or belittled, 37% have experienced physical violence, and 22% have experienced sexual violence. The same ZDHS report found that 42% of women have experienced emotional violence, such as being insulted or belittled, and 13% have experienced physical violence. Women in Zambia are also more likely to experience GBV than men.

## The Zambian government has implemented several policies and programs aimed at addressing gender-based violence (GBV). These include the National Strategy for Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment, which aims to prevent and respond to GBV through various measures, including strengthening laws and regulations on GBV.

## Civil society organizations have also been active in addressing GBV in Zambia. For example, the Zambia Police Victim Support Unit (VSU) works closely with non-governmental organizations (NGOs) to provide services to victims of GBV.

## The legal framework in Zambia provides strong protection from sexual harassment. It is reported that Zambia has the highest rate of sexual harassment at the workplace among Southern African Development Community countries (SADC, 2014).

### Political participation
- Women have the same rights as men to vote and run for election in Zambia.
- Yet Zambia ranks 126th in terms of women's political participation, only 16.8% (28 out of 167) of its parliament seats are held by women.

### Labour force participation
- Zambia is a lower-middle-income country with one of the highest rates of income inequality in the world.
- The average monthly earnings for men were $305 per month compared to $1,140 per month (compared to 41% across Sub-Saharan Africa and three quarters of the poor live on less than $2 a day).
- As one of Sub-Saharan Africa's most urbanised economies, it is dominated by the services and manufacturing sector, notably copper mining and processing.
- And construction. 71.2% of adult women in Zambia are employed in agriculture (compared to 48.3% for men according to data reporting in 2019), and women make up 48.3% of the overall labour force.

### Zambian women
- Are most likely to work in low-income, informal occupations.
- 54% of employed women are in agriculture (compared with 76% for men).
- 55% of employed women also work in the services sector.
- In addition to agriculture, many women, especially those in the urban areas engage in micro and small businesses which are characterised by informality. An employment survey conducted by the World Bank found that 80% of women were working in the informal sector with the majority of women running unregistered businesses.
- This informality means they have limited access to information, training and finance that would help them grow their businesses.

### Zambia's statutes
- Grant women full legal rights before the law; however, parallel customary and religious legal regimes do not always recognize the rights of women to own or inherit land and other assets.

### Access to financial resources/knowledge/skills/other
- There has been considerable progress in reducing the financial access gender gap in Zambia. The Bank of Zambia began to consistently measure financial inclusion with a special focus on women over one decade ago.
- The gender gap in formal financial services access has narrowed, partially explained by an increase in the use of mobile money. It is estimated that 59.5 percent of women have access to mobile money compared to 70.3 percent in men.

### Barriers facing women headed households, youth, the elderly and other traditionally marginalized groups
- The lack of infrastructure and affordable transportation options in rural areas, where many of the marginalized groups reside, makes it difficult to access education, health care, and economic opportunities.
- Women-headed households are among those most likely to limit their ability to purchase and maintain their own transportation.

### Women in energy in Zambia**
- The participation of women in the energy sector value chain in Zambia is very limited, especially as entrepreneurs or business leaders in renewable energy. In terms of energy access, the majority of women in rural areas have no access to electricity compared with 80% of men without access to energy.
- Poor women are especially constrained considering their role in the provision of cooking energy and productive uses of energy for rural enterprises. Yet evidence shows that women have extensive knowledge on weather patterns, ecosystems, and management of natural resources.

## Vulnerability to Climate change and needs of the beneficiaries.

### Agricultural sector

- **Agriculture** constitutes a major livelihood source for rural populations.
- Reduced productivity of arable and pastoral farming, caused by drought, waterlogging of fields, heat-stress of livestock, and increased disease is likely to reduce the net income from agriculture. The value of agricultural output in 2015 was $398 million. Losses in the agricultural sector will increase from $29 to 180 million USD by the end of this century.
- This disproportionately impacts women's livelihoods as they are significantly represented in agriculture.

### Most of the agriculture in Zambia is small-scale and subsistence in nature, with women traveling to markets to sell their goods. Direct impacts of climate change are likely to impact these operations negatively (e.g., by reducing yields or increasing costs). Women may choose either to install groundwater irrigation of crops during droughts) or to take on alternative livelihoods if crops fail.

## Cluster B Countries: DRC, Uganda, Nigeria, Senegal, and Côte d'Ivoire

### Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC)

- The Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC) is the largest country in Sub-Saharan Africa (SSA) in terms of land surface area. DRC is endowed with exceptional natural resources, including minerals such as cobalt and copper, hydropower potential, significant arable land, immense biodiversity, and vast forest areas.
- Despite its wealth, the DRC has not benefited from this wealth. A long history of conflict, political upheaval and instability, and authoritarian rule have led to a grave, ongoing humanitarian crisis. In addition, there has been forced displacement of millions of people since 1998, with the peak occurring between the end of the Second Congo War in 2003.
- The DRC has one of the lowest electrification rates in the world and a HDI ranking of 179/188 (in 2011). The Gini coefficient for inequality (Index valued at 0.6, A TNI value of 0.5) highlights high gender inequality reflecting a discriminatory family code, inequality in physical integrity, limited access to resources and assets, and lack of freedom as a citizen. The statistics are further highlighted below.

The Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) is a signatory of several gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, and the African Union Protocol on the Rights and Welfare of the Woman.

- The country has taken steps to integrate these conventions and goals into its national policies, including the National Gender Policy (2008) and the National Strategy for the Advancement of Women (2013–2017). These strategies aim at promoting women's empowerment across all sectors of society, while the National Strategy for the Advancement of Women seeks to increase women’s participation in decision-making processes and eliminate all forms of discrimination against women. The DRC has also taken steps to implement these policies and ensure gender equity in the country. These challenges include limited resources and capacity, as well as political and cultural norms that perpetuate gender inequalities.

Gender equality is a key component of the United Nations Development Programme’s (UNDP) 2015–2020 Country Programme Report of Congo (DRC). According to the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA), two-thirds of Congolese women have experienced some form of GBV in their lifetime. The DRC government has made some efforts to address this issue, including the adoption of a law against GBV in 1987 and revised in 2016, which provides legal protection against GBV and sexual violence. The 2006 Law on Domestic Violence also criminalizes GBV with penalties for perpetrators. In addition, the National Strategy for Combating GBV (2014–2017) aimed to strengthen the government’s response to GBV, including through prevention, protection, and provision of services for survivors. However, the situation remains dire; experts have noted

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

The limited resources of women experiencing gender-based violence (GBV), 48% in women in the Democratic Republic of Congo have to walk for more than an hour to reach a health facility.

### Intersectionality and GBV in the Democratic Republic of Congo

In 2020, the Organization Stabilization Mission in the Democratic Republic of Congo reported over 1,000 cases of conflict-related sexual violence. Some organizations, including a coalition of civil society organizations, have noted a "widispered phenomenon" that women are not reporting to their country. One report shared that sexual violence against women and girls in the DRC is considered endemic. According to a report by Women's International League for Peace and Freedom, there is no law explicitly prohibiting domestic violence.

### Political participation

Political participation of women is limited. Following France's 1925 establishing the powers of ministries, there have been 3 women, or 0.8% of all members of the Government; in the current legislature, there are only 49 women out of 500 elected deputies and the Senate has six women out of ten senators. The 2015 revision of the electoral law dropped the 30% quota for women in local elections, which had been introduced to promote women's political participation. The provincial and local level has even lower representation of women in political, economic, and administrative affairs.

The limited political participation of women in DRC is a result of longstanding conflict that contributed to the majority of the population being faced with limited access to education, health and employment opportunities. Economic and social inequality among the population continues to widen with 40% of the population living on less than $1 per day. The country has a relatively low literacy rate that should support access to employment opportunities.

Most of the employment is in agriculture and services, constituting 77% and 19% of the population in employment, respectively. Women have less access to formal skills training compared to men, with only 20% of women having completed secondary education (compared to 35% for men). In the informal sector, 77% of workers are employed as domestic workers, micro businesses and the informal sector. In 2014, it was reported that women's unemployment rate was 6.7% compared to 4.8% for men; only 45.3% of the population aged between 15 and 24 years was economically active. Among 15-24 year-olds, the female-male ratio is 0.9.

## Access to financial resources/knowledge and skills/other

- Gender norms restrict women's access to resources and assets – for example, assets obtained within marriage are considered the property of the husband. Women have no legal rights over the husband’s, his parents, and brother. Legally, marital property is administered by the husband, and women cannot inherit or dispose of it without his consent. Women can own land according to the law, but they cannot own a house due to prevailing social norms. Women are not legally recognized as heads of households, and there is no prohibition on discrimination based on marriage status.
- Legally, there should not be any discrimination based on gender for the right to land concessions, but attitudes and customs tend to discriminate against women. Despite the nationalisation of all land and the introduction of a new law in 2015 that allows women to own land, the majority of land remains under customary law. Women, especially those who are married thus have limited access to land, and even though women make up the majority of the agricultural labour force – overexploitation of land is still a problem.

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

* Further, women are not able to make productive investments for productive assets, such as improved inputs, technologies, seeds, and fertilizers, as their incomes are controlled by men which limits their ability to invest in productive assets. This is particularly true with regard to women's energy poverty – and women as both consumers and suppliers of energy remain invisible in the sector.
* The proportion of women tend to be lower than that of men due to disproportionate access and consumption.

| More than 95% of the DRC population is unbanked – compared to the continental average of 74%. This makes it difficult for women, who are disproportionately poor, to gain access to finance, especially particularly for micro, small, and medium enterprises. In 2011, only 7% of Congolese firms used banks to finance investment. Further, The Family Code, which was revised in 2017, forbade women from owning land or property. This is a legacy of colonialism and the fact that this has been repeated; norms and attitudes will inevitably take long to change, and we expect that the barriers experienced by women continue to exist.

## Women in Energy in Democratic Republic of Congo

* The path for energy access and youth across the energy value chain is extremely limited – sector policies do not address the challenges they face, and barriers such as lack of access to credit and training limit the possibilities for women and youth to develop and use clean energy or low carbon technologies.
* The proposed KawiFund II aims to improve the DRC's social and economic development by reducing energy poverty in rural areas. Energy encompasses all production, subsistence and leisure activities that are directly or indirectly related to the use of energy. Women play a crucial role in energy production, distribution and use in households, communities, and the private sector. Women and youth can play a crucial role in energy production, distribution and use if they are given access to resources and training. The proposed KawiFund II aims to improve women's ability to participate in the sector. Promoting the use of clean cooking solutions would decrease the time burden on women and allow them more time for education, income generation opportunities, education, environmental management, and gender equality.

## Women in Energy Productivity in the DRC

* Researchers found that female farmers face far more barriers than their male counterparts in the DRC, which results in lower productivity and poverty. Women farmers have limited income and, as a result, have less access to agricultural inputs and produce use technologies. Some of the barriers to productive use technologies come from gender inequality regarding female farming to the exclusion of women's needs and interests.
* The barriers include lack of information infrastructure, limited access to information systems about the market, and difficult climate trends.

## Men and Women in the DRC experience the negative impacts of climate change

* Some researchers noted that women appear to be less adaptive to climate change than men due to resource constraints and less access to information. In one study, researchers found that women farmers are more likely to take on knowledge of the local environment.

# Uganda

Uganda is a low-income country in East Africa with a total population of 45.7 million people. GDP per capita of $837 and poverty rate of 42%. Uganda is included in Kansai’s Country Architecture 1-Republics (CARP) as a country that has the potential to leapfrog from fossil-fuel grids to clean energy-wide development. Amongst 5 countries behind the rest of Sub-Saharan Africa at key poverty and energy-related indicators. Energy access, which is critical for economic growth and poverty reduction, without which countries will struggle to meet their development goals. There is a strong case to invest in basic clean energy solutions – such as off-grid solar and clean cooking stoves – that can be deployed quickly and cost-effectively at scale, and that can be sold the same fashion in these nascent markets.

Uganda was ranked 90th out of 126 on the 2015 Social Institutions and Gender Index (SIGI) with a score of 0.478, placing it in the lowest quartile of the SIGI Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.734.

Uganda is a signatory to various gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and the Beijing Platform for Action. The Uganda government has made significant progress in integrating these conventions into national policy through the development of various policies and strategies, including the National Policy on Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment, the National Strategy for Gender Equality, and the National Action Plan on Gender-Based Violence. These policies aim to address gender inequalities and discrimination in various sectors, including

Education, health, employment, and political participation.

In addition, civil society organizations have played a crucial role in advocating for the implementation of these policies and in promoting gender equality.

• There is currently no stand-alone legislation that addresses sexual harassment. Sexual harassment continues to be widely reported in schools, workplaces, hospitals and other public places. The CEDAW Committee has recommended that Uganda ratify ILO Convention 190 on sexual harassment in the workplace but as noted by the CEDAW in their 2010 Concluding Observations, the law only applies when an employer or an employer representative and only requires positive actions to be taken by an employer if there are more than 25 employees (CEDAW, 2010).

## Political participation

- In Uganda, women have the same rights to vote and stand for election as men, and the Constitution guarantees women's participation in political life by reserving one seat for women in each of the country’s 104 constituencies.
- Women parliamentarians are elected through separate women’s elections in addition to the constituency MPs who are elected through a first past the post system. 34.9% (160 out of 459) members of Uganda's unicameral legislature are women, 93% of them were elected through reserved seats.

## Labour force participation

According to the World Bank, 49.3% of the labour force is female. Uganda is one of the poorest countries in Africa, with a GDP per person of $108 billion and, out of its five cities still living in extreme poverty.
- 68% of adult women are employed (20th out of the 118 low and middle-income countries reporting data in 2015), and women make up 49.3% of the overall labour force. They are most likely to be working as unpaid family workers, with nearly half of all female agricultural workers and nearly 42% of women in the workforce are unpaid family workers.

## A government report revealed a severe drop between 2009 and 2014 in the percentage of girls passing biology and mathematics examinations specifically.

This gender gap reduces the number of women who can enter STEM careers. According to UNESCO, only about 35% of science researchers in Uganda are women. While the Ugandan Government has put measures in place to address this issue, the 2019 National Survey on Gender Equality shows that the situation of girls and women in national STEM education continues to be poorer than that of boys and men.

This gap is attributed to gender bias among teachers, which is a significant barrier to achieving gender equality as teachers play a pivotal role in setting classroom norms and attitudes. There is now a push in Uganda to provide continuous professional development for teachers on issues related to gender equality, gender responsiveness, and inclusiveness, especially related to girls and STEM subjects.

On access to financial services, a study by FSD Uganda highlights that there is hardly a gender gap in terms of access to formal channels such as banks. However, women are shown to be more likely to be served by informal channels. While these provide savings and credit services; they are also likely to be more expensive than formal channels and their informal structures make them less secure.

Women also tend to have less capacity to meet KYC requirements of formal channels than those who currently have access to financial services. This is because women in Uganda still lag behind men in access to the internet (only 12% of excluded women have a phone, and only 1% have internet access).

### Women in Energy Productivity in Uganda

The role of energy productivity for women, men, youth and PLWHD differs due to differences in opportunities, access and control over resources and decision-making power at individual, household and enterprise levels. There is low awareness of OGS for PUE, particularly in the rural areas.

## Women in Mobility and Logistics in Uganda

- Women face poor access to public transport, complex mobility patterns, less time so traffic congestion is more impactful, and less access to employment in urban transport and associated industries.
- Women have been intentionally included in the development of the transportation sector; road infrastructure projects are being designed with women’s needs in mind. Gender equality is emerging as an important component for future road and transportation work. Additionally, public and private sector transportation leaders need to better understand the needs and opportunities of female customers.

### Vulnerability to Climate Change and needs of Beneficiaries

- In terms of access to electricity, the national household surveys indicate that a statistically higher percentage of female-headed households have no access to grid electricity (64.8%) than male-headed ones (10.8%). Only 35% of female-headed homes (vs. 48.4%) have access to the grid or other sources of electricity, and that differences are more pronounced in rural households. More male-headed households (66%) can access the national grid than female-headed households (52%).
- Data are lacking on women’s participation in the energy value chain as entrepreneurs or as employees. However, throughout the country, women are involved in converting wood into charcoal and selling it from doorsteps and agricultural institutions. The Women Energy Capitalization Credit Company (WEC) provides financial institutions with 100% of the funds available for women clients.
- Key factors that account for the differences between women’s and men’s vulnerability to climate change include gender-based differences in time access to assets and credit, treatment by formal institutions, which can constrain women’s opportunities, limited access to police documentation, and lack of awareness about energy-related policies and charges.

### Half of Ugandan households (52%) access at least one form of electricity, with 24% having grid electricity (64% in urban areas). Slightly more (70%) rely on off-grid electricity (33% in rural areas).

- To increase access to energy, the Uganda Government has set a target of achieving 100% access to clean energy by 2035 (Uganda Vision 2040). With increased solar usage, the use of kerosene lamps has declined from 58% to 28% between 2013 and 2017. Further, Uganda intends to reduce the proportion using clean energy for cooking from 69% in 2015 to 74%. To further increase the proportion using clean energy for cooking from 15% to 50%. As part of this, Uganda is promoting the use of clean energy technologies including electric cooking, domestic and institutional biogas among others.
- Access to energy for productive uses Solar devices (35%) and grid electricity (33%) are the most common sources of electricity used by enterprises. 29% of businesses do not have access

# Nigeria

- Nigeria is the largest market in Africa with a population of over 206 million (2020). It is one of Africa’s key oil producers, producing high-value, low-sulphur crude oil. The economy is heavily dependent on oil and had a GDP of USD 432.38bn (2020). The country has an electrification rate of 56%. However, the economic situation improved after the pandemic induced recession in 2020, but macroeconomic stability declined. Inflation rose to 17% in 2020 due to high food prices, fuel price increases, and global commodities shocks, the falling value of the currency, trade restrictions, and monetization of the deficit. Nigeria has also been unable to benefit from the rising oil prices since 2021 since oil output has fallen by over 35% in recent years. The country’s economy contracted by 6.48%, reducing the country's gross oil revenues, 39% of Nigerians (83 million) lived in poverty, and another 27% were at risk of falling into poverty. In addition to this, Nigeria has the highest number of people living with HIV/AIDS globally, with an estimated 10.7 million Nigerians living in extreme poverty as population growth continues to exceed efforts to reduce poverty.

- Nigeria was ranked 5th out of 28 countries in the 2019 Social Institutions and Gender Index with a score of .460 and 3rd out of 146 in the 2022 Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.639.

- Nigeria is a signatory to several gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa, and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action. The country has also ratified the National Gender Policy Act 2015, the National Gender Policy Act 2016, and the Violence against Persons (Prohibition) Act of 2015, among others. The National Gender Policy seeks to promote gender equality and women’s empowerment in all spheres of life, including education, health, economic development, while the Violence against Persons (Prohibition) Act criminalizes all forms of violence against persons, provides for the prosecution of perpetrators of such violence, and provides legal and institutional frameworks for combating such violations.

- Additionally, the Nigerian government has taken steps to integrate gender into its national development plans. For instance, the country’s Vision 2020 document includes gender-sensitive targets and indicators in all sectors of the economy. The National Gender Policy Implementation Committee is responsible for coordinating the implementation of the National Gender Policy and oversees the implementation of gender-related policies and programs at various levels of government. The National Gender-Based Violence Management Committee oversees the implementation of gender-related policies and programs, particularly in terms of women’s representation in leadership positions and access to education, health, and economic opportunities.

- At present, there is no law prohibiting sexual harassment, but state authorities may prosecute violent harassment under assault statutes (US Department of State, 2017). In Nigeria, sexual harassment is widespread where the practice of demanding sexual favour is exchange for

## Gender-based violence (GBV) is a pervasive problem in Nigeria and it is often underreported and impunity for perpetrators is high.
A significant portion of women in Nigeria have experienced gender-based violence. Almost one in three (31%) women aged 15–49 have experienced sexual violence, and almost half (48%) have experienced physical violence, experienced it at home. Root causes of GBV in Nigeria include gender inequality, harmful cultural practices, lack of access to justice, and inadequate implementation of laws by the Nigerian government and civil society organizations about GBV, including the adoption of the Violence Against Persons Prohibition (VAPP) Act in 2015, the establishment of sexual assault referral centers in all states, and the creation of a national action plan on GBV. Implementation of these measures has been inadequate, and more needs to be done to prevent and respond to GBV in Nigeria.

## Gender-based violence occurs in both public and private spaces.
3 in 4 girls who are sexually abused experienced it at school. The 2nd most common location where girls report having experienced sexual abuse is the schools.

In Nigeria, women have the same rights to vote and stand for election as men, and the Constitution guarantees equal participation in political life (MCA’s) are women.

## Access to financial resources/knowledge/skills/other

According to a study by the Gender Centre of Excellence*, formal financial inclusion remains low in Nigeria – 31% percent of adult Nigerians have borrowed from formal sources, and 45% have a formal savings account. The majority of adults (60%) do not use any form of financial services (early 2020). The most common reasons for not having a formal account are negative perceptions of formal institutions, poor access to banks, or not having enough income for savings. This suggests that the lack of access is more about demand than supply. However, many Nigerians borrow informally through low demand or poor provision of the right financial products. However, despite low access and limited use of formal financial services, 80% of Nigerians are left out of formal credit markets, a statistic exacerbated by low education, limited decision-making power, and poor access, as with rural populations.

As expected, gender norms and household dynamics can shape women’s access to finance; and different segments (e.g., younger vs. older women) have unique financial preferences and

### Senegal

- Located on the westernmost part of the African continent, Senegal is bordered by Mauritania, Mali, Guinea, and Guinea-Bissau. Senegal enjoys a dry tropical climate and has a population of 16.7 million people (2020), a quarter of whom live in the Dakar area (35% of the territory), and with a total GDP per capita of $4890.
- The country is one of the most stable countries in West Africa, with three peaceful political transitions since independence in 1960. (World Bank)
- Senegal’s GDP in 2020 stood at $24.4 billion in current terms. Its per capita gross national income was $3587.
- The country has experienced a period of economic growth, with real GDP growing more than 6% per year between 2014 and 2018. Real GDP growth stood at 0.87% in 2020, down from 4.4% in 2019, and +2.8% in 2019 (World Bank). The electricity access rate in Senegal is estimated to be around 53%, with a significant disparity between urban areas where 95% of households have electricity, and rural areas.

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Rural areas where the access rate is just 30.12%. Attracted by the stable political environment, several notable total and international solar PV companies operate in Senegal, using cash sales, pay-as-you-go (PAYG) models, or microfinance to provide access to electricity.

* **Senegal was ranked at 87 out of 129 on the 2019 Social Institutions and Gender Index with a score of 0.370 and 112 out of 146 in the 2022 Global Gender Gap Index with a score of 0.564.**

* **Senegal's 2001 constitution guarantees equality between women and men and in its Article 7, Senegal is also a signatory to several gender-related international conventions, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights. The country, in 2015, adopted the National Gender and Sustainable Development Policy, which aims to promote gender equality and the empowerment of women through various measures such as the criminalization of rape and other forms of sexual violence, which had previously been difficult to prosecute under Senegalese law. This was done in accordance with the African Union Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (in 1985), and the Optional Protocol on violence against women (in 2000).**

* **Sexual harassment is covered under Art. 213 bis of the Criminal Code. The Criminal Code defines sexual harassment as "the use or abuse of authority to 'harass a person through orders, threats, coercion, or any other means, in order to obtain sexual 'favour'. It is punishable of prison terms of six months to three years and a fine of 50,000 to 300,000 francs CFA.**
