# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

According to Senegal’s 2017 Demographic and Health Survey, 27% of women aged 15 or 49 had experienced physical violence from age 15 onwards — in 53% of cases, at the hands of a husband or partner. The survey also found that 68% of women aged 15–49 have ever been victims of at least one visible human rights violation: 68% of surveyed women aged 15-49 never spoke out if no one sought their help for violence against them, and 70% experienced sexual violence (FGM/C), sexual harassment, rape, and human trafficking. LGBTIQ individuals and sex workers also face high levels of violence and discrimination. International government entities have noted the lack of data on violence against women in Senegal.

Violence against children, in particular sexual violence, continues to be a major challenge in Senegal, with an estimated number of about 15,000 girls aged 15–17 victims of sexual violence.

## Political participation

In Senegal, women have the same rights to vote and contest elections as men. According to the Law on Equality of Men and Women in Electoral Lists adopted in 2012, candidate lists put forth by parties must include at least one woman for every three candidates, with a quota of 30% being legally admissible. Consequently, 43% (71 out of 165) members of the legislature are women.

Senegal has gained international recognition for its efforts towards political participation of women through the adoption of the Gender Policy Law (2020).

## Labor force participation

Senegal is a landlocked, mixed economy country that is driven by the service sector (tourism, construction and commodity exports like fish, phosphates, and groundnuts). There is also a large, rainfed subsistence agriculture sector that is prone to frequent droughts. 36% of adult women are employed in agriculture compared with only 15% for men. According to some countries reporting data in 2019, and women make up 40.4% of the overall labour force.

Only 24% of employed women work in agriculture, compared to 33% of all men. Nearly 70% women are in the services sector, compared to 48% men. Only five management positions are held by women.

There are no laws in Senegal mandating non-discrimination based on gender in hiring. No laws penalize or prevent discrimination against pregnant and nursing mothers; however, civil law mandates equal rights for inheritance, parallel codification, and religious regimes that do not recognize the rights of women to own or inherit land and other assets.

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Access to financial resources/knowledge and skills/other

- Women and girls in Senegal face barriers in the supply of and in their demand for financial products and services, the wider enabling environment as well as in their socio-cultural context. On the one hand, women are less likely than men to have access to financial services because women are in a context where women and girls’ mobility is constrained due to socio-cultural norms as women are expected to undertake the majority of unpaid care responsibilities in the household and in the community.
- For instance, 40.6% of women aged 15 to 49 find it normal to be beaten by their husband if they leave the house without informing him. This is due to a combination of factors such as gender mobility and access point constraints, barriers related to access to a mobile phone and level of awareness of mobile money accounts and knowledge on usage. Further, low literacy rates among women also contribute to this.
- In terms of the enabling environment, social norms related to inheritance and land and property ownership result in the majority of women in Senegal at 85% (aged 14–49) not owning a house or land title. This is further compounded by the fact that women are underrepresented in the sector; women account for 70% of the labour force, but they hold only 3% of cultivated land.
- Consequently, women resort to informal mechanisms for credit access and savings, which lack any formal regulatory protection.

## Women in energy in Senegal

- The Government of Senegal has made energy sector development a key component of the Plan Sénégal Emergent, which aims to modernise the country’s economy and achieve a wide number of ambitious goals related to poverty reduction, education, health, gender equality.
- Priorities include lowering the cost of energy by reducing dependence on imported fossil fuels; increasing access to electricity for all Senegalese citizens; and tapping into significant potential to develop solar and wind power, together with large offshore natural gas resources that will come onstream in 2023. The Senegalese authorities aim to achieve universal access by 2025 through the implementation of innovative solutions.

## Women in Energy Productivity in Senegal

- Lack of access to efficient electrical tools and appliances is a major barrier for women entrepreneurs seeking to improve their productivity in marginalised off-grid rural regions across sub-Saharan Africa. The lack of electricity also limits women’s ability to engage in income-generating activities in regions hit hard by the impacts of climate change. As rainfall becomes more erratic, solar irrigation reduces farmer dependence on rain-fed agriculture, enabling them to produce healthier crops and increasing their resilience against droughts. However, women are less likely than men to take advantage of these opportunities because they lack access to efficient electrical tools and appliances.

**Page 195**

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Women in Mobility and Logistics in Senegal

Women are increasingly mobile within urban areas of Senegal creating new transportation needs for women and girls. GGGE's report on Senegal's green energy transition noted the need for stronger public transportation planning so that public transportation is inclusive and gender responsive. Gender-responsive transport policies should consider the specific needs of women, including safety concerns such as their safety; affordability of the fares, the schedules for the transport services fitting their needs; and the availability of transport infrastructure are central to the planning and development of the transport programs in urban development.

## Vulnerability to Climate change and needs of beneficiaries

- The primary effects of climate change in Senegal are desertification and land degradation. Description is a combination of droughts and erosion that result from insufficient rainfall, destruction of vegetation and soil fertility, and salinization of agricultural lands. These processes are accelerated by the salinization of agricultural land, water and wind erosion, soil infertility and reduction of agricultural productivity.
- The extreme disparities in weather conditions affect food security, livelihoods and job opportunities for rural communities, predominantly women. Women comprise 70% of the agricultural workforce but only receive a small share of the benefits from agriculture due to low yield. Considering their labour and income are dependent on the rains, women are vulnerable to the most extreme conditions of poverty because it limits access to food and water, increases job insecurity, makes self-sufficiency difficult and limits their ability to improve their socio-economic status.
- To adapt to the soil and land degradation, farmers adopt practices that are intensive to make up for low yields. However, such practices have negative impacts on the soils. Another deterrent to productivity beyond climate change is a lack of access to modern technology used for agriculture, which subjects women to tougher labour conditions. While climate change is a global phenomenon, it has local effects and requires local solutions in finding solutions to the effects of climate change with groups such as Regroupement des femmes de Pape Diop (RGDPD) and Association pour le Développement Rural (ADR) who have taken charge of reforestation efforts in the Popenguine region of Southern Senegal.
- Successful policies have increased energy access in Senegal to almost 70% of the population. With full access expected to be achieved by 2025, while the electric grid accounts for the majority of electricity consumption, diesel generators are still required and will continue to be critical for the most remote populations.
- LPG is used for cooking by almost 85% of the population today, due to higher shares in sub-Saharan Africa. It is expected to remain as main clean cooking in 2030. However affordability challenges remain to expand this access to the 70% who currently rely on biomass for cooking.

- Côte d'Ivoire is a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Beijing Platform for Action. These conventions have been integrated into national strategies, policies, and plans, such as the National Gender Policy (2014–2023) and the National Action Plan on Gender-Based Violence. # Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

- Despite these efforts, domestic violence is widespread throughout Côte d'Ivoire, in particular sexual, psychological, and economic violence (SCDP, 2015; MIFAS & UNFPA, 2008). However, it is considered a private matter and victims often do not report the authorities.
- Victims mostly remain silent regarding gender-based violence and most cases are not reported to the authorities (MISFWC, 2014). Gender-based violence cases are often settled amicably through traditional justice mechanisms. The law provides for a maximum sentence of three years (Law No. 2016/030) going to court is a public process and may mean for victims exposing themselves to stigma and exclusion from their families and communities (ISPWC, 2014).
- Female-headed households are more likely than other types of households to face constraints that other types of families in Côte d'Ivoire. With fewer male laborers than other households, female-headed households in agriculture have lower income and less access to the formal market.

## Political participation

In Côte d'Ivoire, women have the same rights to vote and contest elections as men. Côte d'Ivoire passed a law in 2019 that requires political parties to meet a female candidate quota of 30% for parliamentary elections; however this has not been achieved.

It is reported that while election processes are held in all regions, women are not given priority when it comes to the nomination and selection process. This is especially true in case in many regions making the 30% quota almost impossible to achieve in most local contexts. Political parties are heavily male dominated, with men making most of the decisions at national and regional levels.

## Labor force participation

- Agriculture is a significant economic driver in the Country and although they do not own large farms, women are an important workforce, especially in the production and marketing of food crops. They are present at 29% in the agricultural sector; 35.6% in industry; 59.1% in trade and 38.7% in services.
- While 46% of labour participation for women (15 years and above) in Côte d'Ivoire is estimated to be in agriculture, most of the employment is in the informal sector with less than 4% of women having formal jobs. In addition, women are more likely to work part-time or on a seasonal basis. For people with higher levels of education, it is observed that women are still more vulnerable to unemployment than men.

## Gender roles and practices in the labour market in Côte d'Ivoire

Gender roles and practices in the labour market in Côte d'Ivoire are noted in the traditional patriarchal system, which is unfavourable to the promotion of women's activities. The precarious and informal nature of women’s work is mainly explained by the principles of separation between public and private spheres. Women are considered as secondary citizens and must therefore work to meet the family's needs, while the role of the woman is essentially domestic. Consequently, it is easy to understand why, despite the existence of employment opportunities, women are excluded or reduced to precarious or marginal jobs.

### Access to Financial Resources/Knowledge and Skills/Other

- According to research by CGAP's financial inclusion indicator in Côte d'Ivoire is low with only 45% of the population having access to formal financial service. However, this exclusion remains high and is not even. Women are 45% more likely to be excluded than men.

- A major barrier is that fewer Ivorian women own mobile phones, especially in rural areas. GMMA's assessment of Côte d'Ivoire in 2017 showed that about 20 percent fewer women than men owned phones and that the gender gap was even larger among young people, with education being a factor.
- The Financial Inclusion Insights survey shows that half the population of Côte d'Ivoire does not have financial numeracy skills or the ability to navigate a phone menu or send text messages. This lack of basic digital literacy affects women in Côte d'Ivoire, where only 40% of women are numerate versus 62% of men.

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Women in Mobility in Côte d'Ivoire™

### Vulnerability to Climate Change and Needs of the beneficiaries:

- **Labour participation:** Women's labour participation is concentrated in agriculture. Agriculture is indicated as one of the most vulnerable sectors to climate change given changes in precipitation and weather patterns, leading to the need for increased adaptation responses to address the potential impacts of climate change.
  
  - **Women's vulnerability:** Over 85% of female employment in Côte d'Ivoire is considered to be in vulnerable employment. This is critical as it means that women are more likely than men to lose their livelihoods and income due to the impacts of climate change and have lower adaptive capacity to climate events or hazards.
  
- **Women's role:** Women are both producers and consumers of energy. The gender division of labour assigns the bulk of household (domestic) tasks to women. These include often spending considerable time (and energy) on cooking, which primarily involves fetching fuelwood. Since cooking is also primarily the responsibility of women, they are also likely to be the principal consumers of energy at the household level.

  - **Energy consumption:** The presence of women in this role highlights their significant contribution to household energy use and their potential vulnerability to climate change impacts on energy resources.


### References:

- World Bank (2015) REPUBLIC OF CÔTE D'IVOIRE FOR THE ABIDJAN URBAN MOBILITY PROJECT

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

or absence of different energy services at the household level, including electricity for lighting and other purposes, will therefore have a greater impact on women than men.

Women are more likely to be responsible for cooking and cleaning in households with different responsibilities in the economy and household mean that their needs, and uses, of energy services also differ. Whereas men may prioritise access to TV, women are more likely to prioritise applications such as lighting, cooking, and heating. Women are also more likely to grind milling and motive power, that reduce the time and drudgery of their domestic tasks.

Fuel for cooking is one of the most critical energy needs. Biomass remains the predominant source of fuel in rural areas, with women being responsible for collecting it. It was emphasised that while the Government promotes use of biogas for cooking, an estimated 62% of rural households still use fuelwood for cooking, while in Abidjan 40% use gas and 42% use charcoal.

## Part III: Lessons learnt from Kaufland Fund I

Kaufland Fund I was a technical assistance facility provided by Kaufland Fund. The Kaufland team conducted annual gender assessments for all portfolio companies.

- As of Dec 21, the Kaufland portfolio had an average gender score of 0.65, indicating above average performance. The portfolio was aligned with the 2K Challenge with 30% of companies meeting the target. This is a significant improvement from 2020 when only 8% of companies were women in senior management and Board roles; availability of training for gender-specific safety and skills; and increased representation of women as candidates in the selection process.
- The Kaufland I Technical Assistance Facility supports gender equality, including: skill enhancement for low-income women; having trained 64 women during 2021; increased awareness on importance of clean energy products; and support for increased financial inclusion for women.
- The findings are highlighted below:

### Policies, Training and Targets:
- All Kaufland portfolio companies had general policies relating to (i) non-discrimination and harassment; 2) diversity and inclusion; 3) accommodation of flexible working arrangements. However, only 50% addressed these issues. 50% of Kaufland portfolio companies had policies specific to gender parity. However, less than 10% of companies had policies specifically addressing women’s above issues (including gender); this was driven by a lack of resources by these companies and highlighted the need for TA support.
- Employment and Management: 31% of total employed staff of the portfolio companies were women as at Dec 21. This however varied significantly from 64% in one company (Opes Solutions - large manufacturing company) to 24% (Inspirafarms - small installer). Women were underrepresented in senior management, sales and marketing positions with little representation in senior management and engineering.
- Board membership: 3 out of 10 of the Companies did not have a female Board member as at Dec 2021. Specifically, Inspirafarms and Bttoo. This was despite being one of the areas where Kaufland Fund I provided TA support. The companies were encouraged to appoint women on their Board.
- Consumers: It is estimated that 32.4% and 31% of households in Kenya and Rwanda are headed by women, respectively. Women are therefore a significant group among the prime beneficiaries of access to modern energy services. Numerous


---

# Gender Assessment and Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

studies have highlighted the benefits of modern energy for women: solar lanterns provide light for studying, reduce security risks, and displace dirty, harmful kerosene lanterns. In one study, women who used solar lanterns reported a 50% reduction in malaria and tuberculosis. Clean, efficient cookstoves bring down the time that women and girls must spend collecting firewood. This is especially important for women living with HIV/AIDS, a major public health risk that kills over 4 million people annually and disproportionately affects women and girls.

## Part IV: Conclusion - KawSafi Gender Mainstreaming Approach

The table below highlights the KawSafi Fund II approach to each of the barriers identified across the two clusters of countries.


| Clusters Identified | Cluster A Countries: Rwanda, South Africa, Kenya, Ghana, and Uganda |
| --- | --- |
| Barrier 1: Closing the Financing gap that women in the energy sector face | 3.1 High representation of women in the investing entity team |
| Barrier 2: Increasing participation and representation of women in renewable energy | 2.3 Increase portfolio policies, gender equality practices and female board representation in portfolios |
| Barrier 3: Increasing access to education and training for women in the renewable energy sector | 2.5 Increase access to education and training for women in the renewable energy sector such as technology and e-commerce |
| Barrier 4: Enhancing leadership, lessons learned and insights in the renewable energy ecosystem | 2.7 Share through leadership, lessons learned and insights from best practices |
| Barrier 5: Increasing adaptive capacity of women to change across the countries of focus | 3.1 Support collection of sex-disaggregated data on gender needs and adaptive capacity of beneficiaries |
| Barrier 6: Improving awareness of clean products and services among women in the energy sector | 2.2 Increase awareness of benefits of clean products and services for women in the energy sector |
| Barrier 7: Increasing access to finance for women in the energy sector | 3.2 Support positive policy developments across the sector |
| Barrier 8: Improving access to information on innovative financing models of consumer credit such as access to mobile money | 3.4 Support portfolio companies recruit female board members and accelerators in the country to support development of a strong pipeline of female founders in the sectors of focus |

# KawiFund II: Gender Action Plan

## Impact Statement:
To drive integration, responsiveness, and participation of women in the African distributed energy, productive, and enabling economy, including their access to support for female-led and owned businesses; increased representation through employment at Fund and portfolio company level; promotion of senior female leadership and increase in female access and participation across the distributed energy and clean mobility value chain.

## Outcome Statements:
1. Enhance women’s economic activities, livelihoods, and empowerment by driving the development of decent jobs for women across the three industry segments KawiFund II intends to invest in across Africa.
2. Support women-owned businesses through increased access to capital and other forms of support.
3. Enhance access to low carbon technologies for women through gender-integrated product design and distribution.
4. Increase female representation at all levels within KawiFund II portfolio companies.
5. Reduce historical gender disparity in skills development across technical fields - including solar PV and other clean generation technologies, battery technology, electrical and mechanical engineering, among others.

## Outcomes:
1. Understand baseline performance of portfolio companies across access outcomes.
2. Throughout the life of the program (i) partner with KawiFund portfolio companies to drive employment of women across their organizations, including representation in senior leadership; (ii) Partner with ecosystem partners working on improving skills and capabilities of women across the segments of interest, to improve female empowerment.
3. Throughout the life of the program, support increased venture capital allocation to female-owned/led businesses.
4. Throughout the life of the program: (i) directly engage KawiFund portfolio companies to drive increased female access of low carbon technologies as well as support sale of products that disproportionately improve women’s wellbeing and (ii) Partner with ecosystem partners working on improving skills and capabilities of women across the segments of service offered to female consumers and users - including but not limited to, increased safety and comfort in e-mobility applications.

## Indicators and Targets | Timeline | Responsibilities
| --- | --- | --- |
|  |  |  |

* Portfolio level activities will be measured against the Gender Action Plans that will be developed post-investment. Ecosystem Activities will be implemented through the Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) and have no timeline.

# Gender Assessment and Gender Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Fund Level Activities (Mgmt. Fee funded)

### (1) Support employment of women at KawiSafi Fund level
- **(i)** 50% of total employees of the KawiSafi fund management company should be/should identify as female.
- **(ii)** A minimum of 30% of KawiSafi Fund II equity companies to be female led. The team will build a diverse team of entrepreneurs, business groups, and investors with different backgrounds and experiences in the investment business.

### (2) Integration of gender-lens investing principles into the investment process
- **(i)** A minimum of 30% of KawiSafi Fund II equity companies to be female led. The team will build a diverse team of entrepreneurs, business groups, and investors with different backgrounds and experiences in the investment business.
- **(ii)** Maintain a diverse team of entrepreneurs, business groups, and investors with different backgrounds and experiences in the investment business.

### (3) Development of Gender Action Plans for each portfolio company
- **(i)** One Gender Action Plan developed for each investee company.
  - **GAP to be developed within 90 days of investment.**
  - **Executing entity to develop the GAPs in collaboration with Investee Company.**

### Portfolio Level Activities (TAF funded)
#### (1) Portfolio gender assessments
- **(i)** Initial KawiSafi portfolio assessment report completed.
  - **By 2023 (3 years after program start).**
  - **Executing Entity to monitor and evaluate as part of monitoring and evaluation activities.**

- **(ii)** Follow up KawiSafi portfolio assessment report completed.
  - **By 2038 (6 years after program start).**
